# What is Stickman Video Director?

We made this skill free for everyone, because making animation shouldn't cost a fortune or eat your whole week. It's an open-source "skill" that teaches an AI assistant how to make stickman videos.

Think of it like this: an AI assistant such as Claude Code or Codex is a very capable agent that can read, write, and use tools on your computer. On its own, though, it doesn't know the craft of making a good stickman video. The skill is a training manual plus a toolbox that you install into the assistant.

## 🎬 Two ways to make a video: Model Engine vs Code Engine

On a serious note, there is no single approach that fits everyone's budget and taste. We we made two modes for your own taste, pocket and desire.

## 🅰️ Engine A: the Model Engine. An AI video generator draws the clips. think of this as a film director with a camera crew. The assistant writes the story and plans every scene, then writes precise instructions, called prompts, for an AI video generator like Google Flow, which shoots the actual moving footage. Paste the prompts in yourself, or sit back and let the assistant do it.



## 🅱️ Engine B: the Code Engine. Your computer builds the whole thing. Picture a tiny animation studio living inside your laptop. It draws the characters, scenes and props as images, then writes code that animates them and renders a finished video, right there on your machine.

We made this skill free for everyone, because making animation shouldn't cost a fortune or eat your whole week. It's an open-source "skill" that teaches an AI assistant how to make stickman videos.

## You can start from almost anything: a half-baked idea, an article, a web link or a full script. Both engines handle quick shorts and longer videos. Pick 16:9 landscape or 9:16 portrait for your platform, and switch captions, voiceover, background music and sound effects on or off to taste.



## Can't decide? Here's my honest advice. Want your first video today without spending a cent? Go Code Engine. Want cinematic motion and already have video credits lying around? Go Model Engine. (ALTHOUGH GOOGLE FLOW GIVES FREE CREDITS TOO UNTIL YOU HIT LIMIT)

# 
🛠️ Installation Process

For results as close as possible to our sample videos, we recommend an AI assistant that's great at coding, like Codex or Claude Code. Any other agent that can read and write files and run commands works too, though results depend on how capable it is.

## Step 1: Install the skill

This is the easy bit. On first use, your agent checks what's needed and helps you install anything that's missing. Just send it this:

When it says it's ready, you're officially a stickman director. 🎉

## Step 2: Know your toolkit

## Here's everything we use, what each piece does, and what you can swap in if you don't have it. Don't panic, you don't need all of this on day one. Check the "Needed for" column against the engine you picked.

## Step 3: Give your stickman a voice

Good audio is what makes a stickman video come alive, and the skill handles all of it for you.

  - Voices. There are loads of text-to-speech tools out there, but I strongly recommend ElevenLabs. It turns your script into natural, human-sounding speech and can give each character its own voice. A free account gets you started; paid plans add more voice minutes each month. Prefer something else, like Google Gemini text-to-speech? Go for it.

  - Music and sound effects: built in. The skill comes with its own library of background music, short musical stings and hundreds of sound effects: whooshes, pops, footsteps, the works. It's free, licensed and already included, so there's nothing to set up. If a track needs a credit, the assistant tells you exactly what to paste into your video description.

  - Mixing: automatic. The assistant blends voices, music and effects into one balanced soundtrack and attaches it to your video. You never have to open audio software. Ever.

To connect your voice tool, just tell the assistant which one you're using. It walks you through creating an API key and saving it in a private file on your computer. One golden rule: never paste the key into the chat.

In short: make one voice account, like ElevenLabs, and everything else is taken care of.

## 🎨 Meet the styles

This is the fun part. Every style has a code, and you'll drop that code into your request, so have a little browse and pick a favourite. Each one has sample videos in the GitHub README, and trust us, it's very easy to lose 20 minutes watching them.

## 

🔥 Everyone can make an animated video, not everyone can go viral.

Making animated videos with ease and at no cost is one thing. Going viral is another thing entirely!.

So I did a thing. I studied 500+ animated shorts and pulled out a framework for why they went viral. Then I turned the whole process into a Claude prompt.

The script template helps you write the script and arrange the scenes for your stickman video, so it's built to go viral from the very first second. Use it to write your story, then bring that script to the Stickman Video Director in the next step.

## 🎬 Making your first video!

Pick an engine, copy its template, swap in your topic and style, and hit send. In both engines the assistant shows you the plan first, and nothing gets generated until you say yes. You're the director here.

🅰️ With the Model Engine

What happens next:

  56. The assistant sends a story outline, an audio plan and a storyboard. Read them, tweak whatever you like, approve.

  57. It writes one self-contained prompt per scene.

  58. You paste each prompt into Google Flow (our go-to), or let the assistant drive the browser and make the clips for you. Start with a low-resolution test clip.

  59. The assistant stitches the clips, adds captions and audio, and hands you a finished video.

## 
Pro tip from me: if a character has to look identical in every clip, give the assistant a stickman reference image and reuse it in every scene.

🅱️ With the Code Engine

What happens next:

  65. The assistant checks your image and rendering tools.

  66. It sends the complete script and a 20-second preview with audio.

  67. You approve both, or ask for changes.

  68. It builds the characters, scenes and props, animates them, adds subtitles and audio, and renders the full video on your computer. Go make a coffee.

  69. One part not quite right? Ask it to fix just that section. No need to redo the whole thing.

📤 Ship it!

Video done? Don't stop now. Send this:

## 😅 The 3 mistakes we made (so you don't have to)

Confession time. Mei and I hit all three of these on our first videos. Each one cost us time; none of them has to cost you any.

  77. A free Gemini key returns quota errors for images. If you use the Gemini API for images, it needs a key with billing. Or skip it and use Nano Banana inside Google Flow for free.

  78. Flow refuses prompts that name real people. Keep real names out of your topic and script. Describe the character instead.

  79. Chrome can silently block multiple downloads. Before your first batch, allow multiple downloads for flow.google.com in Chrome. Otherwise your clips look downloaded but never actually arrive. (Yes, we stared at an empty folder for a while.)

## 💰 How you can spend $0 per video

The skill itself is completely free and open source. You only pay for the third-party services you choose to plug in.

## The $0 route: Code Engine + the assistant subscription you already have + a free image tool + a free ElevenLabs account + the built-in music. No video credits, no surprise bills.



## The rule that saves the most credits: approve the proposal and the setting images before any clip is generated.  A rejected clip costs 30 credits.

## Questions people keep asking us❓

## Do I need a script? Nope. Start from a one-line idea, a news link or a full blog post. The assistant writes the script with you.



## Do I need to code, or own a beast of a computer? Not at all. The assistant writes and runs everything, and you don't need a fancy graphics card. A faster computer just renders a bit faster.



## Will my characters look the same in every scene? In the Code Engine, yes: it reuses the same character assets all the way through. In the Model Engine, use a reference image for anything beyond a simple stickman.



## Can I use the videos commercially? Yes! The skill is MIT-licensed, so you can use it, change it and share it, including commercially. Music, sound effects and other third-party assets keep their own licenses, so add any credit the assistant tells you to.



## Can I monetize a stickman channel? Plenty of channels already run stickman story videos in the YouTube Partner Program. How you do depends on your content and each platform's rules, but the door is wide open.

## 🎬 Your turn dear director!😉

And that's a wrap! You now know what Stickman Video Director is, its two engines, every style, the full toolkit, how to make and publish your first video, the mistakes to dodge, and what it all costs.

Don't let the length scare you off. Install the skill, connect a voice, send one template with a 30-second test, and boom: that's your first stickman video.

Start small. One style, one idea, one short video. Your first try might need a few tweaks, and that's totally normal. Fixing a script costs nothing.

## 3. Thread by @bradsmith

[1/15] I rebuilt our website with AI and almost lost our million-dollar business.

We went from 30–50 high-quality leads a month to ZERO.

Last November, I rebuilt our entire website on Lovable. We were right in the middle of trying to scale to $3M, which has been the hardest part so far.

I got excited about building something new and hit publish. This post isn’t blaming Lovable btw. It’s on me.

It had taken years to get those leads coming in consistently. I was replacing a website that was already bringing us customers.

December rolled around. Zero leads. I blamed the holidays.

January rolled around. Still zero. I blamed myself for not making enough content.

Then February went by. Then March. Nothing. Pretty hard to keep blaming the holidays in March.

We were nosediving, and I kept looking for another explanation.

Then my team sat me down and said, “You made a mistake. We are in fear for our jobs because the company is drying up.”

I knew they were right. I had to own it and apologize.

In my excitement to build something new, I had put 8 years of work at risk. And now my team was worried about whether they would still have jobs.

So we reverted to our previous website.

Immediately after switching back, leads started coming in again. Simple landing pages, simple copywriting, and a website that was already working for the business.

Here’s the mistake I don't want you to make: I trusted AI to do a complete job. It didn't. In fact, it missed a lot of simple things because I didn't give it the right prompts.

If you’re about to rebuild your website with AI, I put together a 378 step checklist. Simply copy and paste it into your AI builder so you don't forget the most important things.

[1/6] if you drop a raw ElevenLabs voiceover straight into CapCut, Youtube already flagged your channel for demonetization

mfs think Youtube has some high IQ AI detector listening in on their voice

it doesn't listen at all

it measures the silence between your words

a real microphone in a bedroom never hits mathematical zero (0.000 dB) between sentences because air, walls, and computer fans and shit all make noise

raw ElevenLabs audio files are generated in a digital void, so every time the voice pauses between words, the audio wave drops to dead 0.000

even worse, default ElevenLabs leaves the exact same 0.25 second gap after every period with zero inhale breaths, and 100,000 slop channels upload the exact same stock "Adam" and "Marcus" voice frequencies every day

that's why 10,000 geeks get rejected for "Inauthentic Automated Content" every week while guys clearing $60k/mo with ElevenLabs get approved in 24 hours

here is the 30 second audio setup that makes your AI voice pass as a real human in a studio:

THE -50dB AIR CONDITIONER FLOOR

download a 60 second recording of an empty room or a quiet air conditioner ("room tone") from Freesound or the Youtube Audio Library

lay that track underneath your ElevenLabs voiceover in CapCut for the entire video and drop its volume to -50dB

you can't consciously hear it at -50dB, but it fills the dead digital gaps between every word so the waveform reads like a real Shure microphone in a quiet bedroom

KILL THE ROBO PACING BEFORE YOU GENERATE

never paste a ChatGPT script with normal periods straight into ElevenLabs on default settings

before you hit generate:

- drop the "Stability" slider down to 35% to 45% (leaving it at 75%+ makes every sentence repeat the exact same robotic pitch wave)
- replace periods with commas, "..." and line breaks so the pauses between sentences come out uneven
- drop two subtle 0.4 second inhale breath sounds into the track right before the longest sentences

BREAK THE STOCK VOICE FINGERPRINT

never use the default "Adam" or "Marcus" preset untouched

either blend a custom voice inside ElevenLabs Voice Design, or speed your vocal track up to 1.03x in CapCut and shift the pitch by 1 semitone

that tiny 1.03x + 1 semitone shift completely changes the audio frequency hash while still sounding 100% human

save those 3 settings inside one CapCut template once and every video you export passes monetization review while everyone else cries about the "AI crackdown"

## 9. How to Make AI UGC Look Like NATURAL iPhone Footage (2027) 

The workflow for this got a lot easier in the last few months, so I decided to make an updated guide on it:

GPT Image 2.6 came out on November 4 with saved likenesses.

You build a creator once, save them, and pull them into any image with an @mention, so the face stays the same across every shot.

Seedance 2.5 holds a character far better than 2.0 did.

It does around 30 seconds in 1 pass and generates audio natively.

That means you're directing a full performance now.

And it also means every mistake in your prompt shows up for 30 seconds instead of 5.

Step 1: Study a Real Video First

Before you prompt anything, find a real UGC video in your niche that feels completely natural.

Watch it 5 times and write down:

Where the phone is: handheld, propped on a shelf, on the dashboard

How close the face is to the camera

What's in the background

Where the light comes from

How the person moves: leaning in, looking away, touching their hair

How they talk: pauses, restarts, filler words

This is your reference for everything that follows.

The point is learning what "real" actually looks like in your niche, so your prompts describe something specific instead of a generic idea of a person talking.

Step 2: Build the Creator in GPT Image 2.6

Design them with flaws

"A woman in her 20s" gets you the most average stock face ever generated.

Real people have imperfections, so write them in.

Photorealistic portrait of a woman, 28, dark brown hair in a messy bun with loose strands around her face, light freckles across her nose and cheeks, slightly uneven eyebrows, a small mark on her chin, no makeup except a little mascara.

Wearing a faded grey crewneck with a small stain near the collar.

Shot on an iPhone front camera at arm's length, slightly above eye level, a little off-center.

Soft daylight from a window on her left, natural shadows on the right side of her face.

Realistic skin texture with visible pores around the nose. Not a model. Not a professional photo.

Slightly messy bedroom behind her, out of focus.

Look at what's doing the work:

Freckles, uneven eyebrows, a small mark

A stain on the shirt

Off-center framing

Light from 1 side only

Every one of those is something a real photo has and a render doesn't.

Save the likeness

Once you have a face you'd believe, save it and give it a short name.

From now on every image uses the @mention, and the face doesn't drift.

Build the first frame

Your video starts from an image, so make that image look like the first frame of a real phone video.

@[CREATOR] sitting on the edge of her unmade bed, phone propped on a stack of books at chest height, filming herself.

Clothes thrown over a chair behind her, charger cable on the nightstand, half-empty water glass, a hair tie on the duvet.

Warm late-afternoon light through blinds from camera-left, soft stripes of shadow on the wall.

Phone camera quality, slight wide-angle distortion, framing a little too low. Realistic skin texture.

Clutter is the most underrated part of this.

A clean room reads as a film set.

A charger cable and a hair tie read as someone's actual bedroom.

Step 3: The Seedance 2.5 Prompt Blocks

Every Seedance prompt gets the same 8 blocks, in this order.

1. Skin texture

Without it, you get smooth, waxy skin that reads as AI in half a second.

Realistic skin texture, visible pores around the nose and cheeks, natural slight unevenness, no filter quality.

2. Anti-polish

This one kills the cinematic look.

Telling the model what it should NOT look like carries more weight than describing what it should.

Handheld phone camera feel, casual framing, filmed in a real environment, not a professional set, not cinematic, no color grading.

3. Lighting

Give lighting its own sentence, never a word in a list.

Direction, softness, shadows and how the skin looks under it.

Soft warm window light from camera-left, natural shadows across the face, no harsh highlights, skin lit without overexposure.

4. Clutter

Describe the room in specific objects.

Clothes on the chair behind her, charger cable on the nightstand, water glass on the side table, slightly unmade bed.

5. Camera motion

Locked-off framing is what production cameras give you.

Phones move.

Subtle handheld motion like a propped-up phone, slight micro-shake, occasional tiny reframe, no static locked-off shot.

6. Emotional arc

Without an arc, you get flat, neutral delivery for the whole clip.

On a 30 second take, that's the fastest way to lose people.

Tired and a bit annoyed at the start, curious in the middle, genuinely relieved at the end, small expression changes throughout, glances away from the camera once while thinking.

7. Consistency anchor

2.5 holds a character much better than 2.0, but locking it to your reference is still worth it.

Keep her exact appearance from @Image1, no drift, no face changes, no flickering.

8. Exclusions

Seedance adds things you never asked for.

And since 2.5 generates audio natively, the exclusions matter even more now.

No background music, no ambient noise, no text on screen, no captions, no logos, no watermarks.

The full template

@Image1 is the creator. [SCENE: where she is and how the phone is placed.]

Realistic skin texture, visible pores around the nose and cheeks, natural slight unevenness, no filter quality.

Handheld phone camera feel, casual framing, filmed in a real environment, not a professional set, not cinematic.

[LIGHTING: direction, softness, shadows, skin.]

[CLUTTER: 3 to 5 specific objects in the room.]

Subtle handheld motion like a propped-up phone, slight micro-shake, no static framing.

[EMOTIONAL ARC: start, middle, end.]

She says: "[SCRIPT]"

Keep her exact appearance from @Image1, no drift, no face changes, no flickering.

No background music, no ambient noise, no text on screen, no captions, no logos.

Step 4: Write a Script That Sounds Spoken

You can nail every visual detail and still get clocked in the first line.

Written scripts sound written.

Real people don't talk in clean, complete sentences.

What real speech has

Filler words: "honestly", "like", "okay so"

Restarts: "I was, okay, I was so skeptical"

Specific details: "8 months" instead of "a long time"

Contractions: "I'd", "didn't", "it's"

Short sentences, sometimes just 2 or 3 words

What gives it away

Marketing words like "game changer", "revolutionary", "must-have"

Perfect grammar from start to finish

Saying the product name 4 times

Every sentence the same length

Rewrite this script so it sounds like a real person talking to their phone, not reading.

Add 2 or 3 filler words, 1 small restart, and at least 1 specific detail (a number, a time, a place).
Use contractions. Vary sentence length.
Mention the product name once, maybe twice.
Remove any word that sounds like an ad.
Keep it under 30 seconds when spoken.

Script: [PASTE]

Bring the product in after the 8 second mark.

Show it too early and people decide it's an ad before the story even starts.

Step 5: Test Before You Pay for 30 Seconds

Seedance 2.5 costs about $0.168 per second, so a full 30 second take is around $5.

That adds up fast if you generate full takes on every idea.

Generate a 2 second test first.

Check the face, the skin and the motion.

If something is off, change the prompt before you pay for the full clip.

If a shot still isn't working after 2 attempts, kill it and move on.

Plan on 6 to 8 takes to get 1 you'd actually post.

For rough concept tests, use the cheap models first:

LTX 2.3 at about $0.01 per second

Kling 3.0 at about $0.10 per second

Seedance 2.5 only for the final version

Step 6: The Edit

The prompt gets you around 90% of the way.

The last 10% is the edit, and it takes 2 to 3 minutes per video.

Add a little more shake

Even with handheld motion in the prompt, AI footage tends to be smoother than a real phone.

A light shake effect at low intensity sells it.

Add grain

Low intensity grain breaks up the overly clean look.

Keep it subtle, or it looks like a filter.

Use a warm iPhone grade

Real iPhone footage leans slightly warm with soft contrast.

Skip anything that looks color graded.

Cut the first frame

The very first frame of an AI clip often looks a bit too still and perfect.

Trim it, so the video starts mid-movement like a real recording.

Native captions

Use TikTok's or Instagram's own caption style, not a fancy template.

Native captions look like a person posted it.

Designed captions look like a brand did.

Trending audio underneath

Put a trending sound quietly under the voice.

It helps distribution on TikTok, and it makes the video feel like part of the feed.

Step 7: The 2 Second Test

Before anything gets posted, show it to someone who doesn't know it's AI.

Ask 1 question:

"Would you scroll past this or watch it?"

Then watch their face in the first 2 seconds.

If they squint, something is off.

It's usually 1 of these:

The skin is too smooth

The eyes don't blink naturally

The room is too clean

The voice sounds read

The light is too even

Go back to the step that controls that part and change it.

Use EchoUGC For This

All of the above works.

It's also a lot of prompting, testing and editing if you need ads every week.

Upload 1 product photo, pick Talking, Demo or Unboxing, and get a finished, natural-looking ad back in under 10 minutes.

It's trained on our own ads and organic content, the stuff that's done billions of views and 12x ROAS, so the natural look is already built in.

Use the workflow above when you want full control over a creator.

Use EchoUGC when you just need finished ads that don't look like ads.

Check it out here: echougc.com.

Mistakes That Get AI UGC Noticed

Perfect faces.

No freckles, no marks, symmetrical everything.

Clean rooms.

If nothing in the background tells you someone lives there, it reads as a set.

Even lighting.

Real rooms have 1 main light source and shadows on the other side.

Locked-off camera.

Phones move, even on a shelf.

Flat delivery.

30 seconds of the same expression is a dead giveaway.

Written scripts.

If it reads well on paper, it probably sounds fake out loud.

Fancy captions.

They make a natural video look like an ad again.

The Short Version

Study a real video in your niche before prompting

Build the creator in GPT Image 2.6 with real flaws, and save the likeness

Make the first frame look like a real phone video, clutter included

Use all 8 prompt blocks in Seedance 2.5, every time

Write the script the way people actually talk

Test 2 seconds before paying for 30

Edit it: shake, grain, warm grade, trimmed first frame, native captions, trending audio

Run the 2 second test on someone who doesn't know it's AI

The models are good enough now.

The difference between AI UGC that converts and AI UGC that gets scrolled past is whether you pull the model off its defaults every single time.

## 10. Thread by @irabukht

[1/15] if you learn marketing engineering, you will never be unemployed again.

you need two things for all of it: claude code and a github account. marketers who use them well and build their own agents will make real money for their companies.

11 steps:

[2/15] step 1: a marketing agent that outmarkets other agents

- learn: claude code, connecting it to your meta and google ads accounts, running it on a schedule, spend limits, a log of everything it does.

- practice: an agent that checks the account every hour, pauses ads that cost over 3x your target price per customer after 50 conversions, raises budget 20% on ads that won three days in a row, writes three new versions of the best ad and posts every action to slack with the reason. then feed it what the competition's agent doesn't have: your review data, your competitor scrape and what a customer really costs you across all channels.

- why: your competitors' accounts are already run by agents in the same auction. two agents with the same rules and the same meta data tie, and the one with better inputs wins.

[3/15] step 2: programmatic seo (hundreds of google pages from one spreadsheet)

- learn: claude code, github, vercel, a spreadsheet as your data source, google search console.

- practice: give claude code a 500-row spreadsheet (cities, products, comparisons) and have it build one page per row with real data, like "crm for dentists" or "hubspot vs salesforce". publish, then watch search console for 60 days and fix the pages google drops.

- why: google indexes thin pages slowly and removes them later. the work is in the data and the checking, the pages take an afternoon.

[4/15] step 3: ai video ads at scale

- learn: veo, seedance, kling, elevenlabs voices, auto captions, a script that runs them in batches.

- practice: one product photo and a 30-second script become 100 ugc-style video ads with different hooks, voices and openings, ready in 9:16 and 4:5.

- why: a studio shoot costs $20k and gives you ten videos. the same budget in api calls gives you a thousand, and the account that tests more creatives usually wins on meta.

[5/15] step 4: rebuilding competitor winners

- learn: meta ad library, a scraper claude code writes for you, image and video generation.

- practice: scrape every ad from 30 competitors, keep the ones running for over 60 days, and have ai rebuild each one for your product with your own footage and claims. upload them paused.

- why: running time is the only public performance signal. nobody keeps paying for an ad that loses money, so an ad live for two months is paying for itself.

[6/15] step 5: getting cited by chatgpt

- learn: a script that asks chatgpt, claude and perplexity questions and saves the answers, llms.txt, schema markup.

- practice: ask all three 100 real buyer questions every monday and log which brands and urls get cited. then rewrite your ten most important pages to match the cited ones.

- why: buyers ask ai before they search. cited pages share the same traits (a direct answer in the first paragraph, original numbers, a clear author), and you can only copy them once you measure.

[7/15] step 6: customer research from 5,000 reviews

- learn: scraping reviews from amazon, trustpilot, reddit and the app store, grouping them by topic with ai.

- practice: pull 5,000 reviews of your product and your competitors, sort them into the ten most common complaints and ten reasons to buy, and write 50 ad hooks in the customers' exact words.

- why: copywriters guess at why people buy. the reviews already say it, and most teams have never read more than 50 of them.

[8/15] step 7: landing pages that match the ad

- learn: a/b testing, how many visitors a test needs before you can trust it, posthog, ai-written page variants.

- practice: a landing page that rewrites its headline and first section to match the ad the visitor clicked, tested with a sample size you calculated in advance.

- why: the ad promises one thing and the page says another, and that gap is where most paid traffic leaves. fixing it is cheaper than any increase in spend.

[9/15] step 8: conversion tracking

- learn: google tag manager, ga4, server-side tracking (stape), meta conversions api.

- practice: send the purchase event from the browser and the server with the same event id, and confirm in events manager that each order counts once and match quality is above 7.

- why: everything in steps 1 to 7 is optimized on these events. most accounts send duplicates or miss a quarter of orders, and the algorithm learns from wrong data.

[10/15] step 9: email and sms that trigger on behavior

- learn: klaviyo, product events (viewed, added to cart, bought, went quiet), holdout groups.

- practice: five flows that fire on what a customer did, with a 10% holdout that gets nothing, so you see what the flows add in revenue.

- why: selling again to someone who already bought costs a fraction of finding a new customer. most stores have one abandoned cart email and nothing else.

[11/15] step 10: incrementality (how many sales your ads really added)

- learn: geo holdout tests, marketing mix modeling (meridian, robyn), the gap between what a platform claims and what it added.

- practice: turn meta off in five matched regions for four weeks and compare the sales drop to what meta said it was driving.

- why: platforms usually take credit for two to five times more sales than they drove. the cfo will ask for the real number, and whoever has it keeps the budget.

[12/15] step 11: automated reporting

- learn: looker studio or metabase, a slack bot, ai writing the summary from the numbers.

- practice: a monday report that compares last week to the week before, explains what changed and suggests the next test, posted to slack before anyone logs in.

- why: about half a marketing team's hours go to pulling numbers into slides. automating it is the fastest way to get noticed.

## 12. Thread by @HowToPrompt__

[1/2] A toothpaste company has quietly killed the entire market research industry and nobody is talking about it.

Colgate published a paper showing you can predict real purchase intent by simply asking LLMs to roleplay customers.

And this is beyond insane.

If you ask an AI, "Rate this product from 1 to 5," it gives safe, middle-of-the-road garbage.

So researchers invented a method called Semantic Similarity Rating (SSR).

Instead of asking the AI for a number, they asked it to roleplay.

They gave the LLM a demographic profile. They showed it a product concept. And they asked it to write down its raw, unfiltered thoughts.

Then, they used a semantic model to translate those written thoughts into a numerical score.

The results are staggering.

Tested against 57 real personal care product surveys and 9,300 human responses, the synthetic consumers hit 90% of human test-retest reliability.

They perfectly mirrored how different age brackets and income levels react to price changes.

And they provided detailed, qualitative feedback that was deeper and more critical than what actual humans wrote.

This destroys the economics of traditional market research.

You don't need to wait a month to see if a product will sell.

You can simulate 1,000 hyper-targeted customer interviews overnight.

You can A/B test pricing across every demographic instantly.

## 14. Thread by @noelcetaSEO

[1/20] From 5 clicks a month to 21,540 in 16 days.

Google's core update kicked off Nov 10 and my feed is full of 80%+ drops, while this site is literally still climbing.

No course, no "DM me for the template", the whole playbook's below. Step 6 is the one competitors can't copy.

[2/20] Nov 1-16 already beat all of October (18,269). Over 16 months that's 53.3K clicks and 4M impressions, with 0 paid ads.

And this is how traffic grows when you don't let AI do your entire SEO.

[3/20] 1. Take the exact search people type and make it your homepage H1 and title. Then turn your H2s into the questions they ask and add an FAQ, dead simple.

Ours went from 10 to 17 questions.

[4/20] 2. Fix the basics before you write anything new. Crawl your own site and make sure every page has one H1, menus are real links (not JavaScript buttons) and pages are prerendered.

One of ours had 11 H1s, and response time dropped from 0.5s+ to 0.1-0.2s.

[5/20] 3. Kill your duplicate pages. Find pages sharing a title or description, give each its own URL, title and description, then redirect the old URLs.

We had 22 product pages sharing ONE title, which is just sad.

[6/20] 4. Give every comparison search its own page. Write the spec first (sections, FAQs, internal links, a verdict), then build every page to it so none ends up thin.

We built 136 pages to one spec: 8 sections, 6 FAQs, 8+ internal links.

[7/20] 5. Build hubs. Group related pages under a hub with a guide section and an FAQ, and show related posts under every blog post so no page sits on its own.

We built 16 hubs at 1,500-1,900 words each.

[8/20] 6. Publish data nobody else has. Use what only you have, like your users' results, your own numbers, your own tool. If Google can get it elsewhere, why would it rank you?

Ours is a tool with live averages from our own users, and nobody can copy that.

[9/20] 7. Translate what already works. Check Search Console for countries already showing impressions, translate your proven pages for them, add hreflang, then actually review the translations.

We run 15 languages and one review pass fixed about 1,800 strings.

[10/20] 8. Keep the blog going. Get real people to write it, on a schedule, at a pace you can keep for a year and not just a week.

Ours ran on a schedule the whole time, so stop overthinking it and just keep publishing.

[11/20] So where does AI fit? Let it type: the code, drafts to your spec, translations, and a check of every page against that spec.

You decide which searches matter, which page answers each, the spec, and what never ships (made-up stats, for one).

Don't let AI decide. Let it type.

[12/20] Bookmark this for the next core update.

And tell me what this one did to your site.

## 21. Thread by @starter_story

[1/2] I chatted with a founder whose first iOS app makes $2,000 a day, with zero ads and zero TikToks.

His name is Rasty. He grew up in a village of 700 people in Slovakia, worked on the Gmail team at Google, and sold startups to Amazon and others.

But he’d never built an iPhone app until this March.

His app, Vaultaire, is about as simple as it gets: an on-device encrypted vault for photos and files. No servers. No shared keys. Even he can’t see what’s inside. (An FBI agent once called asking for help getting into a vault. He couldn’t.)

He built it in 3 weeks with coding agents. Month 1: $66. Six months later: $41K in a single month. 60,000+ downloads.

How did it grow? SEO, the thing everyone says is dead.

The big apps in his category are 15+ years old and ignore the web. So he went after every keyword they skipped:

> Built a simple marketing site plus a series of “how do I…” guides
> Wrote his own open-source tool (gum) that checks Google Keyword Planner + Search every morning
> Finds keywords where he’s getting impressions but no clicks
> Claude writes ONE high-quality guide a day. No AI slop.
> Internal links point to the new guide, and Google picks it up in 3–4 days
> CTAs go where readers usually stop scrolling. About 10% of web visitors convert.

He hasn’t written a single line of code or a single word on the site himself.

And about 60% of his sales are lifetime purchases, bringing in over 80% of revenue. Day-1 conversion is only 5.47%, but it keeps climbing all the way to day 60.

His #1 advice: “Start. That’s the hardest part. As long as they are not out there, they don’t exist.”

## 22. Thread by @brivael

A few hours ago, I wrote: "They're going to pull it off. Jensen Huang is going to be pushed out of Nvidia."

It's done.

Officially, Jensen Huang resigned. In reality, the board was getting ready to throw him out. Effective immediately. He's also leaving the board. The CFO is stepping in as interim CEO. The board is even considering bringing in an outside CEO.

Thirty-three years. A company born in a Denny's, saved from bankruptcy more than once, turned into the most important company of the AI era. Wiped away in a week.

Whatever you think of his private life, look at the mechanics.

A scandal breaks. Pressure builds in Washington. Columnists demand his head. The board, which never prepared a successor, panics. The founder is out. And the man who fought the restrictions, who defended open models, who stood up to Washington, is gone.

The playbook worked. And when a playbook works, it gets used again.

The next target is Elon.

Why him? Because he's now the last builder of that scale who holds the entire chain. Rockets, satellites, energy, robots, AI, and soon chips with Terafab. He's the only one who can build both the superintelligence and the rocket that carries it into space. And he just said publicly that hiring Jensen would be an honor.

For those who want to slow everything down, there's no bigger target.

But they have a problem. You don't fire Elon Musk the way you fire Jensen Huang. He holds voting control at SpaceX. No board can throw him out overnight.

So they'll come at him another way. Regulators. Courts. Investigative committees. Government contracts. Media campaigns. Every weakness will be exploited, every mistake amplified, every procedure dragged out. They don't need to fire him. They just need to wear him down, slow him down, make every project ten times more expensive.

Open your eyes, because this is going to move very fast.

What we're living through right now isn't news. It's History.

In twenty years, this week will be taught in schools. The day America, in the middle of the race to superintelligence against China, took the man who made the chips for the entire planet out of the race. And people will ask what happened next.

The answer depends on what happens to Elon. And on what Jensen decides to do with the rest of his life.

I'm standing by my prediction.

We are living through History with a capital H.

## 24. Thread by @SKatalystAI

[1/18] Dumb Fable 5.2 UI/UX prompts:
&gt;"make my dashboard look modern"
&gt;"improve this form"
&gt;"make it work on mobile"
&gt;"make it accessible"

Good Fable 5.2 UI/UX prompts 👇

[2/18] 1-Audit this dashboard screenshot. List the 5 biggest hierarchy and density problems first. Then define a spacing scale, type scale and max 5 color tokens. Rebuild using only those. No gradients. Screenshot at 1440 and 390, fix what looks off

[3/18] 2-Rework this form for completion rate. Single column, labels above inputs, validate on blur, not keystroke. Errors say what to do. Add focus, disabled, loading and error states. Don't change field order or API calls

[4/18] 3-Make this responsive at 390, 768 and 1440. Tap targets 44px+. Tables become stacked cards under 768. No horizontal scroll. Don't touch the desktop layout. Screenshot each breakpoint and list what broke

[5/18] 4-Run a WCAG 2.2 AA pass. Fix contrast under 4.5:1, add visible focus rings, keyboard nav and aria labels on icon buttons. Respect reduced motion. Output a table: issue, before, after

[1/14] Beto's advice for a first app this month: a demo login, real notes and a screen recording. So I pulled all 59 versions of my 16 live apps through the API to see what I sent Apple.

No recording and no demo login in any of them. 6 of the apps went live with empty notes, including the one that earns the most.

As the rejections came, the notes grew. The median note was 512 characters in July and 2,422 in October. What's in them now:

1. What the app does, in one sentence.
2. Numbered steps from first launch to the result, paywall included.
3. Something to test with. A reviewer has no pills on their desk, so my pill counter comes with a sample tray and the notes link to a photo of it.
4. Whether there's an account. 13 of the 16 say there's none.
5. What leaves the phone. My tick app says the photo goes to my server and isn't stored.
6. What the app is not. "An educational aid, not a diagnosis."
7. For an update, what changed. "This version changes only the App Store listing."

After a spam rejection I add how the app differs from the others. My pill counter got through with that paragraph.

"best ai data labs"

"best data labelling for ai"
"best data labelling platform"
"best data labelling software"
"best data labelling tools"
"best data brokers"
"best enterprise fine tuning services"

we noticed the massive amounts of $ flowing into the "sell your data" space from the likes of @mercor and @micro1_ai 

and immediately knew that the traffic and visibility in this space for keywords like "best data labs" is highly valuable

everyone is racing to either sell or acquire data, but the toughest challenge for these parties is to find one another

1) data sellers want a counterparty that is trustworthy and reliable

2) data buyers want to get their hands on as much data as possible (various data types & counterparties)

these transactions come with high urgency and high avg. ticket sizes

the combination of all these make it the perfect niche to secure positioning in!

so we got to work and:

1) secured highly valuable exact match domains (they match the search intent keywords 1:1)

2) deployed highly relevant, carefully architected content that induces lexical proximity to the keywords of interest (convincing the search engines + LLMs that our information on the topic is rich and relevant)

3) focused on clean and functional entity mapping that consistently and coherently links our entity to the desired keywords

4) waited for 10 days

and now we rank for all these high value keywords and search intent!

we have since received offers by neolabs to rent the space on these domains and a 7 figure offer for acquiring these assets outright

when we asked why, they explained that securing just one highly relevant lead from this type of positioning can yield an asymmetric return and more than offset the costs

what a wild time to be alive!

[2/23] Step 1: pick the season before the idea.

Seasonal buyers have a deadline, so they buy fast and don’t shop around. Start with what people need to get through December, not what you find fun.

Here’s a real example:

[3/23] This Halloween origami book ranks #13,414 in Books, sells an estimated 16-20 copies a day, and shows about $78 a day in royalties. It’s 84 pages and only 2 months old. 

[4/23] Look at what the cover promises:

a number (24 projects), a reader (kids and families), and a result (spooky folding projects anyone can finish). Buyers know in 2 seconds if it’s for them. Copy the pattern, not the book.

Christmas is around the corner, use it. Here's how:

[5/23] Ideas on that pattern:

Christmas origami for kids, cookie recipes for beginners, crochet ornaments, a 24-day family advent activity book, hosting Christmas dinner on a budget, carols for beginner piano.

[9/23] Step 6: make the cover in Ideogram, free. Study the top Christmas covers in your niche. Bold title, a number badge, a festive color scheme, one clear image.

On Amazon the cover is your ad.

[10/23] Step 7: upload to KDP under a pen name and tick the AI disclosure box. Price the paperback at $15.99. KDP reviews can take up to 72 hours, so publish by mid-November to give print copies time to ship before Christmas.

[11/23] Step 8: launch fast, because the season won’t wait. Run $5 a day in Amazon ads on Christmas keywords, share it in 3 niche Facebook groups, and message 10-15 people for honest reviews. Never buy reviews.

[12/23] The math:

$15.99 × 5 copies a day is about $80 a day, or $2,398 a month in gross revenue. Your royalty is lower after printing and Amazon’s cut, so check KDP’s calculator. 

The Halloween book sells 3-4 times that pace.

[13/23] Then keep going. Seasonal books come back every year. Build a calendar:

Christmas, Valentine’s Day, Easter, Mother’s Day, Halloween. Each one sells again next season, and evergreen titles fill the gaps between.

## 35. Thread by @asaio87

[1/13] internal linking is the most underrated SEO trick you can pull, and almost nobody does it

because it's boring

So I made Claude do it for me, every week

here is the exact setup (prompt included, bookmark this):

1/ pull your Google Search Console data, either an export or a GSC MCP

2/ connect your website to claude with an mcp

i use wordpress (sometimes I use Astro too) with a custom mcp plugin, but there are connectors for almost every blogging platform

3/ paste this prompt:

"I've attached my Google Search Console export for the last 3 months. My website is connected through the [site name] MCP.

1. For each article, find the queries where it ranks best (position 1–10, decent impressions). Those are its target anchors.
2. Go through every published post. Wherever the text naturally mentions another article's top query (or a close variant), turn that phrase into a link to that article.
3. Rules:
- Max 3–5 new internal links per post, spread through the body (no headings, intro sentence, or existing links)
- Never link a post to itself, only link each target once per post
- Anchor text must read naturally. Don't rewrite sentences to force a keyword in
- Prioritize sending links to pages ranking 4–15
4. Before changing anything, show me a table: source post → anchor → target URL → target's query and position. Wait for my OK.
5. After I approve, make the edits through the MCP and give me a changelog so I can revert."

4/ check the table, say ok, claude makes the edits

5/ leave the site alone for a few days

6/ repeat every week

thats it

the pages sitting on position 4-20 are the ones that move the most, and they're already on your site

you just need to point at them

still build backlinks and keep publishing; internal linking just makes all of that work harder

i run this exact process on my sites and my customer sites

what's stopping you from doing internal linking right now? is it the connection to your site ?

## 41. Thread by @CristianRus4

[1/17] Left has over 2.8K reviews on the App Store, with a 4.7 rating

my method for getting reviews is simple and it works. no dodgy tricks. it respects Apple's guidelines and the user experience, and you don't need to measure and stress with "aha! moments" or reward triggers 

I tried all of that before and honestly I don't think any of it worked. the biggest jump in reviews (and in positive ones) came in the last few months, after this change, I know because I track it

here's how:

1. make a "What's New?" screen that shows after major updates. not after every update, only a few times a year, when you ship something big like a major new feature. I version my app as 2026.XX.YY so whenever XX changes it appears this screen

2. make your own review screen. show reviews from other users and community proof (if you have them!)

3. connect the two. show the review screen right after the "What's New?" screen every time, unless the user is in a cooldown because they already left a review

this gets you 5 stars almost every time because:
- it never interrupts someone while they're actually using the app
- it shows up right after good news about new features, which shows you care about the app
- only people who actually use your app see it, because no one else opens the app after an update

two more tips:
- notice the "Send feedback" button under the CTA button, pretty explanatory
- after rating, people can close the screen or tap the CTA button again. it now says "Write a review", so they can add a written review if they want. never force it

try it and let me know how it goes!

## 43. The Ultimate Guide to ASO (App Store Optimization).

I know, you built your app, you're excited about it, and you're probably thinking:

"Yo, this app is going to make me a millionaire."

I know because I've been there too.

And I still am, don't get me wrong ahaha. I'm not a millionaire yet.

But I've learned A LOT during this journey, and there are a few things I wish someone had told me before publishing my first app.

If you ever thought that just by publishing your app people would magically find it and start downloading it, well... you were completely wrong.

Nowadays everyone can build apps.

I don't know if your idea is actually a good fit for the market or not, but that's a story for another day.

What I can tell you is that, besides all the chaotic distribution strategies you're probably trying right now, there's a good chance you've completely skipped ASO (App Store Optimization).

Or maybe you're doing it, but you're doing it wrong.

Don't worry, because I'm going to share pretty much everything I've learned about ASO so far, including the mistakes I've made with my own apps.

Hopefully, by the end of this article, you'll have a much clearer idea of what you should be doing to get more organic downloads.

Enough talking, let's jump in.

1. Understanding the App Store Hierarchy

First of all, you need to understand how the App Store works.

Not every field on your App Store listing has the same importance when it comes to keywords.

Some fields are generally considered more influential than others.

And no, putting your keywords everywhere isn't going to magically make you rank #1.

Here's how I think about it:

The App Title

This is one of the most important places to put your main keyword. Apple gives you 30 characters, so don't waste them.

If your app is a habit tracker, you probably want people searching for "habit tracker" to understand what your app does immediately.

The Subtitle

The subtitle also plays an important role.

You get another 30 characters to explain what your app does and target relevant keywords.

It's generally considered less influential than the title, but still very important.

The Keywords Field

Here you get 100 characters.

And trust me, those 100 characters are worth using properly.

You want to include relevant keywords that people are actually searching for.

Apple can also combine words from your metadata to match different searches, so you don't necessarily need to write every possible keyword combination.

I'll explain this better in a second.

Promotional Text & Description

Here's something that might save you some time.

On Apple's App Store, the description and promotional text aren't direct keyword-ranking fields.

So stop stuffing them with keywords, lol.

That doesn't mean they're useless.

Your description should explain why someone should download your app, while promotional text can highlight updates or important features.

Just remember: we're trying to convince real people to download our apps, not only algorithms.

And one more thing: this is about Apple's App Store. Google Play works differently, especially when it comes to descriptions.

2. How to Optimize Your App Title for Search

One mistake I've seen people make is putting their beautiful app name first without explaining what the app actually does.

Yeah, I know.

You spent three days choosing that name, found the perfect domain, and now you want everyone to see it.

I get it ahaha.

But here's the problem.

Nobody knows your app exists yet.

Think about Duolingo.

Duolingo has the luxury of putting its brand first because millions of people already know what Duolingo is.

People literally search for "Duolingo."

We're not there yet.

At least I'm not lol.

So instead of relying only on your brand name, try making your main keyword more visible.

For example:

Habit Tracker: MyApp

Instead of:

MyApp: Habit Tracker

This isn't some magical trick that guarantees better rankings just because the keyword comes first.

But it immediately tells people what your app does, and that's important when someone is scrolling through search results.

Keep the title natural, relevant, and within Apple's 30-character limit.

Hopefully, one day we'll have the luxury of people searching directly for our beautiful app names.

3. Stop Wasting Your 100 Keyword Characters

Now let's talk about the keywords field.

For each localization, Apple gives you 100 characters, and you should try to make good use of them.

But here's something interesting, you don't always have to enter complete search phrases.

For example, let's say you have a habit tracker.

Instead of writing:

"habit tracker, habit goals, habit routine"

You could use individual relevant words separated by commas, avoiding words you've already included in your title or subtitle.

Something like:

"routine,goals,daily,streaks,planner"

Why?

Because Apple can combine keywords from different fields and match multiple search queries.

So you're potentially targeting more searches without wasting characters repeating the same words.

Of course, that doesn't mean every possible combination will rank.

You still need to choose relevant keywords, and some exact phrases may be worth keeping together.

But in general:

Don't waste characters repeating words you've already used.

Oh, and one more thing.

Don't just target the keywords with the highest search volume.

If you're competing against apps with millions of downloads, good luck ranking above them from day one.

Sometimes a smaller keyword with less competition is a much better opportunity.

More about that later.

4. Your Screenshots Matter More Than You Think

Okay, now imagine this.

Someone searches for a keyword.

Your app appears in the results.

They see your icon, title, and screenshots.

And then... they keep scrolling.

Why?

Maybe because your screenshots look terrible.

I'm not saying yours do, but you get the point lol.

Your screenshots are one of the first things potential users see, and they can make a huge difference in whether someone downloads your app.

Here's what I suggest:

Focus on the first 3 screenshots. Depending on the App Store layout, users may see up to three screenshots directly in search results.

Make the message clear. People should understand your app's value without having to read five paragraphs.

Avoid tiny text. Remember that people are looking at these screenshots on their phones.

Use good contrast. Don't put light gray text on a white background and expect people to read it.

Show benefits, not just features. Explain what the user gets from using your app.

Here's how the first three screenshots appear in the App Store.

For example, instead of writing:

"Advanced Analytics Dashboard"

You could write:

"See How Much Money You've Saved"

The second one gives people an actual reason to care.

Another mistake is trying to explain EVERYTHING in the first screenshot.

You don't need to.

You just need to make someone interested enough to keep looking.

If you're unsure whether your screenshots are actually good, I built a free tool that analyzes your App Store listing and shows you what you could improve.

You can try it here:

https://www.minimalistscreens.com/tools/app-store-grader

And remember, getting people to find your app is only half the job.

You still need to convince them to download it.

5. Reviews Are Important Too

Every app has its own visibility in the App Store, and ratings and reviews are among the signals Apple considers.

But they're also important for something else.

--- Trust. ---

Imagine finding two similar apps.

One has 4.8 stars and hundreds of reviews.

The other has 3.2 stars and almost no reviews.

Which one would you download?

Exactly.

I'm personally working on this with my own app too.

One thing you can do is ask your users for a review directly inside your app.

But please, don't ask them immediately after opening it for the first time.

That's annoying.

Wait until they've actually experienced something useful.

Maybe they've completed a task, reached a milestone, or achieved something with your app.

That's a much better moment to ask.

You can use Apple's native review prompt to make the process easier.

And obviously, the goal is to get as many genuine positive reviews as possible.

Hopefully five stars ahaha.

Just don't try to manipulate reviews or reward users for positive ratings.

Build something people enjoy using, and make it easy for them to share their experience.

6. Localization Doesn't Mean Translating Word by Word

This is probably one of the most interesting things I've learned recently.

I've asked quite a few developers how they localize their apps.

And most of them were like:

"Well, I just translate everything from English."

That's it.

But here's the problem.

People in different countries don't necessarily search for the same things.

Even when they're looking for exactly the same type of app.

Let's say your English keyword is:

"Budget Tracker"

You can't automatically assume that its literal translation is the most searched term in Germany, Italy, or France.

Maybe people use another expression.

Maybe they search for something more specific.

Or maybe they use an English keyword even though their native language is different.

I've personally noticed this while researching keywords for my app, Smoke Off.

Some English keywords were getting searched in Italy, and that opened up opportunities I hadn't considered before.

So here's what I'd suggest.

Before localizing:

Research the keywords people actually use in that market.

Compare their popularity and difficulty.

Choose the ones that make sense for your app.

Adapt your title, subtitle, and keywords accordingly.

Don't forget to localize your screenshots too.

You can use ASO tools like Minimalist Screens to research keywords across different markets.

Or, if you don't have access to a tool yet, ask an AI to help you brainstorm relevant keywords in a particular language.

Just don't blindly trust the suggestions.

AI can give you ideas, but it doesn't automatically know which keywords have the best search volume or competition.

You'll still need to validate them.

Also, keep in mind that App Store localizations depend on supported languages and storefronts, not simply one independent keyword field for every country.

So check which languages Apple supports in your target markets.

The main point is simple:

Translate for how people search, not just how words are written.

7. Localize Your Pricing Too

Now here's something that some developers might disagree with me on.

I think you should seriously consider localizing your pricing.

Unless you have significant AI costs, infrastructure costs, or other expenses that make it difficult.

Think about it.

Someone living in a country with lower purchasing power might love your app but find your subscription too expensive.

And you might be thinking:

"But I want to make exactly €10 from every subscription."

Yeah, I get it.

But would you rather get €5 from someone who otherwise wouldn't subscribe, or €0?

I know what I'd choose.

Less money is better than no money.

Of course, you still need to make sure you're profitable.

Here's a simple framework I'd start testing with:

These are just starting points, not universal rules.

You shouldn't randomly throw countries into tiers either.

Research purchasing power, local competitors, subscription behavior, and your actual costs.

For example, if your base subscription is €10/month, you might test a lower price in a market where €10 is relatively expensive.

Apple already handles currency conversions and certain tax adjustments, but that's not the same as optimizing your prices for purchasing power.

With Minimalist Screens, you can explore localized pricing and adjust your pricing strategy for different markets.

I think it's worth experimenting with, especially when you're trying to reach users outside your main market.

8. How I Actually Do Keyword Research

Okay, we talked about keywords a lot.

But how do you actually find the right ones?

There are plenty of ASO tools out there, and you can use whichever works best for you.

Personally, I built Minimalist Screens with my brother because I wanted to have everything in one place, without paying crazy prices for multiple tools.

We wanted to make ASO more accessible and affordable for indie developers like us.

Here's how I approach keyword research.

First, I look for relevant keywords.

I start with what my app actually does.

For Smoke Off, for example, I'd research terms related to quitting smoking, tracking cigarettes, cravings, and similar topics.

The idea is to find keywords with real search intent.

Then I look at popularity.

Obviously, we want keywords that people are searching for.

There's no point ranking #1 for a keyword nobody searches.

After that, I check difficulty.

This is where it gets interesting.

A keyword might have amazing popularity, but if the competition is too strong, it might be almost impossible for a new app to rank well.

That's why I prefer finding a balance between demand and competition.

Finally, I look at opportunity.

Minimalist Screens has an opportunity metric that helps me compare keywords based on their potential.

It makes it easier to decide which keywords are worth targeting and which ones I should probably leave alone for now.

My general strategy is pretty simple:

Find relevant keywords with decent demand and competition that my app has a realistic chance of handling.

I don't always go for the biggest ones.

Especially when the app is still small.

You can always target more competitive keywords as your app grows.

9. Be Patient, Track Your Keywords, and Keep Improving

One last thing.

ASO takes time.

Please don't change your title today and expect to wake up tomorrow with a million downloads.

You need to test things, understand what's working, and keep improving.

That's why tracking your keywords is important.

For example, let's say your app is ranking #47 for a keyword.

After making some changes, you move to #28.

Then #16.

That's useful information.

It doesn't necessarily mean every improvement came from your metadata changes, but at least you can start understanding how your visibility is changing.

And if your rankings aren't improving?

Maybe your keywords are too competitive.

Maybe you're targeting the wrong search intent.

Or maybe there are better opportunities you haven't found yet.

That's why I added keyword tracking to Minimalist Screens.

You can monitor your rankings over time instead of manually searching for your app every single day.

But don't forget to measure actual results too.

Better rankings are nice.

More downloads are even nicer.

And more paying users?

Well, that's the goal lol.

10. Don't Forget About the Actual App

Before wrapping this up, there's one more thing I want to say.

You can have amazing screenshots.

Perfectly optimized keywords.

Great localization.

And even a really good pricing strategy.

But if your app is terrible and nobody actually wants it, ASO isn't going to save you.

I know that's not what everyone wants to hear.

But it's true.

ASO helps people discover your app and can improve your chances of converting them into users.

It doesn't replace having a product that solves a real problem.

So keep improving your app while working on your distribution.

You need both.

One Last Thing Before You Go

That's pretty much everything I wanted to share about ASO.

Or at least the most important things I've learned so far.

I'm still learning too, and I'm constantly experimenting with my own apps.

Some things work.

Some don't.

If there's one thing I want you to remember from this article, it's this:

Building your app is only the beginning. Getting people to discover it is a completely different challenge.

And that's exactly why I'm building Minimalist Screens.

I wanted one place where indie developers like me could research keywords, track rankings, improve screenshots, and work on localization without having to jump between a bunch of different tools.

If you found this guide useful, I'd really appreciate a like and a repost!

I spent quite some time putting everything together, so it would mean a lot if you could help me reach more indie developers.

Hope it helps you get some more downloads ahaha.

## 45. Thread by @IntCyberDigest

[1/2] ‼️ Ubuntu and Red Hat still have no fix for a Linux kernel flaw that Anthropic says its unreleased AI model turned into a remote root exploit.

The bug, CVE-2026-98432, is a use-after-free in the IPv4 TCP handshake, rated 9.8 out of 10.

The exploit needs no credentials and targets servers with a multipath TCP listener, which Go 1.24 and later open by default.

The fix reached stable kernels on 11 October and Debian has shipped it. Ubuntu still marks every release from 16.04 to 26.10 vulnerable.

Red Hat now rates it Critical but has no patched kernel for RHEL 8, 9 or 10.

Anthropic has not released the exploit. By its count, it takes 48 minutes to leak a kernel pointer and five more to run commands as root.

[1/5] Stageshow: a skill that helps your agent make better faux UI stills, animations, bento grids, and more. 

npx skills add benjitaylor/skills --skill stageshow 

[2/5] Models can do these now, but they may look heavy-handed, lack smooth animations, or simply don’t make sense. With Stageshow, the same prompts can produce far better results. 

Eg. Before and after... 

[3/5] The skill walks the agent through how I'd make one by hand, from picking the look and and planning the story in a few beats to starting from a solid example, then has it film the result and fix what it finds. 

[4/5] One way I tested it was by recreating small moments from apps I use daily, like Figma, Gmail, Finder and Spotify, etc. I found Stageshow to consistently outperform the same prompts that didn’t have the skill. 

[5/5] As model taste improves, I suspect it won’t be long until this skill is no longer needed. With that said, every demo made with it is a small, well-built example of this kind of UI, which is the sort of thing models learn from. So if it plays a small part in making itself unnecessary, that seems like a fair trade!

Drop your footage, get highlights, edit together, send links for feedback, and let your agents cook.

AI is becoming a great creative partner. Kino is a new home for your team and agents to collaborate. 

[2/22] We used Kino to make this launch video. Now it’s your turn: 

Here’s how it works and how we got here after starting in Hollywood

[3/22] Kino is the single place you can store, edit, and review footage for your videos. Your teammates and Claude Code work on the same timeline, with the footage and the notes right here.

[4/22] Kino is multiplayer. For our launch I had 5+ agents editing the timeline while Donald had his own swarm working a few minutes further down. His agent did sound design, mine did the rotoscoping VFX, and we both made small fixes by hand. 

[5/22] Every edit decision in Kino is code with a real timeline on top, so you can change any decision by hand. You can let it go big – for the launch video I had Kino build itself inside of Kino to mock the interface, then make a hype reel of our product as if it was a Mac app from 2009.

[6/22] We didn’t start here. I co-directed a documentary at MIT, then we ran Kino on shows like Survivor and A24’s Beef. We were forward-deployed like Palantir, flying to LA constantly and integrating with legacy video editors. Frontier models weren’t allowed, and we couldn’t make editors >10x faster. ## 53. Thread by @SamuelZengML

[1/5] Introducing Audio8-TTS-Realistic, @Edge0_AI’s new open-weight speech model: realistic, zero-shot voice cloning in just 0.6B parameters.

• Human-like prosody and expressive delivery
• Clones a voice from one short clip, in 23 languages
• 601M params: small enough to fine-tune and serve affordably

Currently #1 on the Hugging Face TTS Arena, where people vote blind on which voice sounds more human.

Open weights are available now on @huggingface. Try it free.

[2/5] Audio8-TTS-Realistic is now open source.

🤗 Weights: 
📄 Paper: 
🎧 Samples: 
💻 Code: 
▶️ Playground: 

[3/5] How it learns to sound human:

TTS is usually trained on two things: the right words (WER) and the right voice (similarity).

But neither captures rhythm, pauses, or timing.

So we trained a realism critic that scores each output against a real person saying the same sentence in the same voice—ranked via a blind listening study.

Then: SFT → GRPO on accuracy + similarity → GRPO on the critic.

That last stage only saw Chinese and English prompts, yet it improved all 13 lower-resource languages.

[4/5] How it stacks up at 0.6B:

• CV3 (9 languages): 4.86% error — ahead of CosyVoice3-1.5B (6.89%), Qwen3-TTS-1.7B (7.73%), and VoxCPM2 (5.35%)
• MiniMax (24 languages): 5.89% error — vs. 27.41% for Qwen3-TTS-1.7B
• Runs in ~1.1 GB RAM on an Apple M2 CPU (INT4)

Larger 4–8B models still lead on some sets, but we’re within reach at a fraction of the size.

[5/5] The SFT training code is also included in this version. Enjoy!

## 56. Thread by @queniferlungg

[1/7] your sign to redo your hooks in flopped reels 

a breakdown of the 3/3 rule 

[2/7] there are 3 types of hooks in the first 3 seconds

1. written (text on screen)
2. visual
3. spoken

your goal is to minimise friction so viewers don't scroll

[3/7] 1. written (text on screen)

&gt; readable font only
&gt; too many animations = friction
&gt; concise

[4/7] 2. visual 

&gt; give immediate context to written hook
&gt; first thing that you imagine reading the hook 
&gt; use pinterest for this 

[5/7] 3. spoken

&gt; turns written hook from an abstract idea → familiar 
&gt; makes you feel approachable + viewer lets their guard down 
&gt; hardest one out of the 3 to crack

## 58. Thread by @wespreadjam

[1/19] Granola, a $1.5B company, increased monthly new users from AI search by 4,600% in JUST 7 months.

here’s the genius AEO workflow behind it:

1. They started with buyer decisions, not keywords

-> Granola mapped the questions people ask before choosing an AI meeting tool

-> Then they turned those same decisions into both Google keywords and AI prompts so they could measure where the brand actually showed up

2. Every lost prompt got diagnosed before anything was created

-> If Granola wasn’t showing up, they first asked why

-> Was the right page missing?

-> Did the page already exist but fail to answer the question properly?

-> Was there a crawl/indexing problem?

-> Or was AI getting the answer from third-party sources that barely mentioned Granola?

3 They separated “missing page” from “weak page”

-> This sounds obvious, but it changes the entire content strategy

-> If a relevant page already existed, they improved that page instead of publishing another article targeting the same question

-> New pages were only created when there was actually a gap

4. They stopped treating every technical SEO issue like it mattered equally

-> Crawlability, indexing and performance problems were prioritized based on whether they affected pages tied to real buyer demand

-> A technical issue on a page connected to an important buying question came before cleaning up random site-wide warnings

5. They studied the exact third-party sources AI was already trusting

-> For prompts Granola lost, they looked at the Reddit threads, review sites, articles, directories and other pages showing up instead

-> The question wasn’t just “how do we get more backlinks?”

-> It was “which sources are shaping this specific recommendation, and why are competitors represented there better than us?”

6. They used a simple framework for every important source:

-> OWN: pages Granola controls directly

-> CLAIM: profiles and listings they can take ownership of

-> EARN: editorial mentions, reviews and community discussions they have to deserve

-> CONTEST: outdated or incorrect information they need corrected

-> Different source = different action

7. Reddit became part of the search strategy

-> Instead of posting everywhere, they focused on communities much closer to the product and buyer

-> The same buyer questions being tracked in AI search were used to decide which conversations were actually worth joining

8 They measured Google and AI separately

-> A page could rank better on Google and still barely move in ChatGPT

-> AI mentions could rise while organic traffic stayed flat

-> So Granola tracked both systems independently instead of assuming SEO performance meant AEO performance

9. Then they reran the same questions and scaled what actually moved users

-> measure the gap

-> diagnose why it exists

-> make one specific change

-> rerun the prompts

-> track whether visibility and activated users moved

-> double down on what worked

the result:

-> monthly new active users from AI search increased 4,600%

-> non-branded AI citation rate increased 558%

-> non-branded mention rate increased 739%

-> organic sessions increased 134%

Granola didn’t win AI search by JUST publishing more blogs & content like every other company

instead they built a system for figuring out exactly what they were missing, choosing the right fix, and measuring whether it actually brought in users. then after that just iterating and doubling down on what worked

AI search is now one of their biggest acquisition channels.

and it should be one of yours too!

if you want us to run an audit for your company and make you rank #1 in AI search,

reply “audit”

## 60. Thread by @Alex_tra_memory

[1/6] NVIDIA Nemotron 3.5 Transcribe, ported to Apple's Core ML and Core AI on M5 Pro zero day launch.

Core ML (video): a 49.5-min, 3-person meeting → speaker-labeled transcript with word timestamps in 6.0s , 500 rtfx .

 - Speech encoder + CTC head on the GPU
 - Nemotron-3-Diarization on the Neural Engine, in parallel
 - ~1.5 GB, no LLM

Core AI: same encoder + CTC head as a 1.3 GB .aimodel. Core AI, Core ML and PyTorch give the same transcripts on LibriSpeech (4.62 / 4.64 / 4.64% WER, 400-utt subset). 234x vs 238x on GPU.

 Audio: AMI EN2002c (CC BY 4.0).

## 62. Thread by @RoundtableSpace

Before you launch anything, have Opus 5.5 send 5 subagents in as your first users.

Paste this into Claude Code:

"Act as my launch QA lead. Spin up 5 subagents, each a different first-time user:
1. The impatient one: skims, clicks the first button, quits at the first error
2. The skeptic: reads pricing, terms and privacy before signing up
3. The mobile user: does everything on a 375px screen on slow 3G
4. The non-native English speaker: only follows what the UI literally says
5. The breaker: pastes junk into forms, hits back mid-flow, double-clicks everything
Each one opens the app in a real browser, goes from landing page to first success, and screenshots every moment they get confused, stuck or annoyed.
Merge everything into one list ranked by how many users hit it. Fix it all in one pass, then rerun the 5 users to prove it's fixed."

Your first 100 real users won't tell you why they left. these 5 will.

(Works best with a browser MCP connected, Playwright or Chrome DevTools)
