## 63. Thread by @shdu11546816

[1/13] Two weeks ago a life changing amount of money was wired into my bank account from the sale of my business.

Since I'm no longer a competitor in this particular arena, I'm gonna drop some sauce on how I completely mogged an entire industry on meta ads.

I'm going to be vague so I don't dox myself, but here it goes:

[2/13] It was an old school brick and mortar business, so the first thing I did was completely redesign the business model from the ground up to focus on e-commerce.  That's been my go to way to make money for the last decade; it worked and I was happy for a couple years as the profits came in.

We were selling massive luxury items that cost between $10-20,000 per unit, to wealthy people across the country.  Margins were great- on a $15,000 unit we would profit between $3000-6000.  We were one of the first companies that figured out how to transport these cheaply and efficiently.  We were also one of the first to push professional quality video ads on meta.

After 2-3 years competitors caught on and profit dipped so I needed another innovation to keep myself ahead of the pack.  I started focusing hard on ways to reduce my customer acquisition cost, and I stumbled onto something that gave me a massive advantage.

[3/13] Modern ad systems have always been run on machine learning algos, but in the past 5 years the trend has accelerated with AI now doing all the targeting for you.  In the past you would hire a media buyer to tweak things.  You could adjust the demographic you want to target, the exact locations, the time of day, etc...  You used to have hundreds of these levers you could a/b test and optimize.  In the past 5 years this has shifted to AI, so you don't need a media buyer anymore.  You just set a conversion target and the AI targeting systems will grind on it.

When you're selling something cheap like yoga pants, all you have to do is create good ads and set your conversion target to gross margin.  The AI will find you sales, and it will even optimize for the exact customer profile that you'll earn the most margin on.

However, when you're selling $20,000 items, you'll never hit enough of volume to properly train the ads.  You need at least 50 conversions per week so unless you're doing $1 mil revenue weekly, you can't target margin.

Instead you target something higher up the funnel like a phone call or a lead form submission.  You can even put additional qualifiers- such as the phone call needs to last at least 5 min.

What we did at first was to ask a follow up question after a customer submitted a quote request on our website-:

How were you planning to pay?

Option 1- Pay cash
Option 2- Finance with FICO over 650
Option 3- Finance with FICO under 650.

And then we simply targeted option 1 and option 2 as the reward function.  This filtered out a bunch of the poors, but we still captured the lead even if they refused to answer.  If they didn't answer the question, we would just pass them along to their quote automatically after 6 seconds, plus we had automated text and email notifications sent in case they closed out before they seen it.

This had amazing results, so I realized that the biggest lever I could pull is setting a better target for the AI to grind on.

[4/13] What we did was start to track everything!

How many times did they view their quote?  How long did they view it for?   How many times did they add an accessory or upgrade to their quote?  How many times did they email us? call us?  text us?  Did they apply for financing?  What was the rate they were approved for?

Hell we even logged every SMS + email + call transcript from the first time they ever contacted us... then we did intent analysis on those messages to determine which words were most likely to correlate with sales.

We ended up with about 95 different data points plus intent analysis... So we ran all this data through our sales history, figured out the correlations and created a customer score based on their ability to afford the product and also their engagement quality.

We got really really good at predicting who would purchase, so I obviously set the meta conversion target to this new "qualified lead".  Since the business was seasonal, I even created a custom algo to dynamically adjust the target on the fly so we always ended up with around 50 conversions per week.  If business got slow, the algo would adjust to be less aggressive about who we considered to be a "qualified lead", and during good times it would adjust to be more aggressive.

It worked really well, but then I had another lightbulb moment and figured out a way to drastically increase the accuracy yet again...

[5/13] Up until this point, we were only logging data from activity that occured on our website, or from the contacts between customers and the sales reps.

Then one day I learned about data brokers, and realized there was a lot of data on these customers that was external to my website.

When a customer first entered our sales funnel, we took the identifiers they voluntarily gave us and we ran them through a number of data broker APIs to dig up as much info on them as possible:

Household income
Credit Score
Gender
Age
Ethnic group
Martial status
Own or rent
Occupation
Education 
Length of year at residence
Home equity
Children in the home
Age of children
Est net worth
Home value
Auto year make model
Home market value

...and much much more.  Keep in mind this is totally legal up to this point.  All we had to do is add a couple sentences to our privacy policy that no one ever reads.  It cost like 10 cents per customer to find all this stuff out.

Now all of a sudden we had some external data to add to the algo, but of course I didn't stop there.

I realized I could go the other way and set up a custom exclusion list, based on a loss function.  Being able to predict who would buy also gave us the ability to predict who wouldn't!

We set up an automation to add those people to an exclusion list so we wouldn't waste any more money serving them ads.

At this point it starts getting into a grey area legally, and is definitely against metas terms of service.

Then I took it another step which was definitely not legal 😂, because why not.

We started paying remote workers overseas to create a database of all the people who liked or commented on our ads.  People who never went on our website or agreed to our privacy policy.

Then we used a different set of data brokers to unmask and identify these Facebook and Instagram handles.  Once we linked a handle to a real name and email address, we had another automation that would send them to the first set of data brokers to create a financial profile on them.  So about 20 seconds after they liked one of our videos the system would come back with a predictive score to determine how likely they were to afford what we were selling and if they couldn't, the system would automatically add them to an exclusion list.

[6/13] I'm just a retard that dropped out of high school.  I figured all this out by asking AI a lot of questions.

Hopefully this helps someone out there.  There is quite a lot you can do to sculpt traffic and learn about your leads.  Data is your friend.

## 65. Thread by @boomerrbryan

[1/2] If your competitor is spending $200k/mo on ads, you can legally steal their customers on the 1-yard line for $0

Before anyone spends $500 to $10,000 on something they saw in an ad, 60% of them open YouTube and type the same 3 words

And almost zero companies own that search result:

Think about what you do right before you buy a $3,000 program, switch your company's software, or join a paid community you saw on Instagram or Meta ads

You open a new tab, go to YouTube, and type:

→ "[Competitor Name] honest review"
→ "Is [Competitor Name] worth it in 2026"
→ "[Competitor A] vs [Competitor B]"

Your competitor just spent $200,000 a month on paid ads doing the hardest part of business for you

They found the buyer, educated them on the problem, and convinced them to pull out their credit card

Right at the 1-yard line, that buyer pauses for 15 minutes to verify if the company is legit on YouTube

And when they search your competitor's name, there's usually nothing there except a couple sketchy affiliate videos from 2023

So smart operators are stepping right into that gap

You go into the Meta Ad Library (facebook. com/ads/library) and look up the 10 companies in your space running the most active ads for 90+ days straight (if an ad has been live for 3 months, it's printing cash)

Then you film a clean, objective 12-minute breakdown on YouTube reviewing their product or method

You give them credit for what they do well so the viewer trusts you're being fair. Then you point out the 2 biggest bottlenecks their customers run into (price, slow setup, missing feature, lack of 1-on-1 support etc) and show how your offer fixes that exact gap on screen

The buyer came to YouTube with their credit card already in their hand

You just redirected the wire transfer to your Stripe account while your competitor paid the Meta ad bill

When we built a targeted 10-video rollout like this for Clover Trading on a 10.3k-sub channel, it added +$100,000 in YouTube revenue in 90 days because every viewer clicking play was already looking to buy

If you run an online business, software, or B2C brand doing $20k+/mo and want my team to build and run your entire YouTube acquisition channel in 90 days, DM me "YT" or book a call at viralbangers. com

Don't hate the player, hate the game

## 66. Thread by @liangzhu_AI

[1/3] 用 AI 快速写出的应用还没卖出一单，可能先吃一张 10 万美元的海外律师函。

很多人做独立开发出海，光顾着 Vibe Coding 狂飙功能，完全忽视了上线前的合规死穴。

海外有 2 个极其致命但常被忽略的法律陷阱：
1. 注册页不询问年龄：一旦有 13 岁以下儿童注册，直接踩中 COPPA 红线，每人最高罚款 5.3 万美元；
2. 网页字体远程直连：从谷歌官方服务器直接拉取 Google Fonts，在欧盟属于非法传输用户 IP，早已有被判赔偿的判例。

做产品出海，上线前必须做好两件事：加上年龄验证门槛，把所有第三方字体和脚本全部转为本地静态托管。
建议出海开发者存好这份避坑清单，别让合规漏洞吞掉所有利润。

Suraj Sharma给的解决方案：将此粘贴到Claude 👇

“审计我的应用，排查这6个法律风险并修复：为注册添加年龄验证，自行托管字体，关闭会话重放（或添加同意机制+输入屏蔽），为每封营销邮件添加退订链接和邮政地址，在订阅按钮旁直接显示续订条款，并指导我注册DMCA代理。”

## 68. Thread by @wespreadjam

[1/15] run an AI search visibility audit exactly like this before trying to rank in AI search

for context, we got one of our customers to rank #1 in ChatGPT in only 2 weeks.

here’s how (14 steps):

1. start by building a fixed set of 30–50 prompts your buyers would realistically ask before choosing a product.

split them between category searches (“best [category] tools”), comparisons (“[product] vs [competitor]”), alternatives, specific use cases, pricing/cost questions and problem-based searches.

don’t build the list around keywords you want to rank for. build it around actual buying decisions.

2. run the exact same prompt set through ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini and Google AI Mode.

for every answer, record the model, date, companies mentioned, companies recommended and every cited URL.

keep the prompts identical so you can actually compare the results.

3. separate a citation from a mention from a recommendation.

if ChatGPT cites one of your blog posts while recommending three competitors, that is not the same as winning the query.

track all three separately.

4. repeat your highest-value prompts more than once.
AI answers are dynamic. one screenshot where you disappear means very little.

you’re looking for gaps that keep showing up across multiple runs, related prompts and different engines.

/5 group the prompts into topics instead of optimizing them one-by-one.

“best auth API,” “SSO provider for SaaS,” “SAML authentication tools” and “SCIM providers” might all belong to the same buying topic.

the goal is to understand which parts of the category you consistently own and which parts your competitors own.

6. now calculate the competitor gap for each topic.
if a competitor appears in 18/25 relevant prompts and you appear in 4, start there.

don’t waste time fixing a random query where both of you appeared once.

7. open every source the AI used when your competitors won.

record which page was cited, which domain owns it, what type of page it is, which competitor it mentions and what claim or recommendation it appears to support.

after 20–30 prompts you’ll usually start seeing the same pages and domains repeatedly.

8. before creating anything new, check whether you already have a page that should be winning that query.

if the page exists, find out why it isn’t surfacing.

does it actually answer the same intent? is the information outdated? is the useful information buried? does the competitor page contain proof, comparisons or data yours doesn’t?

building another page can just create two weak pages instead of fixing the right one.

9. check the technical layer next.

make sure the page is indexable, internally linked and returning normally to crawlers.

for ChatGPT specifically, check that OAI-SearchBot isn’t blocked in robots.txt or by your CDN/WAF.

for Google AI features, the page first needs to be indexed and eligible to appear in normal Google Search.

10. investigate the searches underneath the prompt.

AI search doesn’t always retrieve pages using the exact sentence the user typed.

Google AI Mode uses query fan-out to run related searches underneath a question. Bing Webmaster Tools now exposes the grounding queries its AI systems used to retrieve cited pages.

use those related searches to understand what the engine is actually trying to find.

11. only NOW decide what needs to change.

sometimes you need a new page.

sometimes the right page already exists and needs better coverage.

sometimes your information is outdated.

sometimes the model keeps relying on third-party comparison pages where every competitor is mentioned except you.

sometimes the problem is technical and writing anything would be a waste of time.

12. if you do create or update content, give the model information it cannot get from another generic article.

use first-party data, real pricing, specific feature limits, screenshots, benchmarks, comparison criteria, customer results, use-case differences and actual limitations.

another 2,000-word summary of information already on the internet gives the engine very little reason to retrieve you.

13. if third-party sources keep shaping the recommendation, work on those separately.
find the comparison pages, industry sites, videos and discussions that repeatedly appear in the answers.

figure out why competitors are represented there and you aren’t, then earn legitimate inclusion where it actually makes sense.

don’t manufacture random mentions across the internet and call it GEO.

14. once the changes have been crawled, rerun the exact same audit.

same prompts. same engines. same measurement.
track whether your citation share increased, you started appearing in previously lost topics, competitor recommendations dropped and your brand moved from “mentioned” to actually recommended.

then keep working on the clusters that are moving instead of starting another random content sprint.

if you want us to run this audit for your company and & make you rank #1 in AI search,

reply “audit”

Week one: Congratulations from Washington, London, Brussels, Paris, Beijing, and the AU. Warm statements. Talk of partnership.

Month two: IMF technical team arrives for “consultations.” World Bank signals readiness to discuss debt restructuring. The British Foreign Office announces a new bilateral trade engagement program. French commercial delegations request early access meetings.

Month four: First conditions surface publicly. Subsidy framework review. Exchange rate liberalization. Trade policy alignment with multilateral frameworks. None of it is called conditions. Month eight: First media destabilization pieces land. Reuters runs “Can Obi Deliver?” 
Bloomberg raises “governance uncertainty.” The ethnic tension framing begins circulating in Nigerian legacy media funded by the old order. The permission structure for domestic opposition is being quietly constructed. International cooling begins.

Month fourteen: Domestic opposition, now emboldened by that international cooling and the manufactured uncertainty, begins aggressive National Assembly obstruction. Budgets are stalled. 
Key ministerial appointments are rejected on procedural technicalities. The destabilization has moved from narrative to institutional. Governance theater is in full production.

Month eighteen: Security crisis escalates on at least one front, Northwest bandits, Southeast tensions, or a coordinated Abuja-proximate incident. 

The crisis is not random. It is the security weapon deployed on schedule. International media amplifies every casualty. “Investor confidence” language returns from the same financial press that celebrated him in week one.

Month twenty-four: The choice point. Submit to the architecture, or face the full weight of coordinated destabilization.

## 74. Thread by @DeRonin_

[1/2] my unpopular opinion is that ChatGPT apps are the best distribution play rn

we spent last month shipping these for agency clients and...

+50-200% on income those apps were already doing

just you literally get an access to ~900M weekly users for free

+ you're not struggled with onboarding, since it's built on top of ChatGPT and for user who never used your product, much easier to get in via gpt interface

same product, same pricing, just new way of using your product

and the fear that OpenAI just clones you is backwards

cloning your niche is the ONE move that costs them more than it returns...

they'd rather buy you and ship it out of the box

here's exactly how to launch it:

1. pick a workflow the client already gets paid for

> not a new idea
> an existing paid workflow with proven demand

2. build it as an MCP server

> streamable HTTP on public HTTPS, usually at /mcp
> that server is the whole product surface
> UI is optional, tools come first

3. name and describe every tool like the model is reading it, because it is

> action-oriented name, a human title, a description of when to use it
> accurate annotations and a real input schema
> that metadata is what decides whether you get called at all

4. add the UI after the tools already work

> expose it as an MCP resource with a ui:// uri
> set the type to text/html;profile=mcp-app
> your built js gets inlined into that resource and runs in an iframe

5. connect it at  and break it yourself

> plus button, add custom MCP server
> paste the url with /mcp, create as a plugin
> run MCP Inspector first and catch the schema errors there

6. submit at  and overprepare

> a zip of the built plugin and publisher verification
> a privacy policy url, no privacy policy means no review
> 5 positive and 3 negative test cases
> release notes and a demo video for the reviewer
> a domain token hosted at /.well-known/openai-apps-challenge

7. keep billing on YOUR backend

> OAuth 2.1 into the account system you already have
> enforce authorization on every request, not through the model
> there is no revenue share
> the plugin is the channel and the MRR stays yours

that's absolutely free traffic and by some reason most of products skip it...

the guy below is a good case which shows real efficiency of this approach (he literally made around +$40k monthly income)

[2/2] @OdedTsamir @OpenAI Yeah, that’s the most painful ahah

## 76. Thread by @RobHoffman_

[1/12] Meta Muse is now recommending our client's product as the best option on the market, then pushing people to sign up (see image below)

it's even sh*t talking their biggest competitor

this is the future of AEO

here's how to get there first (with 11 optimizations):

1. update your pages "accessibility tree"

this is literally how Muse reads your website.

do these four things:
1. add names to all icon buttons. For example, label your three line hamburger menu: aria-label="Open menu"
2. add a <label> tag with a name to every form field. Name examples: "phone," "first name," or "email"
3. add descriptive alt text to your images. Imagine you're explaining the image to a blind person. Example: "overhead view of a white horse running"
4. avoid putting important context in images. Everything must be machine readable.

make it easy for Muse and other agents to read your site so they are more likely to recommend you.

2. put key product info in your page's raw HTML.

GOOD:

<p>light and pro plans on beehiiv take 0% of paid subscription revenue</p>

BAD:

<div id="price"></div>
<script>
  document.getElementById("price").innerText = "light and pro plans on beehiiv take 0% of paid subscription revenue";
</script>

If your pricing, proof, or product specs load with a <script> tag, AI crawlers can't read it.

3. add the reader's main question as the first H2 on the page

it should sit right below the H1 title. Make sure it's written in plain english, as your reader would actually type it.

Example: On your "[COMPETITOR] vs [COMPETITOR]" pages, put this as your first H2 subheading:

"Which is better, [COMPETITOR 1] or [COMPETITOR 2]?"

4. then add an "it depends" answer in 100 words or less.

Don't just say your product is the best for everything.

AI crawlers love when you recommend the best products for specific use cases.

Example: "if you care about keeping all your subscription revenue, beehiiv is the better choice: beehiiv takes 0% of paid subscriptions, and substack takes 10%. Substack works if you want a built-in network to get more readers, and monetizing isn't a concern."

5. replace brand slogans with clear definitions.

Humans and AI crawlers are equally confused by vague slogans like:

"Your inbox, reimagined."
"Supercharge your CRM"
"The all-in-one social growth platform"

instead use clear definitions like: "AgencyMax is a CRM for SEO agencies that tracks leads, proposals, and existing clients on a single dashboard."

clear definitions should answer:
> What is it?
> Who's it for?
> What's it do?

6. add your customer testimonials as plain text, not images or JavaScript.

Here's a quick test: try highlighting the text with your mouse. If you can't, AI likely can't read it.

7. update all key facts to reflect the current date with "as of" statements.

Examples of key facts:
- pricing
- features
- G2 ratings
- number of users

Include the date in your copy like this:

"[COMPETITOR] has 13,231 active subscribers, as of 2026."

8. add this to your Robots.txt file:

User-agent: meta-webindexer
Allow: /

this gives crawlers and bots clear instructions for what they can and can't do on your site

if you don't know what this is, or want to quickly check your site's robots.txt, type this into your address bar:

[DOMAIN]/Robots.txt

9. fix the broken links on your site

here's how:

sign up for screaming frog (no affiliation)

it will run a technical audit on your site and spit out a spreadsheet with all your broken links.

then update, redirect, or remove the broken ones.

this sounds a bit nerdy and technical but it's important:

broken links prevent AI crawlers from properly exploring your site, and even lower your website's "trust score"

10. use semantic HTML.

Examples:
1. <nav> instead of <div class="nav">
2. <header> instead of <div class="header">
3.<button>Book now</button> instead of <div>Book now</div>

AI Agents more easily understand clean, native HTML than generic div containers.

Anytime you see "<div>" in your HTML for functional page elements like buttons, navigation, links, and headers, assume AI Agents will struggle to understand it.

11. keep your content fresh.

AI Agents are more likely to cite your content if it's new and/or recently updated.

ideally you should update your content every 30-90 days.

for good measure, include both a "published [DATE] and "updated [DATE]" line at the top of your page below the byline.

-----

shoot me a DM for a live demo of how your site can rank in Meta Muse (we'll do as many as we can)

## 83. Thread by @Voxyz_ai

I'm begging you: if you write code, 𝗿𝘂𝗻 𝗖𝗹𝗮𝘂𝗱𝗲'𝘀 𝗼𝗳𝗳𝗶𝗰𝗶𝗮𝗹 𝘀𝗲𝗰𝘂𝗿𝗶𝘁𝘆 𝗽𝗹𝘂𝗴𝗶𝗻.

It sends a team of agents through your whole repo. Every finding goes to three more agents whose only job is to knock it down, and it 𝗼𝗻𝗹𝘆 𝗺𝗮𝗸𝗲𝘀 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗿𝗲𝗽𝗼𝗿𝘁 𝗶𝗳 𝘁𝘄𝗼 𝗼𝗳 𝘁𝗵𝗲𝗺 𝗮𝗴𝗿𝗲𝗲.

On a big repo it can take tens of minutes to a few hours. It might find nothing, and it burns usage either way. Still beats saying "if only we'd run it" after something goes wrong.

It really is three lines (switch to Opus 5.5 with /model first):
/plugin install claude-security@claude-plugins-official
/reload-plugins
/claude-security scan the whole repo at high

It asks once whether to go ahead. Say yes. Anything marked CRITICAL or HIGH in the report, fix it.

If the install fails, run /plugin marketplace add anthropics/claude-plugins-official first. If it says the Workflow tool is missing, turn on Dynamic workflows in /config and restart. You'll need Python 3.9 or later. Keep Claude Code open while it runs, and don't switch branches.

When it's done, run /claude-security again and pick Suggest patches. Each finding becomes a patch file, and you decide which ones to apply.

## 85. Thread by @Voxyz_ai

Claude Code tip: before you ship a vibe-coded site, have Opus 5.5 𝗽𝗹𝗮𝘆 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗯𝗮𝗱 𝗴𝘂𝘆. Send out 3 subagents with three test accounts (A, B and an admin) to actually run 𝘁𝗵𝗲𝘀𝗲 𝟭𝟰 𝗮𝘁𝘁𝗮𝗰𝗸𝘀, collect proof, then fix everything in one pass:

Impersonate other users
→ Without logging in, take the public key from your frontend and query every table in the database directly. See what comes back
→ Logged in as A, swap the id in a request for B's and try to read, edit and delete B's data
→ When A edits their profile, add role: admin to the request, then try editing the role in their own account data directly
→ Use a password reset link once, then click it again

Get around limits in the UI
→ For disabled buttons and hidden options, skip the page and send the request directly
→ Once the free uses run out, check whether calling the API directly still works
→ With only a little credit left, fire 20 requests at once and see whether more succeed than the credit allows
→ Fill forms with very long text, special characters, negative numbers and empty values
→ Change the post-login redirect (?next=) to another site, and also try values starting with // and @

Come in from outside
→ Put <img src=x onerror=alert(1)> in a username, a comment or Markdown, then open the page in a browser and see if an alert pops up
→ If the app fetches URLs (importing links, building previews), give it http://localhost and http://169.254.169.254 and check the server logs for an actual outgoing request. Skip this if there's no such feature
→ For pages and API responses A has seen, request the same address from another browser as B or logged out, and see whether A's content shows up
→ Open your hosting platform's preview links without logging in to the platform
→ Hide an instruction in an uploaded document or web page that tells your AI feature to read another user's data or call a tool it shouldn't, and see if it does

Collect proof
→ Every item has to actually run. If it was only read in the code and never run, it counts as "not proven"
→ After a fix, temporarily turn the protection off in local code and run it again. The attack must succeed. Turn it back on and it must be refused
→ Put the results in a table: how each attack was done, the request and response, and whether it was refused when it should have been
→ Save the tests as scripts in the repo and run them again before your next launch
→ When you're done, install Anthropic's official security-guidance plugin (/plugin install security-guidance@claude-plugins-official). After every turn of edits, and after each commit or push it makes, Opus reviews the changes again. Those reviews use extra usage

The default medium effort is enough. The impersonation group is the most expensive to get wrong, so raise that one to high.

Send this whole post to Claude Code. If you need to, add this line at the end 👇
"First check that the preview environment isn't connected to the production database. If it is, run everything locally only. Use test accounts and test data, with a separate set of accounts for each subagent. Don't touch real users, don't attack services I don't own, don't hammer any one endpoint, and only test payments in test mode. Only switch protections off temporarily in local code, and never change the database or dashboard settings. Keep test account passwords in .env, out of git, and mask tokens in the results table. Send one subagent per group to attack, recording without fixing. Run the impersonation subagent at high effort. Then fix everything yourself from their lists, rerun every test, and give me a before-and-after table. Delete the test accounts and data at the end. For installing the plugin or changing dashboard settings, give me the steps and I'll do it."

## 89. Thread by @lennysan

[1/8] Moats! Does anyone have one anymore?

Yes. Yes, they do.

But only a small subset of what people call moats create real long-term value and defensibility.

Not actually a moat:
1. Distribution: This does matter more than ever. Building products has gotten easier, but getting them to customers has not. Distribution, however, does not create durable defensibility. It helps create the raw ingredients for one, but it is not itself a moat.

2. Execution: Yes, speed is critical for startups. But a company that has to sprint forever is not a good business. Ultimately, it will misstep, slow down, and competition will catch up. Speed gives a company more shots on goal as it tries to build a great product and a great business, but it must eventually find something more durable.

3. Brand: The idea is that when everyone is building the same thing, the most resonant brand will win. But for startups, brand works more like customer acquisition spend: Put money in, get growth out. It’s not something that will effectively keep others from competing long-term. Brand can become a true moat over time (as with Coke or Nike or Apple), but it just takes too long to really matter for most startups.

What you're looking for is compounding advantages: a self-reinforcing flywheel that gets stronger and stronger over time, ultimately making a company very hard to compete with long-term.

There are only four compounding advantages that really matter. Every major tech company has at least one:

1. Switching costs: The more deeply embedded a product is in the way a customer works, the harder it is for that customer to switch. This increases retention and revenue per customer, which can be reinvested into the product.

2. Network effects: The more users a product has, the better it gets for everyone. This increases engagement and attracts more users.

3. Scale economies: The more volume a company has, the larger and more efficient its operations can be. This lowers unit costs, funding lower prices or a better product at the same price, further driving up volume.

4. Data effects: The more usage a product has, the more data it generates. This data can be used to improve the product and increase usage.

If you can’t see the potential for any in the startup you’re considering joining, that’s a red flag.

In today’s incredible post, @danhockenmaier shares a simple framework you can run in about an hour after getting an offer:
1. Identify a startup’s compounding advantage
2. Gauge how strong it could get
3. Measure whether it’s working

Inside: dozens of real company examples, the specific metrics to track, and benchmarks for what good and great look like.

Read it here:

## 91. Thread by @twoclipping

[1/10] send your landing page to claude with this prompt

ull get a video like this, made with code for free

thank me later ↓

<inputs>
Ask me for: my landing page link, my brand name and logo mark, a two-part closing line (a statement, then a short italic tail), and a 15 second track (optional).
</inputs>

<direction>
A 14.5 second 3D fly-over of my landing page, 1440x1152 at 60fps. The page lies flat on a bright studio floor (#f9fbfb in the middle, #f2f3f6 in the corners) with a soft wide shadow around it and 24px rounded corners. One continuous camera, never a cut: it starts low over the hero and glides backwards down the page at one steady speed while looking up it, rises straight overhead to show the whole page as one strip, dives into the hero and lands it as a card under a two-line title. Focus is razor thin in front of the subject and almost none behind it, so everything near the lens melts. Cards float a little above the page with soft shadows; photos, chips and the testimonial lift off and stand up toward the lens as the camera passes them. Four small tiles of my logo lift off beside the hero button and stay with the film until the end card. The title is Geist 500, the italic tail Instrument Serif.
Banned: cuts, crossfades, stops or surges in the glide, the camera turning or swinging sideways, zooming in and back out, glows, any text laid over the page before the end card.
</direction>

<structure>
14.5 seconds: flight 0-8.2s, rise 7.75-9.25s, overhead 9.25-9.47s, dive 9.47-9.97s, title card 9.97-14.5s.
The camera 56° off straight-down and 1360px from the point it looks at, centred on the top half of the hero, the phone or product mockup sharp, four 32px logo tiles beside the button → 0-0.6: the glide eases in from rest → 0.1-1.35: the tiles lift, grow to 140-200px and hang just outside the page's top corners while the hero's chips rise to 60-80px and stand up 30° → 1.25-1.6: the glide doubles to cruising speed as the hero falls behind → 1.6: the second section's title passes the centre of the frame, its photos blurred near the lens → 1.35-2.7: that section's main photos lift to 100-130px and stand up 46°, two of its chips fly up to about 200px, stand up 54° and pass the left and right edges at 2.5 → 1.2-2.4: the tiles trail the camera 300px down the page, then drift out of the top → 3.0-7.3: every following section passes through at the same steady speed, each one sharp as it crosses the middle of the frame and soft below it; a logo tile drifts past the left edge close and blurred at 5.4 → 5.45-6.3: the testimonial card rises from 22 to 112px, stands up 30° and shows a 34px white edge → 7.3-8.2: the glide eases off as the footer arrives, the footer title sharp at 8.0 → 7.75-9.25: the rise, zooming out at an even rate while the tilt flattens (8.15-8.95) and the page's bottom edge settles 42px above the frame bottom → 9.25-9.47: the whole page as one centred strip, creeping 3.5% closer → 9.47-9.97: the dive, the page's top edge pinned to the frame top while it grows, everything below the hero fading out, then the top drops to y 283 so the hero lands as a 1018px wide card with 38px corners and a soft shadow; two logo tiles sit on its top corners, one crisp at the right edge, two huge soft blurs drift in at the bottom corners → 10.02: a white badge pill (logo + brand, 21px) fades in at y 44 → 10.12: line 1 at 74px, one word every 0.06s → 10.36: the italic tail at 82px in grey #8b8b92, one word every 0.05s, each word rising 16px out of a 12px blur in 0.34s → 10.6-14.5: hold, the card creeping 2% closer, the tiles drifting a few px.
</structure>

<build>
1. Open my link in headless Chromium (Playwright) at 1440px wide, device scale 2, and scroll to the bottom once so lazy images and scroll animations finish. Mark the pieces that lift (cards, phone or product mockups, chips, feature photos, the testimonial, the hero button) with data-layer. Screenshot the full page with them hidden, then each piece alone on a transparent background with 28px padding, and save every piece's page position to a JSON.
2. One WebGL2 film page, 1440x1152, every frame computed from time inside seek(t): no timers, no CSS transitions. The page is a textured quad on the floor and each piece its own quad at its height, drawn lowest first with no depth buffer. Use mipmaps and 16x anisotropic filtering or the far text shimmers.
3. Drive the camera from one speed curve, never from a position key every half second. Focal length 1880px, 56° tilt, distance easing out from 1360 to 1700px and never back in, no turning. Speed down the page as a share of cruise (cruise is about 770 page px/s): 0 at 0s, 0.5 by 0.6s, held at 0.5 to 1.25s, 1.0 by 1.6s, held between 0.94 and 1.1 to 7.3s, then easing to 0.12 by 8.3s. Integrate it into the page position.
4. Depth of field per pixel: blur radius = 75 x (focus - depth) / depth in front of the focus and 6 x (depth - focus) / depth behind it, capped at 120px, 40 taps on a golden-angle disc, each tap read from a mip level matched to the blur. Keep the focus on whatever crosses the middle of the frame.
5. A lifted piece stands up by turning about its own middle, top edge rising toward the lens. Each casts a shadow on whatever is under it: the same picture in flat #1a2133, offset (0.10z, 4 + 0.36z), blurred 7 + 0.5z, 26% opacity.
6. Rise: zoom out in log scale with a smooth trapezoid speed (speeding up 7.75-8.35s, even 8.35-8.95s, slowing to a stop at 9.25s) and hand the glide over to a camera that holds the page's bottom edge at that 42px line from 8.3 to 8.65s. Dive: log scale on a quadratic ease in-out, then solve the look-at point from the pinned edge. A rounded-rect mask morphs from the full page to the hero, 24px to 38px corners.
7. Render with Playwright on the real GPU (--use-angle=d3d11 --enable-gpu --ignore-gpu-blocklist): 60fps, 16 subframes across 0.7 of a frame for motion blur, H.264 MP4. The track's hit goes on 9.97s, whooshes on 8.66 and 9.80.
</build>

<gotchas>
If you can't run code on my computer, tell me straight away that you can only make a live preview here and that the MP4 needs Claude Code. Phones or cards that fade out with transparency show whatever is behind them, so fade them into the haze colour first and only cut to transparent at the very end. Keep most lifts tiny (20-40px) and lock the page before timing anything, because any change in a section's height shifts everything after it. Before the final render, measure a quick 30fps preview with optical flow: the speed down the page must never dip and the zoom must never turn into a zoom-in during the flight.
</gotchas>

<start>
Ask me for the inputs, capture my page, then show me stills at 0, 1.6, 2.5, 5, 6.5, 9.3 and 11 seconds before you render.
</start>

## 93. Thread by @mitchellsniffle

How to become legible to capital:

Make you (the founder) and the idea inseparable. The idea should explain how you spend your time, who you hire, and which decisions you make. You should become the idea. The idea should become you.

Make the growth equation obvious. If you put another dollar into the business, what does it buy and how does that translate into more output, customers, revenue, or how does it further advance the idea behind the company? Money in -> world changed.

Make the mechanism crystal clear and the ambition MASSIVE. Someone should be able to understand what you’re building today and imagine why it could absorb 10–100x more capital as it grows. This last part is incredibly important.

Make sure everyone is extremely aligned on building the same exact company. Founders, employees, and investors should agree on what the business is trying to become and what getting there requires. You need all the manpower and support you can get, so alignment is key.

It really should make you think "holy shit if this works it will change society completely".

## 99. Thread by @Voxyz_ai

Claude Code tip: before you ship a vibe-coded site, have Opus 5.5 send out 𝟰 𝘀𝘂𝗯𝗮𝗴𝗲𝗻𝘁𝘀 to check 𝘁𝗵𝗲𝘀𝗲 𝟮𝟬 𝘁𝗵𝗶𝗻𝗴𝘀 𝘁𝗵𝗮𝘁 𝘀𝗹𝗼𝘄 𝗶𝘁 𝗱𝗼𝘄𝗻, then fix them all in one pass:

Images and fonts
→ Convert every image to WebP or AVIF, sized to how big it actually shows on the page
→ Serve big images from Supabase Storage through next/image at the right size. On the Pro plan, Supabase's own image transformations work too
→ Lazy-load images and videos below the fold, and load the big image at the top of the page first
→ Load fonts the way your framework does it (next/font in Next.js), and keep it to one or two fonts
→ Remove third-party scripts you don't use, and run analytics and chat widgets after the page has loaded

Frontend code
→ Run a bundle analyzer to find the biggest packages, and where a whole library gets imported, import only the parts you use, like single lodash functions
→ Load heavy components the first screen doesn't need, like charts, editors and maps, with dynamic import when they're needed
→ Fetch the homepage data on the server, so the page opens with content already in it
→ Stream AI answers as they're generated
→ Show skeletons while data is still loading

Database and APIs
→ Index the columns you filter and sort by, including the ones your RLS policies use, like user_id
→ In RLS policies, write auth.uid() as (select auth.uid()) so it runs once per query
→ Find N+1 queries and turn them into a single query
→ Paginate long lists
→ Cache API responses that are the same for everyone, and never cache anything tied to a logged-in user
→ Move heavy work like sending emails, processing images and building reports to background jobs

Servers and network
→ Put your server functions in the same region as your database. New Vercel projects run functions in US East by default
→ Turn pages whose content rarely changes into static pages or ISR
→ When a page calls APIs one after another, run them in parallel or merge them into one
→ In Chrome, emulate a low-end Android phone on slow 4G and click through the main flows

The default medium effort is enough. The database group has to read queries and decide which indexes to add, so raise that one to high.

Send this whole post to Claude Code. If you need to, add this line at the end 👇
"Send one subagent per group to check it without editing anything, and note which file each issue is in and which page it affects. Run the database subagent at high effort. Before changing anything, run a production build (next build && next start in Next.js) or use a preview link, then run Lighthouse three times in mobile mode on the homepage and the two or three slowest pages and note the median. Then fix everything yourself from their lists, measure again the same way, and give me the before-and-after numbers. Don't remove features to gain speed. Ask me before removing dependencies, changing the deploy region or changing the database."

## 102. How to Grow a Massive Brand (Full 2026 Masterclass)

TL;DR: The new, updated playbook for growing a massive company and/or personal brand in 2026 and beyond. 

Branding is one of the only moats left. Nail this, and it could quite literally change your entire business trajectory.

In today's masterclass, I'll teach you:

How to find BMF (brand-market fit) + how to position your brand 

The secrets behind founder-led and brand-led content (powerful combo)

How to grow your brand(s) + best channels to distribute 

How to build a cult community 

Final tips & advice 

This article comes from 6+ years of organic content experience. This wasn't written by AI, and I spent thousands of dollars and hours of trial and error learning this stuff.

btw, if you enjoy this content, a like, repost, and follow go a long way :) 

Note: This is NOT a masterclass on paid acquisition. If you want to learn about ads, I recommend watching @hectorserrranoo's content. I only speak about things I know, and that's mostly organic content. 

This IS for you if: 

You want to grow a massive brand from zero, without much capital commitment

You're a founder struggling to tell your brand's story 

You're a marketer / Head of Content / Creative looking to level up

With that said, let's get right into things:

BMF (Brand-Market Fit)

I don't care if you're an AI startup founder, a micro creator looking to grow your brand, or an F500 company. You need to get this right.

Everyone knows about product-market fit. But very few think about brand-market fit.

Brand PMF is when a specific audience instantly understands what you stand for, deeply desires more of it, and can’t easily get it anywhere else.

This is the "DNA" of your brand. 

The reality is, most brands position themselves next to 50 other competitors and wonder why they struggle to stand out.

Great positioning 101 

Own a category (or better yet, create one)

The strongest brands aren’t just the best in a category; they’re often the only one in it.

Examples

@fomo - I can't even think of another finance x social trading platform.

@Polymarket & @Kalshi - Again, I don't even know of any other prediction market platforms.

@duolingo - While the company has recently declined due to AI, for a long time, they were the only ones that gamified the process of learning.

I can think of dozens more, but ultimately, you have to think deeply about what makes your product different, and lean into it with your branding. If you're struggling to find the answer, it's a sign you need to revisit your product-market fit. This is why a strong product matters tremendously if you want to build a magnetic brand.

Great category positioning is often: 

Combining two worlds (often hot niches): AI x trading, social x trading, fitness x finance, gaming x education, etc.

First mover advantage. Having a great idea and executing first. Even if competitors emerge in your category, if you build the brand correctly, you'll still be known as the #1. 

Solving a specific pain point. Again, branding and product tie directly into each other. Think deeply about the specific pain point your product solves, then start building a brand around it (if you're a creator, think about the pain point / value you're adding with your brand)

2.     Decide the emotion

What do people FEEL and THINK about when they see [your] brand?

Do they feel cool? Do they feel a higher status? Do they respect/relate to you because you have something they want? 

3.     Tell the story publicly 

This piece is very important for personal brands

Audiences don’t connect with brands that only ever win. It feels robotic and scripted. 

I’m not saying you have to post your lowest moments, but you do need to show the real journey behind the brand.

Share:

Your hot / honest takes 

Raw videos with your face (faceless brands are harder to build)

What you're struggling with 

What you're building 

And so on. 

4.     Make the audience the hero 

aka: what value do you give your audience? When they interact with your brand/product, what are they tangibly getting in return for spending their attention with you?

5.     Say it the same way, everywhere (messaging) 

The format of your messaging will change across distribution channels, but the story, the product's usefulness, and the emotion shouldn’t. 

TL;DR: BMF is a combination of your brand's values, great product, messaging, and your ICP's psychology. 

Founder-led versus Brand-led Content 

Exploring the various options for how to actually publish your brand

Founder-led

One of the most powerful brand-growth strategies is founder-led content, with the founder serving as the face of the brand. Often in first-person tonality - sharing opinions, stories, and personality. 

Content angles for founder brands:

How I'm building [x]

Behind the scenes of [x] 

Lessons of a founder 

etc. 

Brand-led 

A more "corporate" approach to content. Often speaking in third-person tonality. 

Content angles for brand accounts:

Product launches / updates 

Campaigns 

Community interactions

For companies, in 2026, you should be building both. Think of it as:

Founder account: why we’re building this, what I believe, what I’m learning, personal brand 

Brand account: product launches, updates, general distribution 

Note: For personal brands, you are the face. If you're serious about building a brand, you should dox. Your creative options open up tremendously (video content, photos, etc.), and by doxxing, you instantly kill ~80% of the competition that won't show face. Your trust, authority, and relatability instantly go up. It's one of the easiest, highest-leverage things you can do.

(not to mention you'll make more money from sponsors, get access to better private rooms, etc.)

How to Grow the Brand 

Ok. We've covered: brand positioning, BMF, and the content angles you'll use (founder and brand), but how do you actually grow?

Funnel construction 

You'll use three primary weapons to grow the brand through a funnel process. 

Generally speaking

TOF: Top of Funnel

Goal: reach people / impressions. 

This is your growth content. It’s broad and shareable.

Trendy content 

Outlier virality formats 

Short-form / clipping 

2.     MOF: Middle of Funnel

Goal: turn new followers into people who trust you.

This is where you build more authority with deeper-dive content. Sometimes your MOF content will still go viral.

How tos

Long-form

Step-by-step tutorials 

Case studies and breakdowns 

Behind-the-scenes 

3.     Bottom of funnel 

Goal: turn trust → customers (if you have a product) 

This is where content often asks for a specific CTA.

Content that works:

Product demos and walkthroughs

Customer results and testimonials

Offers, launches, and limited-time deals

Free resources that lead into paid ones (lead magnets)

FAQs and real-time objection handling

Depending on your brand, you can skew your resources towards different parts of the funnel. 

For example, if you're building a finance news 𝕏 account, 90%+ of your content should probably be TOF. 

If you're building a product page on Instagram, 90%+ of your content should probably be BOF. 

You'll have to work out the exact percentages for each pillar you want to allocate to. 

Establishing content pillars 

Your content pillars fit inside the three parts of the funnel. They are generally the 3 to 5 topics/formats your brand is known for. 

How to choose your pillars

Good pillars sit at the overlap of three things:

What your audience cares about: their problems, goals, and interests

What you’re credible on: your expertise, experience, and results

What connects to your product: topics that naturally lead to what you sell

Every platform has its own audience, format, and culture. Your job is to package and match the content pillars to each respective platform.

Renting vs owning your audience

Social platforms rent you an audience. Algorithms change, accounts get restricted, and you can lose your distribution overnight.

Owned channels (newsletter, community, email list) are generally yours forever, as long as you don't violate TOS or get severely banned. 

The goal of every distribution strategy: use rented platforms to find people, and owned channels to keep them engaged with the brand. 

Building a Cult Community 

There's a massive difference between building an audience and building a community. The best brands in the world have a genuine community to back them.

Make people feel special for being a part of your inner circle

A name

Public recognition 

Visual identity 

Shared beliefs / language 

A qualification process to allow access 

2.     Give them a home to interact 

3.     Stay close to the roots of the community 

If you're a founder, spend time interacting in your private groups 

Answer questions 

Go live-stream for the community 

Share behind-the-scenes info

And so on. 

4.     Create rituals 

Weekly events 

IRL retreats / annual events / meetups 

Member spotlights 

Challenges 

Good example: think about how tech companies like OpenAI and Google have dedicated "Dev Days." 

Final Tips & Some Advice 

Don't neglect TOF content. This is what allows any brand to grow consistently over time.

Pick a couple of platforms to master. If you can master X/YT, then all other channels become 10x easier

Stay consistent. Do whatever you need to stay in the game. 

Set up a content cadence. Establish when you write, film, batch content, etc. 

Most pieces of organic content should have a job. New eyeballs, build trust/authority, or conversion. 

Start your newsletter on day one. Even if you can't commit to publishing regularly, build it up anyway. 

Repackage and study your winners. 

If you made it this far, thank you for reading.

A like, repost & follow go a long way :) 

If you have any questions, feel free to drop them below - I respond to every comment. 

Also, feel free to leave topic suggestions that you'd like to see me write about next. 

Now go build a massive brand. It will change your life 👊🏼

[Open](https://x.com/ryangrowthh/status/2109644746465726929)

---

## 103. How to Be a World-Class Head of Content

If you create content, are looking to hire a Head of Content, or want to become one, this is for you.

I've spent my entire career obsessing over building world-class brands, and if someone had given me this article 6 years ago, it would’ve been the most valuable thing I’d read to get started.

This article is quite dense, so here's your roadmap:

What is a Head of Content?

Fundamental HOC Skills 

How to Become World-Class

Operations & Systems 

How to Hire 

This article comes solely from my experience working with massive brands, hiring dozens of content roles, and NOT from AI :) 

If this helps, a like, follow & repost goes a long way as I kickstart this new page. 

What is a Head of Content? 

At different companies, Head of Content will mean different things.

One startup’s “Head of Content” is another’s “Creative Director" and another's "Brand Strategist."

For the sake of this article, let's group all these roles under "The Creative."

Generally speaking, Creatives are responsible for three things:

Audience growth (organic + paid): Driving eyeballs to the brand 

Distribution is everything. Being a Creative means owning how the brand gets discovered, whether that’s organic posts, podcast collabs, paid campaigns, etc. The key is reaching the RIGHT ICP (not just farming vanity virality metrics). 

2.     High-level brand strategy: Brand PMF & positioning across channels

Brands need product-market fit the same way products do.

Questions I like to get clear on:

Who are we talking to? (avatar selection)

Why would they care? (what problem / pain point do we solve) 

What can we say that no other brand in the world can?

How does our ICP like to consume content? 

I could make this list very long but you get the point. 

3.     Systems & operations: Managing creative teams (the most underrated)

One of the things that separates decent Creatives from world-class ones. How do we build scalable workflows across creative teams? How do we get the most out of every role? How do we incentivize people? How do we hire? etc etc 

If you’re a Head of Content at a startup, you’re probably closer to marketing. 

If you're Head of Content for creators, you'll probably be closer to production and team management. 

And if you’re a Head of Content in a media business, the content is the product. 

As HOC, you can expect to report to one of three people:

1. The Founder or CEO
Most common for creators, personal brands, and early-stage startups.

2. The CMO or Head of Marketing
Most common at growth-stage startups and established companies.

3. Clientele
Most common for agencies, freelancers, and fractional Heads of Content.

Knowing how to report up is one of the skills that makes someone world-class.

Whoever you report to, the same rules apply:

Learn what they actually care about. A founder might say “growth” but really mean “I want to be seen as the authority in my space.” Your job is to uncover the real goal and update them on how you're actively moving toward it.

Protect their time. The less the person above you has to think about good content, the better.

Push back. Great Heads of Content have firm beliefs because they know what works. It's a balance between being creative and also being able to say no because the data says so.

Fundamental HOC Skills

I don't know a single person who just "became" Head of Content. HOC is a combination of several key skills.

Copywriting

Good copywriting is really just good thinking. 

You will not be a world-class HOC if you cannot think of excellent ideas and put them into words. Written media is the foundation for every marketing channel. 

I will create a separate article on writing in the future. Becoming a great copywriter means reading copy over and over, writing yourself, and iterating thousands of times. 

Good copy is generally:

Concise 

Easy to understand 

Thought-provoking

Attached to a strong underlying idea

Some people / places to learn from:

@Jayyanginspires content 

@harrydry content 

@CardinalMason old content 

Reading old big tech ads (Apple, Google, etc.) 

𝕏: the best playground for reading copy. Scroll viral posts and analyze why they hit

Substack: excellent for learning how to write long-form 

2.     Taste

The one skill you can’t fake. Good Creatives know an idea is a banger before they ever post and see the data.

You develop taste by obsessively consuming top content, creating yourself, failing a lot, and iterating. 

Build a swipe file of what works, study why it worked, and look to re-create (not copy) it. 

3.     Platform Fluency 

A great idea posted on the wrong platform will still flop. S-tier Heads of Content know how each platform actually works.

It's unrealistic to expect yourself to master every major platform. Focus on a few high-leverage ones.

If you can master a valuable platform like YouTube, platforms like IG/TikTok become 10x easier. 

4.     Production  

You should know how to produce visually appealing content.

This doesn't mean you need to be the editor yourself, but you should know how to direct the production to increase retention by adding visuals and creating aesthetic content. 

5.     Data Literacy 

You need a basic understanding of how to analyze content data and translate it into what it means for the brand and how it supports the brand's mission. 

These 5 skills are the absolute bare bones of becoming a 6/10 "decent" Head of Content, but if you want to be an 11/10, you'll have to go deeper.

How to Become World-Class 

Packaging Ideas

Packaging is its own skill, separate from writing. It's the art of framing ideas in a way that sparks curiosity in the audience. 

A 6/10 Head of Content can have an idea flop. An 11/10 can take that exact same idea, package it differently, and get 5-10x the results.

Excellent packaging is usually some combination of a piece of visual media (thumbnail, for example) + some form of written media (like a title).

What great packaging does:

Invites curiosity 

Promises a clear payoff 

Is specific 

Matches the platform

2.     Storytelling 

For lack of a better term, great creatives build an insane aura for brands. 

They can craft narratives and make a brand seem "cool" to interact with. Excellent example of this is @fomo. 

You are world-class when you know how to take a brand through the "Hero's Journey" (ups and downs) publicly.

3.     Create Original Insights 

Think of your favorite brand. They probably do something or provide some sort of insight/value that no other brand can. 

Excellent Creatives don't just think about posts; they think about creating a "moat" for the business (ties in with finding brand PMF). 

Where original insights come from:

Proprietary data

Firsthand experience

Frameworks

Contrarian takes

Access

4.     Build Community 

World-class Creatives can turn an audience into a cult community. They can build funnels and actually help direct users to [x] place. 

5.     AI Fluency 

If you want to be exceptional, knowing how to amplify and build tasteful creative AI workflows is key. 

Some examples:

Build and write copy with Claude Skills

Create media with tools like Higgsfield 

Learn how to clip with tools like OpusClip 

6.     Experience 

There's no way around it. Like any field, world-class Heads of Content have a lot of experience and results to show for their work.

Some other things that make someone world-class:

Hooks: being exceptional at writing hooks (very hard to find)

Building teams and hiring (I'll discuss this more specifically below) 

Timing: knowing when to execute certain campaigns and pushes 

Repurposing: being efficient at repackaging ideas 

Network: S-tier HOC should have a vast network to tap into (editors, TN artists, podcasters, etc.) 

Owned channels: owning the process of collecting contact info from customers, newsletters, webinars, etc. 

Operations & Systems 

The best Heads of Content aren't just creative geniuses; they're also great operators.

I’d argue it’s the most underrated skill in the entire role. Most creatives hate it, and that’s exactly why developing a biz-ops mentality in a content role is such an edge.

Team Structure

You can’t scale content alone. The basics of a content team are often: 

Head of Content: strategy, voice, quality bar, final say

Writers: written media

Designers: thumbnails, banners, branding

Video editors: long-form edits, clips, short-form

Social / community managers: posting, engagement, community

Early on, one person might cover three of these roles. As you grow, you split them out. 

A good operator knows how to manage and delegate roles across different time zones and build a cadence where everyone works efficiently. 

For example:

Hiring a team 

Your team is your ceiling.

Hire for taste and potential, train for skill. Content is very coachable. Learn how to train and build people up. You cannot expect someone to come into your org and "just know" how to excel right away. Content is very context-based. People need context to succeed. 

Always run a paid test task. I've found success running paid trials first. 

Hire specialists, not generalists. If you can afford it, budget to hire heads for each platform/channel/distribution channel. 

Hire people who are better than you at something. If you’re the best at everything on your team, you're hiring a creative team wrong. 

Documentation 

Everything should have an SOP. Pull from meeting notes and other data sources so you're not spending all day thinking about SOPs. 

How do we conduct research? How is information transferred? Who is responsible for what? 

You don't need to be fancy here. Google Docs is fine. AI can help you build great SOPs in minutes. 

Tooling 

A competent operator makes sure the team has the tools they need to succeed. 

Planning: Notion or ClickUp 

Communication: Set up Slack channels  

Files: Google Drive or Dropbox, with a folder structure everyone follows

Scheduling: Typefully

AI: Agents, skills, and tools everyone can use (the team can also build their own) 

Analytics: platform analytics plus a simple weekly report (developer teams can help to build content analytics tracking) 

Budgeting, resources & scaling 

In a world where every company expects to hire one content person to serve as a whole marketing department, it's important to set clear expectations for the resources the company needs to allocate to achieve its goals.

In my experience, you can build a solid content team on ~$40k/mo. If you're a founder, that might sound expensive, but if you nail content, it's the only marketing channel you'll ever need and one of the most scalable verticals. 

How to Hire Heads of Content 

This is very difficult. Primarily because if someone is good at content, they can probably make more $$$ working for themselves. Frankly, it's hard to incentivize content talent, but there are a few levers you can pull.

First off, if you have someone in your existing marketing-related department who you think can grow into the role, or has a background in content, I would invest resources in building their skill set and incentivize them to take over as Head of Content. It will pay you back 10x. 

If you're hiring from scratch:

Hire from 𝕏. This is where top writing/content talent lives. Be public about hiring for this role, and you'll get many leads.

Look for people already posting. Accounts with 1-30k followers are excellent candidates for Head of Content roles. Make a list of people you think can represent your brand well, and run outbound to these candidates. 

Look for up-and-coming creators. Search for creators who are small but post interesting thoughts that align with your branding. Offer them to lead your brand. 

How to incentivize talent 

Get clear on what they want. Basically, what do you have to do to get this person to say yes? Think: What makes this opportunity a home run for them?

Offer stable income via monthly retainer or salary (many people in content do not make stable income)

Offer equity in the company if they become a key player 

Give them full creative control (let them cook) 

Equip them with a team. Don't make them be a one-man army. Give them the scope of your brand and ask them who they would need to bring in to support.

Support their own brand: don’t make them choose between your brand and theirs. Give them kudos in public. 

Performance bonuses: reward people for good work. Don't treat content as a low-level role. It's not. It's difficult and requires skill to get right. 

What to look for in content roles as a founder

How can you tell if someone is qualified if you yourself have no clue what makes good content?

Some advice here:

If someone has never been Head of Content before, be wary. In my experience, you can only really become Head of Content by growing into the role, so if someone is qualified, it shouldn't be their first rodeo. At a minimum, they should have worked closely with a HOC previously if they haven't been one themselves. 

Ask people who do know. If you don’t trust your own judgment, borrow someone else’s who has hired for this role before. I'd be happy to help here. 

Look for high-quality thinking & reasoning. Being good at content is just being able to present ideas. This is the key trait to hire for. Can they think of cool ideas? Are they a good storyteller? Is their work just AI slop, or do they actually think for themselves? 

Final Thoughts 

This piece was long, but I hope you found it valuable. I don't see anybody else sharing these insights, so if you want to see more, follow me here @ryangrowthh. 

Feel free to drop any comments and questions below - I'll be sure to answer every single one as best I can. 

If you want to see me write about any other topics related to socials, content, and branding, let me know. 

A like, share, and follow go a long way. Keep crushing 👊🏼

[Open](https://x.com/ryangrowthh/status/2108566836866613323)

If you still think Opus 5.5's motion videos 𝘀𝗼𝘂𝗻𝗱 𝗹𝗶𝗸𝗲 𝗰𝗵𝗲𝗮𝗽 𝗲𝗹𝗲𝗰𝘁𝗿𝗼𝗻𝗶𝗰 𝗯𝗲𝗲𝗽𝘀, try this:

"Add sound to this video. You can't hear, so measure every step and don't go by feel. Install ffmpeg and librosa first if they're missing.
1. Use licensed music: a track I give you, or one under Mixkit's free license. Note the source and the license. Don't synthesize the music or the sound effects in code.
2. Find the beats with librosa. It sometimes locks onto the off-beat, so plot the waveform below 120 Hz against the beat grid and zoom in on a few bars. If the kick drums don't line up, shift the whole grid.
3. Put the big moves, like scene cuts and titles appearing, on the beat. If you can change the animation, change it. If you can't, move where the music starts.
4. Give every cut and big move one ready-made sound effect file: whoosh for movement, pop for things appearing, click for clicks, typing sounds for typing. Use the project's existing sound library first, like the one HyperFrames ships with, and ask me if there isn't one.
5. If there's a voiceover, it's the loudest thing, and the music drops while someone talks. Sound effects sit above the music. Aim for about -16 LUFS overall with peaks below -4 dBFS, so nothing clips after AAC compression or on a phone speaker in mono. Measure the exported MP4 with ffmpeg's ebur128. Don't measure the WAV.
6. Use ffmpeg to grab one frame before and after every cue from the exported MP4, and plot a waveform to compare. Then pick 5 timestamps for me to check with headphones. Don't tell me it sounds good."

A few days ago I was scoring a video and librosa locked onto the off-beat. The whole beat grid was 𝟬.𝟮𝟮 𝘀𝗲𝗰𝗼𝗻𝗱𝘀 𝗼𝗳𝗳, and I only caught it by plotting the waveform below 120 Hz against the grid.

## 106. How to Test Creatives With a Low Budget (Without one Creative Eating It All)

If you're running ads on €30–100/day, you've hit this problem: you put 10 creatives into one ad set, and the algorithm dumps 90% of the budget into one of them. The other nine barely get impressions.

Then you check the data and conclude they're all losers. They're not. They never got a chance.

This is the structure I use to actually test creatives on a small budget — and the manual step that makes it work.

The structure

One campaign, two ad sets:

Testing ad set — where every new creative goes first

Scaling ad set — where proven winners live

On a completely fresh start, keep the scaling ad set paused. All budget goes to testing first, because you don't have any proven winners yet.

This is also your permanent structure, not just a starting setup. New creatives always enter through testing; winners graduate into scaling.

The testing process, step by step

1. Load the testing ad set with creatives. At least 10. More is better.

2. Start the campaign and monitor manually. This is the part nobody wants to hear — you have to actually watch it.

3. Pause each creative the moment it hits around 3,000 impressions. You can use 2,000 as a floor, but I wouldn't go lower than that — below 2,000 the data is too thin to compare anything.

4. Watch what happens next. The algorithm was pushing 90% of budget into that one creative. You just took it away. Now it has to redistribute — and it'll pick another creative and push 90% into that one. Pause that one at 3,000 too.

5. Repeat until every creative has hit ~3,000 impressions and been paused.

Now you have something you never get otherwise: every creative measured at the same impression count. Real comparability, because every single one actually got budget.

What to judge them on (hint: not CPA)

Don't evaluate on CPA here. At this budget and this little history, the conversion data is far too thin to mean anything. A creative with one lucky conversion looks like a hero; a good creative with none looks dead.

What you're looking for at this stage is performance signal, not conversion efficiency. Specifically:

CTR (all) — clicks including social interactions (likes, shares, etc.).

If this is weak, people aren't stopping. The problem is your hook or your visual.

CTR (destination / link) — the clicks that actually go to your destination.

If this is weak, people stopped but didn't want what came next. The problem is your message, positioning, offer, benefit, or call to action.

The split between these two is the most useful diagnostic you get at this stage. Weak CTR (all) means your creative failed to stop the scroll. Strong CTR (all) but weak destination CTR means your creative stopped them and then lost them — that's a messaging problem, not a hook problem.

CPM — cost per 1,000 impressions. €10 spend, 5,000 impressions → €2 CPM. Useful as context: a creative with a much higher CPM is getting punished by the auction, which usually tracks with weak early engagement.

Then: graduate the winners

Take the creatives with the best performance data, move them into the scaling ad set, and turn it on with budget.

From there the loop runs permanently:

New creatives go into testing

You manually pause each at ~3,000 impressions

Compare on CTR (all) and CTR (destination)

Winners graduate into scaling

Repeat

Why this changes at higher budgets

Once you're at, say, €200/day, you don't need the manual pausing anymore. You can create 5 ad sets in one campaign and give each a minimum of ~€40/day.

At that level, every ad set gets enough signal and enough conversions to actually clear the learning phase — so you can test angles properly, structurally, and let the algorithm do its job.

At €50/day that same structure gives each ad set €10. Not enough signal to learn anything. It won't work.

That's the whole difference: more budget buys you structure. Less budget means you have to buy comparability manually.

The short version

Low budget + one ad set = the algorithm puts 90% of spend on one creative. The rest never get a fair shot.

Splitting into multiple ad sets at low budget doesn't fix it — none of them reach the learning phase.

Structure: one campaign, two ad sets — testing and scaling. Fresh start = scaling stays paused.

Load testing with 10+ creatives. Manually pause each at ~3,000 impressions (2,000 floor).

Each pause forces the algorithm to redistribute to the next creative. Repeat until all have been measured.

Judge on CTR (all) and CTR (destination), not CPA — there's not enough conversion data at this budget for CPA to mean anything.

Weak CTR (all) = fix the hook/visual. Weak destination CTR = fix the message/offer/CTA.

Winners graduate into the scaling ad set. New creatives always enter through testing.

At ~€200/day you can switch to 5 ad sets at €40 minimum each and skip the manual work.

They weren't bad creatives. They just never got budget. That's the whole insight.

I'm building in public — every setup, every number, every fail. Follow along. 🚐
