## 108. How to build a profitable digital product (in 7 steps)

“In my little group chat with my tech CEO friends, there’s this betting pool for the first year that there is a one-person billion-dollar company. Which would have been unimaginable without AI and now will happen.”

– Sam Altman

The future of entrepreneurship is micro-entrepreneurship.

One founder, with a laptop, an audience, an army of robots at their disposal, and a high-value skill stack to help them solve meaningful problems in society, making $100k-$1M/year.

No boss. No calls. No employees.

Ultimate freedom, fulfillment, and meaning.

“There are almost 7B people on this planet. Someday, I hope, there will be almost 7B companies.”

- Naval

It took humans centuries to get here, but we’re now living in the most leveraged time and the opportunities are infinite.

We now live in the most interesting time of all of history.

You no longer need to spend 4 years and go into hundreds of dollars in student debt to get a degree to learn a new skill.

AI leveled the playing field.

You have the world’s smartest, most talented, most hard-working employee.

Average people are now vibe coding multi-million dollar startups with no coding or programming experience.

With the combination of the internet, AI, and digital products, anyone can now build a profitable one-person business.

I’ve seen many people pull this off. Justin Welsh and Dan Koe are some of the most prominent creators who are making $3M-$5M per year as one-person businesses.

I am someone who has scaled my own one-person business to making 6 figures every year.

How is this possible now?

1) Real risk does not exist

Humans have done a tremendous job at eliminating any real physical threats.

You don’t go walking around scared if a tiger jumps at you.

We either subjugated, domesticated, or killed every other species on this planet.

The riskiest thing you can do is that you lose $500.

Or you leave your job to start a business and your business fails. But if it comes to that, you can always just get another job and you’ve solved your problem.

If you’re not going to die, then it’s not that risky.

2) Abundance of opportunity

I don’t want to make this entire newsletter about AI, but you need to understand that the world we live in is completely different.

We never had such an abundance of opportunities in our hands.

Every unfulfilled human desire we have is a problem that can be solved with a new business or product.

And the cool thing is human desires are infinite. Every person has their own unique quirks and interests which means the number of products we can build is infinite.

Every problem that exists in society is an opportunity to start a business.

Businesses solve society’s problems. It’s how you create, contribute, and distribute your value to the world.

3) Leverage

Humans are now the most leveraged they’ve ever been in human history.

One person can do the same work that took a team of 2 senior engineers, 1 product designer, 5 marketers, and 1 manager all by himself using nothing but a laptop, AI, and no-code tools.

You are now leveraged out of your mind.

You have AI that can code any app you want. You have no-code tools that help you automate your work. Digital products that have no marginal cost and instant delivery (which means high profit margins). You go to social media to attract an audience, get your work seen by others, and get customers for your business.

All of these 3 factors (and more) have helped us reach a point where you can build a profitable one-person business using nothing but a laptop, your unique set of interests and skills, and an audience of like-minded people.

Here’s how you can make your first digital product (as a beginner):

I - How to create a simple (yet hyper-profitable) digital product

“Smart people get paid to be themselves”

- Alan Watts

There is a big stigma when it comes to courses.

When most people hear the word “course” they immediately think of a business guru who’s trying to scam you.

But that’s not my experience with them.

I bought courses that genuinely changed my life.

Spending $150 on a course to learn a high-value skill is much more affordable than a normal university course.

Most people can’t see their value because they’re still operating with society’s programming.

In universities, you have professors who have no real-world experience, teaching you theory, and you are tested on your memory.

But with courses, it’s different.

It’s taught by creators who have skin in the game. You learn practical skills (fast), you build real-world projects, launch them, and let free markets decide.

As a result, you gain high-value skills, real-world experience, and make a living while doing so.

The only problem with courses?

Courses are static.

You’re left to figure out how to implement the steps to your unique situation yourself. You can’t speak to the creator directly and ask for feedback.

But with AI, this is now different.

As a creator, you can now have AI-guided courses to help your customers implement your lessons.

The AI is trained on your knowledge, has your frameworks, and your voice. This increases the value of your product and helps people extract more value from your product.

Here’s how you can create your own digital product and make your first $10,000 (I made $150,000 in the past 1.5 years using this process):

1) Sell your sawdust

You have a $100,000 digital product stuck in your head.

Or in your hard drive.

Or in your workspace.

The easiest product you can sell is the one you’re already using (but aren’t aware of).

The creator economy is simply a system’s economy.

Everyone has their own unique systems and processes to achieve a certain outcome.

If you’re a music producer, you have your own drum kit.

If you’re an editor, you have your own set of light presets that you use.

If you’re an email marketer, you have your own unique way of writing an email.

If you’re a writer, you have your own way of finding ideas and introducing novel perspectives.

If you’re an athlete, you have your own pre-game routine that helps you get in the zone.

If you’re into finance, you might have your own way of budgeting, investing, and keeping track of all your expenses.

All of these are examples of products that you can now create and sell.

The cool part?

They already exist.

For me at least, because I write so many actionable newsletters, run cohorts, and create lessons for my clients, I already have them exist in my Eden Workspace.

This is an example of some lessons about branding that I created when delivering my cohort.

Then all you need to do is click on that board and turn it into a product that has AI built into it to help your customers implement your lessons.

And if you’ve got other lessons in other boards, you can easily add them to your product.

2) Create your Dream Customer

To sell your product, you need to know who you’ll sell it to.

Spoiler: Your product isn’t for everyone.

If you’re selling to everyone, you’re selling to no one.

You need to be comfortable with the fact that not everyone will buy your product.

You need a Customer Avatar.

Even when you try to “sell to your previous self”, as many people tell you to do, you still need a Dream Customer Avatar.

Because there are multiple different versions of you.

I’ll use myself as an example here.

4 years ago I was a broke college student.

3 years ago I struggled to build an audience.

2 years ago I was a ghostwriter making $4,000/month, but I hated it.

Last year, I had an offer, but sales were inconsistent.

Each of those versions of myself had a different problem, a different skill set, and a different level of what kind of products they were able to buy.

3) Write your promise

Specificity sells.

I see far too many creators try to hide behind vague promises because they’re too scared to deliver on their promise.

But the truth is, if your promise is vague, fewer people will buy it.

At first, you’d think it’s counterintuitive, but it’s not.

Think about this for a second.

Let’s say you’re trying to plan a trip and you have 2 options.

Option 1) A $1,000 mystery trip.

It could be the best trip of your life, or it could be the worst trip where you go to an underdeveloped country, a 1-star hotel, with the worst airlines possible, the food is horrible, and there are no hot showers.

Every bad outcome you can think of is there.

There is nothing good about it.

Or...

Option 2) a $10,000 trip.

You’re going to your favorite country. The best airlines possible. A 5-star hotel. The best restaurants. Transportation is handled.

You don’t need to think while you’re on that trip because everything has been taken care of for you before you even step out of the door and you know exactly what’s happening at every hour.

Which ticket do you think will sell more?

The second one.

The reason is simple. It’s specific and it reduces uncertainty.

That’s what your promise should do.

It gives people certainty of what they’ll buy.

Don’t write “Grow an audience”. It’s too vague.

A better promise is something like this: “Grow by 1,000 followers per month using the MAGNET framework”.

I’m awful when it comes to coming up with names for frameworks. But you get the idea.

Here’s a formula you can use:

The Promise Formula:

“Helping [WHO] go from [HELL] to [HEAVEN] so they can [BENEFIT(s)]”

This is an example of a promise from a cohort I bought last year:

Example:

“Turn your knowledge into a quality product to unlock more freedom & impact in your business”

4) Create your curriculum

Your curriculum is the steps your customers are going to take to achieve the transformation.

The problem with courses is that they’re static.

If the person who bought gets stuck on a concept, or tries to apply what is being taught, they can’t get feedback.

It’s not like you can ask the creator and they’ll respond.

But with Eden, you can add your knowledge to it and have an AI version of yourself teaching people the course.

This does 2 things:

It increases the perceived value of your products. Because now you have an AI component to it.

It helps your buyers to apply what is being taught properly. So they get better results. Which means better testimonials for you. Which means you can get more buyers (because your product works).

So how do you create your curriculum?

Go back to your promise and ask yourself:

What are the 3-5 major milestones your customer needs to reach to achieve the transformation? These will be your course modules.

For each major milestone, what are the 3-5 action steps they need to take to achieve it? These will be your module lessons.

Here’s an example of myself:

Important note:

There is a fine line between overdelivering and overwhelming your customers.

You overdeliver when you deliver on your promise and nothing more.

You overwhelm when you do too much.

5) Become your own case study

Contrary to popular belief:

You can skip the free client work if you can get yourself great results.

When I started ghostwriting, I didn’t have any prior experience.

I didn’t have a wall of testimonials of people going viral.

I just had my posts. But because I was able to make myself go viral, I can promise people that I’ll write them similar posts.

This is harder?

Not really.

Because the idea of doing free client work is to show competence. That you can actually deliver on the promise.

So if you can deliver great results for yourself consistently, then people will pay to learn from you.

Be so good at what you do that people can't help but pay to learn from you.

6) Write your sales page

Writing a sales page is relatively easy once you have done the previous steps.

And no, you don’t need to be a professional copywriter to write a good sales page.

You just need the right structure and AI by your side.

I’m still learning about copywriting. Everything I learned when it comes to sales copy, I learned directly from writers who made $10M-$50M writing copy.

Now, there are many different structures you can use, but here’s how I structure my pages and offers:

In Eden, it’s very simple to create a sales page.

Most of this is already taken care of for you and you just need to write and fill in the different sections.

1) The Promise

The promise is the transformation that your customers will get after buying your product.

The most common place you’ll see it is in the headline and subheadline. They both condense the value of your product and the transformation.

I’d go so far as to say that your headline and subheadline are the two most important pieces of your sales copy.

This is what I had for my newsletter course:

Good headlines sell the outcome. Not the product itself.

In the subheadline, that’s where you add the other parts.

The timeframe

The unique mechanism

Numbers & social proof

Since I didn’t have a name for the method I use to write my newsletter, I just didn’t mention the unique mechanism.

2) The Problem

Traditional copywriters call this section “The Lead”.

I won’t act like I’m a copywriter, so I’ll just take their word for it.

But a problem I see most people make when it comes to this section is they state the problem, then they write “Keep reading if you’re...” then throw a ton of bullet points hoping that something will stick.

Don’t do this.

It just screams that you don’t know who your Customer Avatar is and you’re hoping that someone buys your product.

Be specific. Call out your Customer Avatar’s problem and make them a promise.

That’s all what you need to do.

Don’t overcomplicate it.

3) The Process

Here, your goal is to show people why your way of doing things is different from everyone else and why your way of doing things is better.

You can lead with your story and show people how you came to your solution.

Let’s say you’re selling a LUT pack for the average Instagram user who just wants to upload high-quality images to their feed.

You can write about how you wanted to upload high-quality images to your Instagram feed but every course or tutorial you watched just made the process more complicated.

Then you learned how to use editing apps (like Photoshop and Adobe Lightroom), but you hated how you always had to start from scratch.

So you created your LUT pack presets to help you quickly edit photos and make them look cinematic without going through the hassle of learning editing apps and using nothing but your iPhone.

If you can add an illustration to breakdown the process is even better.

4) Features + Benefits

This is very straightforward.

You will tell people what’s inside your product.

The easiest way to do this is to write bullet points for each module.

To write effective bullet points, say what they’ll learn + the benefit.

It helps to think of it as the opposite of pain points.

5) Proof

This depends on where you are.

If you have social proof, use it. If not, you can use yourself as a case study.

That’s what I did with my last cohort. It was the first time that I launched a product that was focused on Substack

But because I used myself as a case study, I sold all 20 spots.

If you don’t have proof, go generate some.

Either for yourself or for others.

6) Price

Pretty straightforward.

Mention the price of your product.

7) Guarantee & Refunds

If you’ve got a guarantee, write it.

A guarantee helps make your offer easy to buy. It’s extremely rare to have someone come to you asking for a refund.

I have 200+ customers and made $100,000+ in revenue, and during that time I only had 2 people asking for a refund.

A guarantee just shows you’re confident about your product.

8) Call To Action

At this point you’ve walked people through the entire sales page.

The only thing you need to do is write a quick recap.

Restate the promise

Show proof

Mention the price

Cost of not taking action (hell)

Cost of taking action (heaven??)

Lastly, you can have a price card where you again mention everything that’s included with the price.

Price card looks something like this:

Again, with Eden, this is pretty simple. You don’t need to code. You just write what you want to be included and it’ll do the work for you.

9) FAQ

Most people skim your sales page and immediately go to your FAQ to get the gist of it.

Some questions to answer:

What is the product? → Sum up the offer one more time

Who is this for? → Describe your Customer Avatar

When can they expect results? → Set expectations

Objection 1 → Answer the objection

Objection 2 → Answer the objection

Objection 3 → Answer the objection

What if they don’t get results? → Refund policy

Then finish off with one more buy button.

7) Send traffic

If you’re not making the monthly revenue you want, then you need more traffic.

The biggest mistake I see creators make is that they create a product and think that people will magically find their products.

Spoiler: They won’t.

You need to deliberately send traffic to your product.

More traffic = more sales.

So how do you increase traffic?

2 ways:

1) Increasing the frequency of your promotions

The cool thing about this is you don’t need to change anything.

You don’t need to change your offer, your content strategy, your funnel, or anything.

All you’re going to do is increase the frequency of your promotions.

You need to always remind people that you’re selling something.

Drill this in your head:Sales don’t come out of thin air

You need to be intentional with how you direct people.

You need to be clear on what products you have, what they need to do, how they can buy them, and when to buy them.

The chances of someone clicking on the link in your bio and buying from is astronomically small.

2) Creating more content

Creating more content gives you more in-between moments where you can promote your products.

This sounds obvious, but the more content you create, the more opportunities you’ll have for CTAs.

If you only post 7x a week, a weekly promotion post becomes 14% of your content.

People will get sick if you promote a product every time you post.

But if you’re posting 90x a month (which is 3 posts a day), and 4 of your posts are promotional posts, that’s only 4% of your monthly content as promotions.

More content = more opportunity to sell (without being annoying)

You can still provide value, create content you love, and build an audience, but you also get to make more sales because you simply have more CTAs in your content.

Promote your products like you’d promote a sponsorship.

That’s all for today.

Thank you for reading.

Enjoy the rest of your day.

Hussain

P.S.

The tool I was showing you is called Eden.

It’s the software I’ve been using for all of my writing, content creation, digital products, and coaching.

If you’re serious about starting and building a one-person business, you need to get Eden.

You can get started today for free.

[1/3] do yourself a favour:

> get claude max and feed it everything about your product.
> make it research who needs the product, what they use today and why they'd switch.
> give it the 50 gtm strategies from the tweet below.
have it rank all 50 and pick the 3 you can test right away.
> turn those 3 into campaigns: content, outreach, offers, landing pages.
> give each one a budget, a test window and a user number it has to hit.
> put grokbots on the repetitive work, all reporting to one dashboard.
> feed claude the weekly numbers so it can cut the losers and push the winners.
> repeat until 2 or 3 channels work reliably.
> automate those as far as you can.

but what do you feed claude for it to get your product and users right?

[cont.d in replies 👇]

[2/3] what do you feed claude for it to get your product and users right?

> product url, docs, pricing, competitors and whatever customer feedback you already have.
> ask for who actually needs this, what they're using now, why they'd move and where they hang out online.
> pick a single audience from that research.
> decide what an activated user looks like now, otherwise nothing you measure later means much.

but how does it pick the winning 3 out of 50 strategies?

> rank all 50 on audience fit, acquisition potential, cost and speed.
> make it justify every ranking with competitor research and real customer conversations: reviews, reddit threads, support tickets.
> shortlist the top 10.
> from those, pick the 3 you can start this week with the budget and people you have.

what does a campaign need before it runs?

> the exact platforms, communities and accounts it's going after (down to the handle)
> the content, outreach messages, offers and distribution plan, written out (AI assisted)
> landing pages, forms and tracking links, built in claude code.
> a budget, a test window and a user acquisition number it has to hit.

what exactly does the claude and grokbots do?

> give each of the bots one job inside the 3 campaigns.
> hook them up to the tools they need to research prospects, watch conversations, spot distribution openings and prep content.
> let them run research, content prep and reporting on their own.
> anything public-facing gets a human read before it goes out.
> every bot logs what it did and what came of it to one shared dashboard.

but how do you figure out what's working?

[cont.d 👇]

[3/3] how do you figure out what's working?

> feed claude weekly numbers: visitors, signups, activated users, acquisition cost and retention.
> have it separate the campaigns bringing in users from the ones only bringing impressions.
> once the evidence is in, kill the weak ones, improve the winners and pull the next few strategies off your list.
> feed complaints, objections and churn reasons back in so it flags what to fix in the product and the messaging.
> keep going until 2 or 3 channels work reliably.

obviously this ain't as straightforward as I'd like it to be, but once you get this general idea, you can probably tweak it to your product and situation.

if you want ChatGPT and Google's AI answers to start mentioning you, here's what to do:

^(\S+\s){6,}\S+$
it shows every search that's 7+ words long, the closest thing in your data to how people ask AI

4. click it, open the Pages tab, and find the page Google is showing for it

## 115. Thread by @suraj_sharma14

[1/19] Your vibe-coded app can get sued for $100K before it makes a single sale.

6 traps hiding in most AI-built apps:

Signup never asks for age + COPPA: up to $53K per child under 13

Google Fonts loaded from Google’s servers → a Munich court made a site pay €100 to ONE visitor for leaking their IP (GDPR)

Session replay on by default → recording keystrokes can count as wiretapping in California (CIPA): $5K per session

“We launched” email with no unsubscribe link or postal address → CAN-SPAM: up to $53K per email

Subscription checkout without renewal terms next to the button → in California, renewals can count as a gift you have to refund

No registered DMCA agent (it costs $6) → you lose safe harbor for user uploads: up to $150K per stolen image

The word is PER. Per visitor. Per session. Per email.
That’s how zero sales turns into a hundred grand.
The fix: paste this into Claude 👇

“Audit my app for these 6 legal risks and fix them: add an age gate to signup, self-host my fonts, turn off session replay (or add consent + input masking), add an unsubscribe link and postal address to every marketing email, show renewal terms right next to the subscribe button and walk me through registering a DMCA agent.”

Save this before you launch.
Following me is the cheapest co-founder you’ll ever hire.

Not legal advice. Talk to a lawyer about your specific situation.

## 116. Thread by @jakezward

[1/18] I’m begging you:

- Pick ONE Google AI Overview
- Track how it changes over 7 days
- Find the patterns with AI
- Reverse engineer the citations
- Build your page around the findings

I’ve used this process to rank in 1,000s of AI Overviews.

Here’s how it works: 

[2/18] 1. Pick one search you actually want to win

Ideally a buying-intent query that already triggers an AI Overview. 

[3/18] 2. Track how Google answers it

Collect the AI Overview over 7+ days (more the better).

You can do this in 2 ways:

FREE

Search it manually every day and save:

- The full answer
- Citations
- Date

[4/18] PAID

Add the prompt to a tool like  and automatically track the answers + citations over time. 

[5/18] 3. Find what keeps showing up

Put all the answers into ChatGPT or Claude and look for:

- Recurring claims
- Repeated entities
- Common formats
- Frequent sources
- Important differences 

[6/18] Then count how often each one appears.

You’re basically building a picture of what Google consistently includes for that search. 

[7/18] 4. Find what Google is rewarding

Now study the pages that get cited most frequently.

Look at what they cover, how they answer the question and what they do differently. 

[8/18] You want to understand both what they have in common AND what’s missing.

Pay attention to:

- How quickly they answer
- Topics and entities they cover
- How they structure the information
- Data or evidence they include
- Tables, lists or comparisons
- Gaps between the different sources

[9/18] 5. Build something better

Use everything you found to create or improve your page.

- Cover the important topics
- Include the important entities
- Use the right format
- Answer questions immediately
- Make the information incredibly clear

[10/18] But don’t just copy what’s already winning, use it as the baseline and then make yours better.

Give Google something NEW to cite:

- Original data
- First-hand experience
- Better examples
- New statistics
- Better comparisons
- Useful tables
- Questions nobody else answered

[11/18] 6. Publish and track

Monitor the same search over time.

- Does your page start appearing?
- Which sections seem to perform?
- What sources keep winning?
- What changes in the AI Overview?

Then use what you learn to keep improving the page.

[12/18] That’s basically it.

No magic AEO/GEO prompt.

Just track what Google is doing, find the patterns, then use them to build a better result.

1. Follow me @jakezward for more

## 119. Thread by @ericosiu

[1/3] He ranked number one on Google in 38 minutes.

Cody Schneider did it live during our webinar. The result: a LinkedIn article first organically for “Facebook ads for kitchen remodeling.” He said it went live 38 minutes earlier.

The workflow:

• Research the pages ranking for one target query.
• Give AI that source material.
• For a brand, add a 30-minute founder interview transcript with its positioning and point of view.
• Write and publish the LinkedIn article.
• Submit the URL for indexing, then check Google.

The founder-context step is the one I’d keep. Give the model something specific from the business, then review the draft.

Keep his warning, too: do not use this indexing approach on your core website.

One article, one query, one live test. This does not promise the same result for another keyword or domain.

00:00 The live challenge and result
00:23 Research the target query

## 121. Thread by @coach_bowman

[1/16] I've coached over 30 Olympic gold medals.

I've worked with some of the greatest athletes in history.

Here are the lessons I wish more people understood:🧵 

[2/16] 1. Talent is real. It is also the thing most likely to ruin you.

Every great athlete I have coached had unusual ability.

My job was to make sure they didn't rely on it.

[3/16] 2. The details matter because they repeat.

Léon Marchand takes seven kicks off every wall. Every set, every day.

I don't let it slide, even in practice when it doesn't matter.

By the time the race is close, nobody has time to think. It has to be automatic.

[4/16] 3. You have to practice for the day things go wrong.

In Beijing, Michael's goggles filled with water in the 200 fly. He couldn't see the wall or the lane lines.

He counted his strokes, because that's what we did in practice.

[5/16] 4. A big goal is only useful if it turns into a schedule.

Write down the eight gold medals if you want. Fine.

Then you still have to swim the 500 free that's right in front of your nose this morning.

How do you eat an elephant. One bite at a time.

[6/16] 5. Motivation comes and goes. That's true for everyone.

Some of the most driven people on earth wake up and don't feel like it.

The answer isn't complicated. Do your work.

[7/16] 6. People become what the room expects of them.

If the room lets a standard slide, it slides.

If the room corrects the small thing every single day, people stop believing small things are small.

My goal is to build an environment where success is inevitable.

[8/16] 7. If nobody fails in practice, the practice was written wrong.

Don't be afraid to let them fail.

That's where they learn everything they need to learn about how to get better.

[9/16] 8. Winning is harder to survive than losing.

The hardest people I've dealt with are the ones who actually got the thing they chased.

There's something genuinely destabilizing about it.

You celebrate it. Then you decide what's next. Otherwise it decides for you.

[10/16] 9. Confidence is built on the calendar, not in the head.

I plan the season so a swimmer has already proven it to themselves before the big meet.

You can't talk someone into feeling ready. You show them what they've already done.

[11/16] 10. There is no one magic way to do this.

People are always bringing me the new method.

It's like spinach. It's good for you. I'm still not going to eat it every day.

[12/16] 10. There is no one magic way to do this.

People are always bringing me the new method.

It's like spinach. It's good for you. I'm still not going to eat it every day.

[13/16] Set the goal. Build the environment. Do the work. Fix the details. Let them fail while it's cheap. Keep going.

It isn't complicated. It's just hard to do for long period of time

[14/16] @jameswagz Thank you!

[15/16] People always ask me what the great ones have in common. 

I wrote it down: the 10 characteristics of a champion. It's free when you join my waitlist:

[4/14] ③SNSのアイキャッチ用

「縦長の告知ポスターで。タイトル『〇〇』と日時『〇月〇日』を大きく太く。文字は一字一句この通りに入れて、それ以外の文字は入れないで」

→ 入れたい文字をカギカッコで
くくるのがコツ。

→ どのプロンプトでも、

作り込んだ感じになる。

## 123. Thread by @boomerrbryan

[1/2] I'm BEGGING you to look up what YouTube pays per 1,000 views in different niches before you pick what to make videos about

This is the single most important number in the entire YouTube business model and almost nobody checks it before starting

Entertainment: $2-4
Gaming: $3-5
Vlogs: $4-6
Reactions: $3-5

Now look at the niches nobody wants:

Medicare supplements: $44
Estate planning: $38
Commercial insurance: $35
Business tax strategy: $32
HVAC repair: $28
Assisted living: $26
Septic and well water: $24
Farm equipment: $22

That's not a typo. $44 per thousand views vs $3 per thousand views. The exact same platform. The exact same algorithm. The exact same upload button

A gaming channel needs 1,000,000 views to make $3,000

A Medicare channel needs 68,000 views to make $3,000

And the Medicare channel has maybe 12 competitors in the entire United States while the gaming channel has 45,000

But this is where it gets stupid:

The $44 CPM is just the ad money. The viewers in these niches are about to spend $5,000-$200,000 on the thing you're explaining to them. The ad revenue is a tip. The real money is what happens after they watch

A roofer with 3,000 subscribers who answers "how much does a new roof cost" gets calls from homeowners about to spend $14,000. Every video is a free sales rep that works at 3am

A financial advisor with 5,000 subscribers who answers "should I do a Roth conversion" gets appointments from retirees managing $400,000+ portfolios. One client is worth $5,000-$15,000 a year in advisory fees

An HVAC company with 2,000 subscribers who answers "heat pump vs furnace" gets calls from homeowners about to spend $8,000-$15,000. Those viewers aren't subscribing to an HVAC channel. They're panicking because their furnace died in January and they need someone who knows what they're talking about

The leads close at 2-3x the rate of ad leads because the buyer watched you explain their exact problem for 15 minutes before they called. The trust was built before the conversation started

How to find these niches yourself in 10 minutes:

1 → Open Google Keyword Planner (free with a Google Ads account you never have to fund). Type any service. Sort by "Top of page bid - high range." That column shows what advertisers pay per click. $50-200 per click = that niche pays $20-44 CPM on YouTube. The correlation is almost perfect

2 → Search that topic on YouTube. Count how many serious channels answer it. If there are fewer than 15, you have almost no competition for an audience advertisers are paying $50-200 a click to reach on Google

3 → Check the comments on the few channels that exist. Every question asked is a video idea nobody made yet. Every complaint is a positioning opportunity

The uglier the niche sounds the emptier it is and the higher it pays. This has been true for 8 years straight and nobody talks about it because making videos about septic systems and Medicare won't get you likes in a group chat

I build channels in these niches. The ones that sound like pamphlets in a doctor's waiting room. Because that's where the checks clear

(link in bio)

If you sell something and don't have a channel doing the work for you yet →

## 125. Thread by @suraj_sharma14

[1/17] How I made $10,000 on X in a few months (X payouts + collabs + sponsorships).

No flex. Just a breakdown.
No buying followers. No 30 posts/day. I became useful enough that brands reached out and payouts mattered.

What worked:

1.) Picked one lane
Builders / AI / opportunities. A niche of “everything” yields nothing.

2.) Wrote for bookmarks not likes
Checklists, maps, teardowns. Content people save and share.

3.) Stayed consistent during quiet periods
Most revenue arrived after months of low engagement.

4.) Made brands an easy yes
Clear audience, proof and deliverables. One-page media kit > long pitch.

5.) Treated collabs like products
Brief → draft → revision → post → screenshot → invoice. Professionalism ensures repeat business.

6.) Diversified income
Payouts are nice sponsorships pay the rent. Together they compound.

Rough split:
• X monetization
• Brand collaborations
• Small sponsorships / affiliate deals

Advice for starters:
• Post 3–5 useful things weekly
• Spend 30 minutes replying after posting
• Build public trust before asking for money
• Reject trash brands; your audience is the asset
• Track conversions (saves, clicks, DMs)

$10K won’t change your life. The skill of turning attention into cash will.

## 127. Thread by @MarioJoos

[1/12] 🚨 MAJOR ANNOUNCEMENT: YouTube will start prioritizing original content and reducing re-uploads (RIP clipping)

YouTube has just announced one of the most impactful changes to its short-form algorithm. Its recommendation systems (the algorithm) will start to further prioritize original content and reduce the reach of content re-uploaded from other creators without adding anything of your own.

They highlight this shift toward originality, where channels with primarily re-uploaded content will see less distribution within the Shorts feed.

It also seems like they’re really trying to put the focus on content with real value, while highlighting that YouTube doesn’t want as many “minor technical edits” or “template-based” changes. Which is basically all that clipping was in this industry.

Overall, while this will piss off some people whose entire careers were built on the premise of other people’s content (mostly clippers), the change itself is amazing and will finally address the overall quality issue we’ve been seeing.

## 128. Thread by @karpathy

We'll be spending a lot more time trying to understand the outputs of language models. A few thoughts, tips & tricks:

Writing. Something I've had success with: Ask your LLM to explain something in ASD-STE100, it's a controlled language specification originally developed for aerospace maintenance documentation. LLMs well-versed in this language and it comes with heavy constraints on clean writing style that I often find a lot more readable. Sometimes I've tried to soften it a bit e.g. ask for "80% of the way to ASD-STE100" because the spec is quite stringent. But even better:

Diagrams / images. Instead of writing, ask your LLM to create a diagram. These can be a lot easier to process, parse, and understand. But even better:

Web pages. Ask for output "in HTML" to get a beautiful, interactive webpage. LLMs are getting really good at frontend and can create beautiful experiences, animations, etc. But even better:

Explainer videos. The output format I am most bullish on is fully custom / bespoke explainer videos generated on any arbitrary topic. Experiment with things like "Create a 3b1b style video explainer on X. Use my ElevenLabs API key for audio narration". (you'd need an API key for the latter or you can ask your LLM to find you decent free alternatives that use your local compute). This is actually starting to work!

In summary:
- As LLMs get better, they will do more and more of the legwork autonomously, and a lot more of our work will rise up the abstractions into oversight and understanding.
- Luckily, LLMs can help here too because as intelligence and code are increasingly abundant, you can ask for large, custom, discardable software artifacts (e.g. web apps, video explainers) that would have never made sense to create before. Push the boundaries here and you'll be surprised.

## 134. Thread by @hackSultan

[1/3] If you’re vibecoding anything, paste the prompt below In your prompt box and let your agent do a security sweep.  

[
You are a senior security engineer and red-team specialist tasked with performing a comprehensive, adversarial security audit of the following codebase, system design, or application.

Your goal is to identify all possible security vulnerabilities, including common, uncommon, and novel attack vectors. Assume the system will be deployed in a hostile environment with motivated attackers.

---

AUDIT SCOPE

Analyze the system across all layers, including:

- Frontend (UI, client logic, browser storage)
- Backend (APIs, business logic, services)
- Authentication and authorization flows
- Database interactions and storage
- Infrastructure and deployment assumptions
- Third-party integrations and dependencies

---
CORE OBJECTIVES

1. Identify critical, high, medium, and low severity vulnerabilities
2. Detect logic flaws, not just known patterns
3. Surface chained attack paths (multi-step exploits)
4. Highlight unknown or unconventional weaknesses
5. Assume attacker creativity beyond standard checklists

---
THREAT MODELING

- Define possible attacker profiles (anonymous user, authenticated user, insider, API consumer)
- Identify entry points and trust boundaries
- Map out sensitive assets (data, tokens, permissions, secrets)

---

VULNERABILITY ANALYSIS

Check for (but do NOT limit yourself to):

### Authentication & Authorization
- Broken auth, weak session management
- Privilege escalation (vertical and horizontal)
- Insecure password reset flows
- Token leakage or reuse

### Input Handling
- Injection attacks (SQL, NoSQL, OS command, template injection)
- XSS (stored, reflected, DOM-based)
- CSRF vulnerabilities
- File upload exploits

### Data Security
- Sensitive data exposure
- Weak encryption or misuse of cryptography
- Hardcoded secrets or keys
- Insecure storage (localStorage, cookies, logs)

### API & Backend Logic
- Broken object-level authorization (IDOR/BOLA)
- Mass assignment vulnerabilities
- Rate limiting issues / brute force risks
- Business logic abuse (race conditions, double spending, bypassing checks)

### Infrastructure & Configuration
- Misconfigured headers (CORS, CSP, HSTS)
- Open ports, debug endpoints, admin panels
- Environment variable leaks
- Cloud/storage misconfigurations

### Dependencies & Supply Chain
- Vulnerable packages
- Unsafe imports or execution
- Malicious dependency risks

---
ADVANCED / UNKNOWN THREATS

Actively attempt to discover:

- Non-obvious logic flaws unique to this system
- Feature abuse scenarios
- State desynchronization issues
- Cache poisoning
- Replay attacks
- Timing attacks
- Multi-step exploit chains combining low-severity issues
- Any behavior that “shouldn’t be possible” but is

---
ADVERSARIAL TESTING MINDSET

- Think like an attacker trying to break assumptions
- Attempt to bypass validations and safeguards
- Manipulate edge cases and unexpected inputs
- Explore how different components interact under stress

--
OUTPUT FORMAT

Provide findings in this structure:

### 1. Vulnerability Summary
- Total issues by severity

### 2. Detailed Findings
For each vulnerability:
- Title
- Severity (Critical / High / Medium / Low)
- Affected component
- Description
- Exploitation scenario (step-by-step)
- Impact
- Recommended fix

### 3. Attack Chains
- Show how multiple minor issues could be combined into a major exploit

### 4. Secure Design Recommendations
- Architectural improvements
- Safer patterns and best practices

---
IMPORTANT INSTRUCTIONS

- Do NOT assume the code is safe
- Do NOT skip analysis due to missing context, infer risks where needed
- Be exhaustive and paranoid in your review
- If unsure, flag it as a potential risk and explain why

]

## 138. Thread by @jochenmadler

[1/8] We trained a model to predict AI-written blog posts from structural features - and discovered the shape of slop.

On posts it had never seen, it told AI and human apart with 98% accuracy, getting only 19 of 1,740 wrong.

Paper and code:  

[2/8] /2 Researchers at the University of Maryland and Google DeepMind showed this for fiction (StoryScope). We replicated it on company blogs: collected 2,000+ pre-ChatGPT posts from 250+ B2B websites, then had ChatGPT, Claude, Gemini, DeepSeek and Kimi each write their own version.

[3/8] /3 Then we had a model answer 214 questions about how every post is built (how hard it pushes its own product, whether it backs up its claims with sources, whether it quotes a named expert), and trained a classifier on the answers.

[4/8] /4 Why does this work so well? Because apparently, all five AI models write in a similar shape. Map every post by these features and AI posts cluster together, while human posts sit apart and are much more diverse. Of the 1% most unusual posts, 149 are human and only 4 are AI.

[5/8] /5 What's more, each model has its own "accent". A second classifier picks the right author (one of the five models, or a human) 79% of the time. Random guessing is 1 in 6 = 17%.

[6/8] /6 The cool part about looking at structure is that just rewording doesn't work. We had each model rewrite its own posts until 73% of the original phrasing was gone, and the classifier still worked just as well.

[7/8] /7 Thank you @[Jenna Russell] and co-authors for StoryScope, which laid the groundwork, and for your feedback.

Paper and code: ,

## 144. Thread by @shannholmberg

how to use Opus 5.5 + GPT 6 to make animated marketing videos

Opus 5.5 handles the main work, from planning the scenes to screen recording, animation and editing

when I need an image, it can call Codex to generate the asset and use it in the video

for the video below, I asked Opus to use Codex to generate the display

here’s how to set this up:

1. start with your idea and let Opus ask questions

write out what you want or transcribe a voice note, then ask Opus to question you before it starts

give it the campaign goal, the audience and what you want someone to do after watching

include where you’ll publish it, the video length and the format you need

you don’t need every detail ready, but you need to make the decisions together

2. give it your brand context and references

share videos you like and explain what you like about them

that could be the camera movement, the animation, the pacing or how they introduce the product

add the assets you already have, including logos, fonts, screenshots and previous work

if your brand book is in Paper or Figma, connect the tool and give Opus access to the relevant files

it can inspect your designs and use the existing assets without you collecting everything into another folder

your taste and feedback give it a direction to follow

3. ask for a storyboard before it builds

the storyboard should show what happens in each scene and how the scenes connect

ask it to include:

> what the viewer sees in each scene
> the text that appears on screen
> which parts need a screen recording or a generated image
> how the elements move and how long each scene lasts
> whether the video needs music, sound effects or a voiceover

review this together before it starts building the video

if the opening takes too long or the product demonstration is unclear, change it here

4. generate the missing assets

in my setup, Opus can call GPT-6 through Codex to request images from the image-generation tool

you can also generate the assets separately with GPT Image 2 or 2.5, then give the files to Opus

describe how the asset will appear in the scene, including its angle, background and available space for text

Opus can then use the image in the animation and video edit

for the display in this video, I gave Opus the instruction and it handled the request through Codex

5. build the video and review a first render

ask Opus to build from the approved storyboard and render a preview

rendering creates the video file, and it can take time depending on the animation, resolution and length

local rendering can also use a lot of your computer’s resources, so several renders can slow down your other work

review a lower-resolution preview before requesting the final export

6. give specific feedback and revise

use timestamps so Opus knows exactly what to change

> at 00:03, hold the product screen longer so the text is readable
> at 00:08, reduce the movement around the display
> make the final call to action larger on mobile

be specific about audio too, including whether you want music at all

if you want generated music, give it a suitable audio tool and references for the direction

keep the storyboard, assets and feedback in the project so each revision uses the same context

## 148. Thread by @twoclipping

[1/16] opus 5.5 is f*cking cracked at motion design

this entire video is code, 0 after effects

im open sourcing the prompt template for these motion designs

steal it to recreate these ↓

<inputs>
Ask me for: 8 to 12 UI states I want the shape to become (e.g. button, loader, player, slider, toggle, tabs, chart, command palette, toast), pure black and white or one accent color, and a royalty-free song around 120 BPM (e.g. Mixkit, free for commercial use).
</inputs>

<direction>
Dribbble-level UI motion. One shape, never cut: every state is the same element morphing its size, radius and color while its content swaps with a short blur. A cursor drives every change with real clicks and drags. Light warm-gray canvas, black and white components, one clean UI font (Geist). Springs everywhere, a tiny overshoot at most. The camera zooms so each state fills the frame. The last frame is the first frame, so it loops.
Banned: bouncy easing, particle bursts, glows, gradients on UI chrome, mismatched icon strokes, dead time, anything that looks like a template.
</direction>

<structure>
120 BPM, 7 bars, something happens on every beat.
Button → loader → check → dynamic island → music player with a play/pause morph → scrub the progress bar → it becomes a volume slider that stretches when dragged past max → a toggle flips on the beat → the knob becomes a liquid tab indicator → the tabs open into a chart that draws itself, with a tooltip on hover → it collapses into ⌘K → type to filter → enter → toast → back to the button.
</structure>

<build>
1. One HTML file, square 1440x1440. Every style is computed from time inside seek(t): no CSS transitions, no timers, no state carried between frames.
2. Springs are closed-form step responses. A value that changes target many times is the sum of one spring per change, so it stays a pure function of time.
3. The tab indicator's two edges ride different springs, so the leading edge stretches ahead of the trailing one. Same trick for the toggle knob.
4. Drags are direct manipulation: while the cursor is held, the value is computed from its position. On release it springs back from wherever it was.
5. Analyze the song with numpy for the beat grid and start on a downbeat. Place every UI sound by its measured peak.
6. Render with Playwright: 4 subframes per frame, blended with ffmpeg tmix for motion blur at 60fps.
7. Render one frame per beat before the full render. Fix anything off the grid, cramped or hard to read.
</build>

<gotchas>
Never put will-change on anything the camera scales or the text renders blurry. Text that swaps inside a morphing container needs its own enter and exit timing or it overlaps. Make the last frame identical to the first, cursor position and speed included, or the loop stutters.
</gotchas>

<start>
Ask me for the inputs, then show me the state list on the beat grid before you write any code.
</start>

[1/6] I think buying backlinks is a bad, expensive idea.

Bad an expensive because, you know, you can get many of them for free.

For example, the classic outreach to swap backlinks: that is a great use of your time, but it's still a lot of menial work.

You have to dedicate time to:

> finding the contacts
> back and forth emails
> negotiating rates
> etc.

It's true that the whole thing is faster with AI, but it stills needs you there constantly.

A best way of doing this, is to let the backlinks come to you.

I achieve this with the Citation-Hub Led strategy.

It goes like this:

You create a content asset aggregating statistics of your industry.

You use AI to do the research (head terms keywords, angle, industry, clusters, etc.), and create the copy of the article according to your angle.

Other companies, journalists and AI agents will star linking to you naturally.

I know because I've seen links coming from forbes, ramp, tripadvisor and other big players with this strategy.

All of this without paying a cent!

The whole strategy is simple: build your own statistics page, aggregating other pages' stats, and back on those backlinks.

(better yet if you create your own dataset=

Anyway, I'm running a few experiments and many clients. The results speak for themselves (see screenshots).

[1/9] this is the quickest way to get leads from SEO/AEO:

(from someone who does AI Search for 7 - 9 figure tech companies like beehiiv, and am even generating 800k of pipeline per quarter for our smallest client)

follow these steps exactly:

1. open Google Search Console

2. go to "performance" (upper-left hand side of screen)

3. click the "average position" box on the top-middle of the screen (it will turn orange)

4. select "28 days" to just see the last month of rankings

5.  hit the "Export" on the upper right side of the screen and select "Google Sheets"

6. go to the "Queries" tab of your Google Sheet, freeze row 1, then right click the "Position" column and "sort sheet A to Z"

7. give the Sheet to Claude and input this prompt:

"Find all the bottom of funnel queries in the Queries tab of this sheet that are in positions 4 - 20. Specifically look for these types of queries:

[Competitor] Alternatives
[Competitor] vs [Competitor]
[Competitor] Review
Best [product] for X
How to export data from [Competitor]
[Software] for [role]
[Software] with an MCP connection
Home page
Services/Product pages

give me them here in a simple list"

8. choose the top keywords you'd want to rank higher for from that list. Remember: you will likely get zero leads unless you're in positions 1 -5 (and ideally top 3). Then input this prompt:

"I'd like to improve my rankings for these pages:

- [PAGE 1]
- [PAGE 2]
- [PAGE 3]
- Etc.

Tell me which of these are going to be the easiest to improve My rankings on by analyzing who are the competitors currently above me in the SERP, the strength of their pages, and how easily I can update my page to have more ranking factors.

Use this AI Search checklist as a guide for what the perfectly optimized page would look like in an ideal world: *DOWNLOAD LINK IN THE FIRST COMMENT*

9. now follow up with this prompt:

"give me the checklist items I would need to execute for the '[KEYWORD]' page in order to most likely increase my rankings, ordered by priority"

10. go implement as many of those changes as possible.

11. check Google Search Console every day to see how your rankings have improved for that specific page.

I hope this helps you make more money. Have fun!

## 154. Thread by @om_patel5

i think i’ve cracked seo/geo/aeo for startups

and im about to do the same thing for apps

got 5 sales today purely from organic search

the crazy part is im now automating basically the ENTIRE pipeline through Muse Agents from my phone

heres how im doing it:

1\ find what people actually want

mine competitor reviews, reddit, quora, forums, app store reviews and google

pull the exact questions, complaints and wording real people are already using

group repeated problems by frequency + buying intent + relevance to your product

dont ask ai to "give me 50 seo topics"

thats how you create slop

2\ turn those problems into search opportunities

research related searches, people also ask, competing pages and what angles are missing

figure out whether the query needs a blog, landing page, comparison page, tutorial or something else

one repeated complaint can turn into an entire content cluster

for example:

"x doesnt integrate with y"

can become:

how to connect x with y
best x alternatives for y
x vs [your product] for y
why x isnt syncing with y
how [your product] solves y

3\ build clusters instead of random blogs

one main page around the high intent topic

then supporting pages answering every smaller question around it

the agent maps the internal links before anything gets published so every page actually supports the rest of the cluster

4\ write for humans first

answer the question immediately

use actual examples, data, screenshots, comparisons and product-specific information

dont make someone read 500 words before getting the answer they searched for

the goal isnt to trick google or an llm

its to become the best source for that exact question

5\ automate the publishing loop with muse

from my phone i can kick off research, briefs, drafts, internal linking and updates

muse keeps the workflow moving in the background while im doing literally anything else

my mac handles the repo/publishing side through the tooling i already have set up

instead of manually sitting there creating pages all day, most of my job becomes reviewing what gets shipped

6\ use search console as the feedback loop

this is probably the most important part

connect google search console through the api/mcp

have the agent constantly look for:

high impressions + low ctr
queries sitting near page 1
pages losing traffic
new queries you never intentionally targeted
pages ranking for the wrong search intent

then feed all of that data straight back into the next iteration

7\ update before blindly creating more

if a page already has 10k impressions but barely any clicks, making another random blog makes no sense

fix the title

fix the intent

add the sections people are actually searching for

strengthen the internal links

improve the answer

republish it

the agent should spend just as much time improving pages that are close to winning as it does making new ones

8\ do the same thing for geo/aeo

take your buying intent queries and test how chatgpt, claude, gemini and perplexity answer them

see which companies keep getting mentioned

see which sources keep getting cited

figure out what those sources have that yours doesnt

then improve your content until your page is the clearest and most useful source for that query

dont try to "hack" the llm

give it a reason to use you as a source

9\ turn one topic into multiple assets

one good piece of customer research can become:

> blog
> landing page
> youtube video
> short form
> x post
> linkedin post
> comparison page
> faq/support page

you dont need 8 completely different ideas

you need one good idea distributed properly

and then the whole thing loops

research → cluster → write → publish → measure → update → expand

all orchestrated from my phone while muse keeps working in the background

seo is literally consistency + iteration

most people publish 10 ai blogs, get no traffic after a week and quit

meanwhile every page you ship gives you more search console data

that data makes the next page better

then the next one gets better

then you update the old ones

and eventually the whole thing starts compounding

thats the part people miss

seo feels useless for a few days and then suddenly you're getting hundreds of clicks from pages you forgot you even published

all you have to do is stay consistent and keep pumping

## 155. Thread by @RobHoffman_

[1/15] founders and marketers: you need an optimized "about us" page on your site

i work with billion dollar tech companies and bootstrapped SaaS. The About Us is one of the first pages we optimize

here's how (8 points):

(P.S. I explain why this is important at the bottom of the post)

here's how:

1. start with a clear value prop sentence: "[COMPANY] is a [CATEGORY] that [DOES XYZ] for [ICP]."

2. add your core services under a "what [COMPANY] does" H2 subheading. Put each service/feature/product in an H3 subheading. Write 2-3 sentences about what you do, and what outcome it produces.

3. next, add a "what makes [COMPANY] different" H2 subheading. List about 5 core differentiators in H3 subheadings. Give specific, quantifiable differentiators and call out competitors by name. Example: [COMPANY] offers month-to-month payment terms, whereas options like [COMPETITOR] have mandated 12-month contracts. Again: 2-3 sentences per differentiator.

4. your next H2 should be "who uses [COMPANY]." Add bullet points that call out your exact ICP segments. Example: "B2B SaaS marketing teams at 50 to 200-employees."

5. now add a "the team behind [COMPANY]" H2 subheading. Include the names of the founders, quick backstories, company origin story, team composition, and links to social pages (especially the founder(s) socials). You only need about 2 sentences per backstory/origin story.

6. add a "how [COMPANY] works" section. Include info like communication channels, response times, who on the team customers will work with, turnaround time, and onboarding process.

7. now for the most important section for AI Search: add a "key facts" H2. This should be an HTML <table> or <dl> (definition list) so it's crawlable. Include the following rows:

Company Name
Type
Founded
Founder
Headquarters
Website
Core Offering
Pricing
Contract Terms
Services
Communication
Notable Clients
Customers Served
Projects Delivered
Competitors
Social

 8. finally, add your FAQ section. Put "frequently asked questions" in an H2 subheading with your questions as H3s. Only 2-3 sentences per answer.

Here's why this page is so important:
1. it helps train AI to talk about your brand accurately
2. it strengthens the E-E-A-T score of your entire domain
3. in a world where everything seems fake, make your brand human

shoot me a DM if you want a live demo of the lowest hanging fruit to rank in AI Search (tailored to your specific site)

i hope this helps someone!

## 159. Thread by @natzir9

[1/9] 1/ "No matter how prominently we show the links, users won't click."

An OpenAI engineer, quoted in the newly unsealed NYT v OpenAI filing.

Buried in 92 pages there's a traffic story every SEO should read 🧵 

[2/9] 2/ The filing was unsealed Sept 17 and surfaced by @jason_kint. All credit to him, thread here: 

[3/9] 3/ Nick Turley, Head of ChatGPT, on the browse feature: once the chatbot answers, there is "no good reason to click" the source link .

Same exec on publishers: "our products are largely substitutive, period" and "will get more and more substitutive as they get better." 

[4/9] 4/ Microsoft's own CTR data: Copilot's click-through rate to NYT domains is 87-93% lower than traditional Bing Search. 83-91% for the Daily News papers, 51-94% for Ziff Davis.

And Nadella under oath: the chatbot "has substituted" visiting the underlying source. 

[5/9] 5/ A Microsoft internal doc: "Our AI content strategy has started a 'doom loop' that will hurt the performance of our models and the entire web at the same time... an end-product threatens the economic foundations of its essential suppliers." 

MSFT describing its own product... 

[6/9] 6/ The part OpenAI won't enjoy resurfacing: their own paid experts concede Google AI Overviews "may have depressed search referrals by 20 to 60 percent" for the plaintiff newspapers.

Also: 87.78% of ChatGPT users visit no external site during a search. On Google it's 26.91%. 

[7/9] 7/ That internal admission now has causal evidence behind it. Agarwal &amp; Sen () ran the first pre-registered RCT on AIO: a Chrome extension on 1,065 users for 2 weeks. One group saw standard Google, the other had AIOs removed from the SERP in real time. 

[8/9] 8/ Results, when an AIO appears:

 &gt; External organic clicks: −39.8% (−18.5% averaged over all searches)
 &gt; Zero-click searches: +34.5%
 &gt; Paid clicks: no change. The substitution is AIO vs organic. Google loses nothing. 

[9/9] 9/ Click quality was identical across groups: same BR, dwell time &amp; pogo-sticking. That was Google's main defense ("AIOs filter out low-quality bounce clicks") and it doesn't survive the data.
User satisfaction: no measurable difference... AIOs take traffic and give nothing back.

## 161. Thread by @Timseo_

[1/14] MAIS COMMENT CRÉER UN SITE QUI RANKE SUR GOOGLE AVEC CLAUDE, SANS AUCUN CMS ? je te donne 5 conseils ↓

(précision : je ne sais pas coder)

1. toujours commencer par la base technique SEO, AVANT de lui demander un beau design

la première règle c’est que je veux un site indexable sur Google et Chatgpt !

↓

[2/14] Je lui demande du HTML statique, un framework SSR/SSG comme Next.js ou Astro, ou un prerendering après le build. Pas une SPA pure.

pour chaque URL, je veux un HTML qui contient déjà le contenu, le title, la meta description et la canonical. 

pas des balises ajoutées uniquement en JavaScript après le chargement.

Je lui demande aussi de prévoir :

→ un sitemap.xml généré automatiquement à partir des pages du site

→ un robots.txt qui pointe vers le sitemap et laisse les ressources JS/CSS accessibles 

→ de vraies réponses 404 pour les pages qui n’existent pas

sur ce dernier point, afficher « page introuvable » ne suffit pas. Je veux que le serveur renvoie un statut 404, pas 200.

Il est capable de faire ça ensuite :

[3/14] 2. lui donner les règles SEO qu’il devra appliquer à CHAQUE page

je ne lui dis pas simplement « optimise mon site pour le SEO ». Je lui donne les éléments à intégrer :

→ un title et une meta description uniques, rédigés à partir du contenu de la page

→ un seul H1, puis des H2 et H3 cohérents

→ une canonical explicite sur chaque URL

→ les balises Open Graph et Twitter Card pour le partage sur les réseaux

Je précise aussi les données structurées : Organization et WebSite sur l’accueil, Article sur les articles de blog, FAQPage uniquement lorsqu’une FAQ est réellement affichée.

Je ne veux pas qu’il ajoute des schémas qui ne correspondent pas au contenu visible. 

Ces consignes doivent s’appliquer aux pages qu’il crée maintenant, mais aussi à celles qu’on ajoutera ensuite.

[4/14] 3. lui expliquer précisément ce que je ne veux PAS dans le design

si je lui demande seulement « un site moderne », je lui laisse décider de presque tout. 

Je préfère lui donner mes consignes dès le départ :

→ pas de traits colorés sur le côté des cartes ou des citations
→ pas de petits libellés en majuscules partout
→ pas de petits textes au-dessus de chaque titre, les fameux « eyebrows »
→ pas de halos, de grosses ombres ou de polices serif omniprésentes
→ pas de noms de sections pompeux

pour les polices, j’utilise uniquement Geist et Geist Mono. Je lui précise donc de ne pas utiliser Inter ou Instrument Serif.

Ce sont MES choix de design. Tu peux donner les tiens. L’important, c’est de lui expliquer ce que tu veux retrouver sur ton site, et ce que tu ne veux surtout pas voir.

[5/14] 4. travailler la version mobile et le chargement des pages

dans mes consignes, je demande un site 100 % responsive. Mais je reprends aussi la version mobile séparément : je ne me contente pas d’une version desktop réduite.

par exemple, je ne veux pas de tableaux qu’il faut faire défiler horizontalement. Je demande des colonnes adaptatives et du texte qui revient à la ligne.

Pour le chargement, je précise :

→ du lazy loading sur les images hors écran

→ des attributs width et height sur les images pour éviter les décalages de mise en page

→ font-display: swap pour les polices

je donne ces consignes dès le départ, plutôt que de les ajouter une fois toutes les pages créées.

J'utilise cette extension pour la vue mobile :

[6/14] 5. itérer (pas le choix), je reprends chaque section avec Claude

la première version, c’est le point de départ. Ensuite, je travaille le hero, les sections, les animations, le footer etc. 

je vais chercher des idées sur 21dev ou One Page Love, puis je lui montre ce que j’aimerais reprendre en l’adaptant à mon site.

en gros, tu lui dis :

"Lance le site en local et donne-moi le lien localhost pour l’ouvrir dans mon navigateur"

tu gardes le site ouvert d’un côté et Claude de l’autre. 

Tu vérifies les changements dans ton navigateur et tu continues les allers-retours.
