## 162. The GPT-6 Astra SEO Agent Setup Behind 78k AI Mentions

If you want AI to help you grow search traffic, but:

↳ You keep copying data between tools manually

↳ Every audit is confusing and adds work

Then give GPT-6 Astra the data and instructions to take over the SEO work your team keeps repeating.

Give Astra an SEO brain to automate these 15 tasks with this plugin

I analyzed 100 pages from a fresh Search Console export and sorted them by clicks, then by impressions. Only 2 pages appeared in both top 10 lists. Choosing a different metric changed almost the entire shortlist. This was a capped sample from one site, so it doesn't tell us which edits will increase traffic.

We built the Distribb skill around 15 jobs, from researching what people search to reporting what changed after you published.

You can hand it one job today. Give it an existing page, connect the data it needs and review the first output before expanding to the rest of your site.

The skill is free to download. Your AI subscription and connected services can cost extra, including the paid Distribb platform.

TL;DR

The same 100 pages produced two very different priorities depending on the metric. Our automated SEO software article had 28,761 impressions and 20 clicks in the saved export. That makes it worth investigating before commissioning another article about the same topic.

The skill supports these 15 jobs:

↳ Keyword research: find searches and buyer questions worth answering.

↳ Search Console: inspect page performance and Google AI impressions.

↳ AI mentions: check whether answers name your business.

↳ Citation gaps: inspect the sources AI answers use instead.

↳ Opportunity scoring: decide which job deserves attention first.

↳ Topical authority: map buyer questions to pages.

↳ Content briefs: specify the evidence a new article needs.

↳ LLM structure: make the main answer easy to find and read.

↳ Internal links: connect relevant existing pages.

↳ Cannibalization: investigate pages competing for the same intent.

↳ Content refresh: update pages with a specific reason to change.

↳ Technical audits: find crawl, indexing and page problems.

↳ Link outreach: research publishers and prepare useful pitches.

↳ Link exchange: inspect relevant placements and verification.

↳ SEO reporting: explain what moved and what to do next.

Only 2 pages appeared in both top 10 lists

Our homepage led the click list. Our automated SEO software article led the impression list.

Before refreshing that article, I want to know which searches produced the impressions, where the page appeared and whether those searchers could become customers. A page about the wrong topic can collect plenty of impressions.

Ask the agent to explain why a page deserves attention before adding work to the queue. Sorting a spreadsheet is easy. Choosing a useful next action requires the business context.

Install the skill

The public repository includes this installation command:

Follow the installer for Codex, select GPT-6 Astra and ask it to confirm that it can read the skill. Connect your own account for the services you want it to use, or supply exports where the workflow supports them.

Give the agent your website, audience, main offer and target country. Tell it what counts as a useful conversion and which actions you want to review.

For Distribb, a founder looking for SEO publishing software is relevant. Someone looking only for a free Instagram hashtag is a different prospect, even if that page gets more clicks.

Have the agent list its available data before starting. The ChatGPT screens below are separate examples of citation checks and planning, not a test of Astra's performance.

A playbook for 15 SEO jobs

These are jobs you can delegate individually. On an established site, start with the audits before adding more pages. The examples use our automated SEO software article wherever possible so you can follow one page through the work.

Step 1: Run keyword research

Start with searches already bringing your pages into results. In Search Console, select a page and open Queries. Our article appears for searches including "automated seo tools" and "seo automation software."

Then add questions from sales calls, support conversations and product comparisons. A buyer might ask, "Which SEO tool lets my editor approve a draft before it reaches WordPress?"

↳ Label searches from Search Console as observed.

↳ Label questions suggested by AI as ideas to test.

↳ Group them by what the reader needs to do.

↳ Match each group to an existing page before proposing another.

An AI-generated question has no measured search demand just because it sounds plausible. Keep the source beside it. The screenshot is a separate live walkthrough from the saved study.

Step 2: Audit Search Console

Have the agent compare complete periods with the same filters. Review pages gaining impressions, losing clicks or appearing for searches that do not fit the offer.

Search Console also has a Generative AI features report for AI Overviews and AI Mode. Our live screen shows 78.2K impressions over three months. This report has no separate click, CTR or user-prompt metrics.

↳ Check the page's queries and position before blaming its title.

↳ Keep Google AI impressions separate from external AI citation checks.

↳ Never add AI impressions to Web impressions: Google already includes them in that total.

The 78.2K figure is a later live example, not a result produced by installing the skill.

Step 3: Track AI mentions

Use questions a buyer would ask without already knowing your brand. I tested: "Which SEO automation tools let a small team review content before publishing to WordPress?"

ChatGPT cited Distribb but said it could not verify details such as separate reviewer and publisher roles. That gives me a documentation question to investigate. It does not establish that the product lacks those controls.

↳ Record the exact prompt, date, engine and mode.

↳ Save what the answer says about your business.

↳ Separate an unlinked mention from a link to your site.

↳ Repeat the same questions to see whether a pattern holds.

One answer is one observation. Keep unavailable answers marked as missing so they do not become false zeroes in your report.

Step 4: Find citation gaps

Open the sources behind the recommendation. A competitor's name tells you who appeared; the cited page tells you what evidence the answer used.

In this answer, Frase and AirOps appeared in the opening recommendation. I would inspect the cited pages for the publishing and review details the question asked about.

↳ Save the exact cited URL, not only the domain.

↳ Identify the fact the source supports.

↳ Check whether your site answers that question clearly.

↳ Decide whether you need a documentation update, a test or a new page.

For our example, a clear explanation of how draft approval works could address the uncertainty in the answer. Test that explanation in future checks rather than assuming it will earn a citation.

Step 5: Use opportunity scoring

Give the agent a choice between specific jobs. For our software article, that might be checking approval details, refreshing outdated comparisons or writing a separate setup guide.

Score buyer fit and strength of evidence from 1 to 5, with a reason for each. Add an effort estimate that names the work involved.

↳ Would the answer help someone choose or use the product?

↳ What observation supports doing this now?

↳ What access, research and editing would it require?

↳ What missing fact could change the priority?

I would investigate the existing comparison first because the page and relevant searches already exist. The scores help choose the next investigation; they are judgments, not traffic forecasts.

Step 6: Map topical authority

Build the topic map around decisions your buyer needs to make. For SEO automation, choosing software is different from setting up WordPress publishing or checking whether an editor can approve a draft.

Assign an existing URL to each question. Propose a new page only when the question needs its own answer and your site does not already have one.

↳ Give each page a main buyer question.

↳ Record the evidence that page needs.

↳ Show where it fits beside the comparison and setup guides.

↳ Connect pages when the reader would naturally need the next one.

The screenshot is a proposed map. Publishing every row would not prove authority. First check which answers would help your customers and which pages already cover them.

Step 7: Write content briefs

Ask for a useful addition before asking for an outline. Our comparison could answer whether an editor can review a draft before it reaches WordPress.

The brief should require the publishing mode, approval controls, plan required and a dated source for each product. If you test the workflow, record the same steps for every tool. If you only read documentation, say that.

↳ State the question the finished page will answer.

↳ Specify the evidence missing from the current page.

↳ Leave unverified results empty.

↳ Explain where the reader can check the finding.

That is information gain a writer can work with: a specific comparison backed by evidence. A longer outline alone adds no new finding.

Step 8: Improve LLM structure

Make the main answer easy to locate on the page. Under a heading about WordPress approval, explain the workflow directly and put the supporting comparison beside it.

↳ Use descriptive headings that match the reader's question.

↳ Keep important facts in readable text and properly labeled tables.

↳ Check that crawlers can access the main content.

↳ Place sources and limits close to the claims they support.

Google's current guidance encourages readable content and sensible HTML. It does not require special AI markup or a new schema for its generative AI features. Other assistants have their own retrieval systems, so clear formatting cannot promise a citation everywhere.

Step 9: Add internal links

Use a crawl to find relevant source pages, then ask for the exact sentence where each link belongs. A guide to setting up SEO monitoring could link to our comparison when its reader needs to choose software.

The Search Console Links report helps inspect what Google has found. It is a sample, so a missing row does not prove a page has no internal links.

↳ Show the source page and destination.

↳ Include the proposed anchor and surrounding sentence.

↳ Check that the destination works and is the intended canonical URL.

↳ Review the edits before applying them across the site.

A page linked from every footer may have a large count. Inspect relevant links inside the content before deciding it already has enough support.

Step 10: Check cannibalization

Filter Search Console to one query, then switch to Pages. For "automated seo software," our live report shows both the automated-seo-software article and the best-automated-seo-software article.

The first has 5,849 impressions and the second has 2,434 in that three-month view. Those are separate live counts from the study. They show overlap worth inspecting, not proof that one page harmed the other.

↳ Compare what each page promises and answers.

↳ Check which URL appears across comparable periods.

↳ Review clicks, position and whether the intended page is appearing.

↳ Choose a distinct purpose, a refresh or a merge only after that review.

Two useful pages can appear for the same query. The decision depends on whether they answer different needs and whether either page is underperforming for a reason you can substantiate.

Step 11: Plan a content refresh

Ask the agent why an existing page needs changing. Useful reasons include outdated product details, a changed reader question or a documented decline that needs investigation.

For our comparison, the approval question gives the refresh a specific purpose. Check the current facts, write the missing explanation and keep the useful material already on the page.

↳ Name the problem and its evidence.

↳ Show the current passage beside the proposed replacement.

↳ Record the reviewer and publication date.

↳ Save the baseline before publishing.

The Distribb screen shows proposed rewrites awaiting review, with auto-push off. A recommendation in that queue is still a proposal; it has no measured effect until it is applied and observed.

Step 12: Run technical audits

Give the agent crawl results and indexing evidence. A homepage fetch cannot establish that the rest of the site is accessible or indexed correctly.

Our report includes both "Alternate page with proper canonical tag" and "Server error (5xx)." They need different treatment. A correctly excluded alternate URL may be working as intended. A server error needs a check of the affected URL and its current response.

↳ Group issues by cause and affected page type.

↳ Check whether the page should be indexed at all.

↳ Inspect redirects, canonicals and access restrictions.

↳ Prioritize important pages with a reproducible problem.

Use URL Inspection and a current fetch when investigating an old report row. The goal is to make the right pages available, rather than make every excluded URL turn green.

Step 13: Prepare link outreach

Find relevant publishers and read the page you would contact them about. The screenshot shows Webbiquity's SEO tool roundup, a public example of the kind of page an agent could research.

Suppose we complete the approval-workflow comparison. The agent can check whether a roundup covers that decision, identify a specific omission and draft an email explaining the tested finding an editor could add.

↳ Record the prospect page and why it is relevant.

↳ Explain what your evidence adds for its readers.

↳ Draft a message that refers to the actual article.

↳ Track replies and any resulting mention separately from messages sent.

The public page is a prospect to research. No outreach was sent for this example.

Step 14: Review link exchange placements

The skill also supports Distribb's link exchange workflow. Review the actual source page and destination behind a placement, including its verification status.

For a link to our software comparison, I would open the source page and read the surrounding paragraph. A topical connection and a useful destination matter more to that reader than the site's domain score.

↳ Check the source page is accessible and the link is present.

↳ Confirm the destination and anchor match the intended placement.

↳ Review relevance, link attributes and any commercial terms.

↳ Record the verification date so it can be checked again.

The captured account has network participation disabled. The screen demonstrates inspection of recorded placements, not a new exchange carried out for this article. Platform verification also does not mean Google approves a placement; the policy check below applies.

Step 15: Automate SEO reporting

After reviewing a manual run, schedule the agent to prepare a weekly report using the same site, saved buyer questions and measurement definitions.

Compare complete periods with matching filters. Check the actual dates and seasonality before interpreting a last-28-days comparison.

↳ List shipped changes separately from recommendations.

↳ Report search clicks, impressions and useful conversions where available.

↳ Keep Google AI impressions and external AI citations distinct.

↳ End with the next action, its owner and the evidence behind it.

For our comparison page, the first report would record the proposed refresh and baseline. There is no improvement to attribute to an edit that has not been published.

Use plain English: "The page gained impressions, clicks stayed flat, and we need to inspect which queries changed." Keep a change log so future reports can check what happened around the same time.

The 15 steps again

↳ Run keyword research using searches and customer questions.

↳ Audit Search Console before choosing a page to change.

↳ Track AI mentions with a saved set of buyer questions.

↳ Find citation gaps by opening the sources.

↳ Use opportunity scoring to choose the next investigation.

↳ Map topical authority around questions your pages need to answer.

↳ Write content briefs that specify new evidence.

↳ Improve LLM structure with readable, accessible answers.

↳ Add internal links where they help the reader.

↳ Check cannibalization before merging overlapping pages.

↳ Plan a content refresh with a reason and a baseline.

↳ Run technical audits on the pages that should be available.

↳ Prepare link outreach around a useful contribution.

↳ Review link exchange placements and their terms.

↳ Automate SEO reporting that names the next action.

Pick the job your team repeats every week. Run it on one page, review the output and save the instructions that worked.

Give your agent an SEO brain with this tool

Thanks for reading.

Borja

## 165. Thread by @BasicProtein26

Former Scale AI founder and newly appointed @Meta Chief AI Officer @alexandr_wang dropped a bombshell during his YC conversation with @garrytan, and you could almost hear the tech leadership world go quiet.

He said Meta has already seen this internally:

Build the right agentic loop, give it an evaluation system and metrics that let it optimize itself, and a group of AI agents can complete more work than a team of 100 senior engineers.

And they do it “very easily.”

But the most interesting part wasn’t the 100-engineer comparison.

It was how simple the system underneath it actually is.

You’d expect some insanely complex, almost alien architecture powering a swarm like this.

Instead, Wang described it with a few almost comically basic words:

“Markdown files, cron jobs, goal, metrics, data.”

Once you strip away the hype, the implications for traditional software engineering become pretty clear:

1️⃣ It’s not that the models are magically smarter. The eval loop is doing the heavy lifting.

Traditional approach: humans write prompts, run the code, inspect the output, and hope nothing broke.

Meta’s approach: turn the business goal into something a machine can score automatically.

The agent submits its work. The system runs tests, calculates metrics, finds what’s wrong, and sends it back for another pass. Repeat until it passes.

Nobody has to babysit every step. The metric becomes the supervisor.

2️⃣ The real alpha is burning 1,000x more tokens inside the feedback loop.

A lot of people are still optimizing for the cost of a single AI call.

The frontier labs are playing a different game: spend 1,000x or even 1,000,000x more tokens in the background so agents can constantly review, rerun, challenge, and verify each other’s work until they reach a reliable business outcome.

Token cost is fixed. The payoff is a pipeline that keeps running.

3️⃣ Memory doesn’t need some fancy database.

Persistent memory can live in Markdown files.

Scheduling can be handled by the server’s built-in cron jobs, running overnight.

The simpler the scaffolding, the more robust the system can be. Less infrastructure also means fewer ways for context to fall apart.

This is a pretty brutal change in how technical organizations work.

The ceiling for a tech lead used to be partly about how many people they could manage, how many meetings they could sit through, and how many teams they could coordinate.

The leverage for the next generation of technical leaders may look very different:

Can you turn a messy business objective into a rigorous set of metrics that an AI can evaluate automatically?

If 100 people’s output can be replaced by a few cron jobs, Markdown files, and a well-designed eval loop, the era of “just take the ticket and write the code” is coming to an end.

The people who can design the evals and orchestrate the swarm aren’t just holding a new tool.

They’re effectively running a virtual company.

## 169. how to cook killer advertorials with fable 5.1 (for ecom google ads) 

the highest-converting page we've ever shipped for an ecom account had no pricing, no buy button, and no brand name anywhere in the first 900 words

it pulled 4-13x ROAS on keywords the product page was converting at 1.5x

and the reason has almost nothing to do with the writing

it's that the page was built for a buyer who doesn't know your product category exists yet, and 80% of the traffic your non-branded campaigns are buying right now is exactly that person

here's the full system we use to build them inside claude fable 5.1

why advertorials print harder on google than anywhere else

on meta, your creative dies every 14 to 21 days. the same audience keeps seeing it, saturation kicks in, and performance decays no matter how good the asset was

google works on the opposite mechanic

intent-based traffic rotates naturally. someone typing "why do i wake up at 3am" for the first time today has never encountered your advertorial. the audience refreshes itself

which means a winning advertorial keeps printing for MONTHS

you build the asset once. the traffic arrives fresh every day. the economics of putting real work into a single page stop looking insane and start looking obvious

there's a second reason specific to certain categories

in supplements, health, and anything adjacent, google restricts what you're allowed to claim in ad copy. your ads are handcuffed by policy

the advertorial is where the selling legally happens. which makes it less of an option and more of a requirement in those verticals

the 3 coordinates that decide whether an advertorial works

before writing a word, you need to know where the page sits on 3 axes. get any of them wrong and the asset fails regardless of how well written it is

coordinate 1: awareness level

advertorials serve PROBLEM-AWARE buyers

these people know something is wrong. they don't know the solution category exists, and they've never heard of you

they're typing complaints into google. "why does my skin get worse in winter." "can't stay asleep even when i'm exhausted." "my dog's joints are stiff going down stairs"

send that buyer to a product page with a price and a buy button and they leave in 3 seconds. no rapport, no context, no reason to care

send a solution-aware buyer to an advertorial and you lose them for the opposite reason. they already accepted the category and want a comparison. the education feels like a delay

coordinate 2: funnel stage

advertorials sit at TOF and upper MOF

they're fed by search campaigns on symptom queries, demand gen with 9:16 shorts, and youtube in-stream. cold traffic that needs a belief built before a purchase makes sense

they're the wrong destination for branded search or remarketing. that traffic already knows you, and educating a warm buyer wastes the trust you already earned

coordinate 3: market sophistication

this is the one nobody accounts for and it decides your angle entirely

schwartz's 5 stages, applied to ecom:

stage 1: nobody has made the claim yet. state it plainly and win

stage 2: claims are escalating. make yours bigger

stage 3: mechanism differentiation begins. explain HOW it works

stage 4: mechanisms compete. yours has to be sharper or more specific

stage 5: the market is exhausted. identity and experience carry the sale

nearly every ecom category worth being in sits at stage 4 or 5

which means a bigger promise does nothing. every competitor already made one and the buyer has stopped believing all of them

at stage 4, the mechanism IS the angle. at stage 5, the mechanism plus who this is for

running a stage 2 angle in a stage 4 market is why founders conclude their traffic is bad when their positioning is the problem

step 1: the context load

fable 5.1's reasoning gains are real, and they're worth nothing if the model is guessing about your buyer

open a project and upload:

every product page

every review you can export

refund reasons and support tickets

sales call notes and objection docs

your winning and losing meta creatives

90 days of search terms reports

your top 5 competitor URLs

current landing pages

the load prompt:

"act as the advertorial strategist for this brand. read everything uploaded and build a working profile: ICP segments ranked by revenue, the pain points in the customer's own words, the objections ordered by how often they appear across reviews and tickets, the proof assets we already own, and the beliefs a buyer must hold before purchasing. do not write anything yet. summarise what you found, then tell me what context is MISSING"

that final instruction is the one to steal. it converts the model from a confident guesser into a research partner that shows you your own gaps

the claude md file

save this as your project instructions so every session runs to the same standard:

"you are an advertorial strategist for ecom google ads traffic. you write for problem-aware buyers arriving from symptom queries and cold video. you write in editorial voice and the brand does not appear until the mechanism has landed. the mechanism section carries at least 40% of the word count. every pain phrase is copied verbatim from the research, never paraphrased. you always ask which keyword cluster and campaign feeds the page before writing it. when a claim has no proof in the research, you flag it instead of writing around it."

step 2: research (where 5.1 pulls away)

the research jump is the reason this workflow changed. 5.1 crawls a wide set of sources in a single pass and holds all of it in context while it reasons

the language mining prompt:

"search reddit threads, quora answers, amazon reviews and Q&A sections, youtube comments on category and competitor videos, trustpilot, and niche forums for people discussing [PROBLEM]. extract VERBATIM language, never paraphrase. for each: the quote, the pain underneath it, what they already tried, why it failed them, the outcome they want, and the emotional state behind it"

paraphrasing is how advertorials end up sounding like marketing. the entire value sits in their exact words

3-star reviews and amazon Q&A carry more signal than anything else on that list. 5-star reviews tell you what people liked. 3-star reviews tell you what nearly stopped them buying, which is your objection map

the belief audit:

"from the research, list every belief the buyer must accept before purchasing, ordered by how hard each one is to install. for each: the evidence that would install it, and whether we currently own that evidence"

this output becomes your advertorial's spine. the page exists to install those beliefs in order

if you want us to build this out for your brand instead, DM me "ADV" and we'll show you what it'd look like

step 3: the competitor teardown

"research [COMPETITOR 1, 2, 3]. use google ads transparency center, meta ad library, their landing pages and email capture. for each: every live ad and where it routes, the landing page TYPE, hero promise, the mechanism they claim, the proof they lead with, objections handled and in what order, offer and guarantee, and what they are NOT saying"

then the prompt that finds your opening:

"for each competitor page, tell me which awareness level it was built for. then identify which of their traffic is landing on the wrong page type"

a competitor running search ads on symptom queries and routing them to a PDP is leaving that entire pool uncontested

and if none of your competitors run a mechanism-led advertorial in a stage 4 market, that's not a gap in the market. that's the market handing you the only angle still working

step 4: the brief before the build

skip this and you get a 1,400 word page that reads well and installs nothing

"build the advertorial brief for [PRODUCT] targeting [SYMPTOM QUERY]. specify: the awareness level, the market sophistication stage and what that permits, the exact opening symptom in verbatim buyer language, the 3 failed solutions to name and the mechanical reason each fails, the hidden cause, the mechanism and why it's more specific than what competitors claim, the beliefs installed in order, the proof mapped to each objection, and the word count per section"

enforcing word counts per section is what stops the model spending 600 words on the hook and 80 on the mechanism

step 5: the build

"write the advertorial from the brief. 1,200-1,500 words. structure block by block with the word counts enforced: symptom opening in their verbatim language, agitation naming 3 failed solutions and why each failed, hidden cause, mechanism carrying 40% of the total, product introduced as the implementation of that mechanism, proof organised by objection, soft CTA. editorial voice throughout. no brand language before the product section. output as a deployable page with design notes so it does not look like it belongs to our brand"

that last instruction matters more than it looks

an advertorial carrying your brand's fonts, colours, and nav bar reads as an ad within 2 seconds and the guard goes up. it should look like an article on a publication

then push it into claude code to ship it. 5.1 writes the page, the styling, and the mobile behaviour in the same session. what used to sit in a dev queue for 3 weeks now takes an afternoon

step 6: the ads that feed it

the ad and the page are 1 unit. the ad sets an expectation and the first screen either confirms it in 3 seconds or the click is wasted

"write 15 responsive search ad headlines under 30 characters for [SYMPTOM QUERY], routing to this advertorial. headline 1 must mirror the search query almost word for word. include problem-led, curiosity-led and mechanism-hint variations. no product name, no offer language, no discount. then write 4 descriptions under 90 characters that promise an explanation rather than a purchase"

advertorial ads promise an explanation

the second you put a discount in the copy, the buyer expects a product page. they arrive at an article, feel the mismatch, and bounce

the youtube layer:

"take the advertorial section with the highest scroll depth and turn it into a 15-second 9:16 script. the page's opening line becomes the opening 3 seconds. hook, agitation, mechanism, soft reveal. route this ad to the same advertorial, never to the PDP"

repurposing a proven section means the creative carries evidence before you spend a dollar

step 7: the PDP behind the advertorial

the advertorial's job ends at the click through. the page it hands off to decides whether the sale closes

and sending a reader who has already accepted your mechanism to a standard 400 word PDP throws away everything the advertorial built

"write the post-advertorial PDP for [PRODUCT]. this buyer arrives having already accepted [MECHANISM]. do not re-explain it. open by confirming the mechanism in 1 line, then move straight to how this product implements it better than alternatives. sequence: mechanism confirmation, formulation or build reasoning as proof of the mechanism, transformation, review wall organised by OBJECTION rather than by date, comparison against the 2 alternatives they're realistically considering, FAQ from real support tickets, risk reversal, offer"

organising the review wall by objection turns social proof into an objection-handling engine. the buyer worried about taste finds 3 reviews about taste

step 8: the optimisation loop

export your landing page report and your search terms report weekly

"analyse this landing page CSV against the query clusters routing to each page. for the advertorial: sessions, bounce rate, time on page, scroll depth, click-through to PDP, and conversion. tell me whether the losses are an awareness mismatch or a page problem, then name the specific section where readers drop"

how to read what comes back:

high bounce with high intent traffic means an awareness mismatch. the wrong queries are routing there. fix the campaign, not the page

strong time on page with weak click-through to the PDP means the mechanism landed and the product introduction failed

scroll depth dying at a specific section tells you exactly which block to rewrite. usually the mechanism, usually because it stayed abstract

good click-through with poor PDP conversion means the handoff is broken. the PDP is re-explaining something the reader already accepted

then:

"rewrite the section where readers are dropping. keep everything before it identical so we can attribute the change"

changing 1 block at a time is the only way you learn anything. rewriting the whole page teaches you nothing about what was wrong

the principles underneath all of it

the reader never trusts your brand. they trust the mechanism

trust transfers from logic to whatever implements the logic. once the reader accepts that a specific cause creates their problem, anything correcting that cause inherits credibility they already granted

your product doesn't earn belief. it inherits it

specificity is the entire hook

"struggling to sleep?" stops nobody. "you fall asleep fine, it's the 3am wake-up where your brain switches on like someone flipped a light" stops the exact person you want

the more precisely you describe the symptom, the more the reader assumes you understand the cause

every failed solution you name is a micro-validation

when you list melatonin tolerance, sleep apps, and blue light glasses, the reader thinks "i tried that too" 3 times in a row

each one deepens their investment and pre-frames your mechanism as the thing all those attempts missed

the mechanism section is where accounts are won

this is the section brands cut for length and it's the reason their advertorials underperform

in a stage 4 market, the mechanism is the only differentiator left. cutting it to 200 words to get to the product faster removes the only thing making the product credible

editorial voice is a conversion mechanic

the buyer's ad filter fires on brand signals. logos, brand fonts, nav bars, and marketing cadence all trigger it

an advertorial that looks like an article keeps that filter off long enough for the argument to land

the brands running advertorials on google are converting cold traffic their competitors are writing off as unprofitable

the ones sending everything to a PDP conclude google doesn't work for their category, when the channel was fine and the destination was wrong

this is the same funnel architecture behind the automotive brand at 21x ROAS from $57k in spend, the supplement brand we scaled from $155k to $328k a month in 90 days, and the clothing brand producing $61,783 from $3,845 in spend

fable 5.1 collapsed the production cost of the asset. the thinking is still the hard part, and now it's the only part

if you want us to audit your ecom brand and build the advertorial funnel architecture that turns your cold google traffic profitable, DM me "ADV" and we'll make it happen

($10M+ in ad revenue generated through google ads ecosystems, fully done-for-you)

amin

## 176. Thread by @om_patel5

mobile app founders are sleeping on seo

everyone fights for app store rankings and nobody builds a site that ranks on google

1\ get your keywords from complaints, not keyword tools

> pull the 1 and 2 star reviews of the top 30 competitors in your niche
> those reviews contain the exact phrasing people use, which is never the phrasing marketers use
> e.g. "my period tracker keeps losing my data" is a real query
> every recurring complaint is a page

2\ build 4 page types and nothing else

> problem pages: "how to [outcome] without [the annoying part]"
> alternative pages: "best [competitor] alternatives" and "[competitor] vs [you]", these convert the hardest because the person is already leaving
> comparison roundups: "7 best [category] apps", include your competitors honestly, rank yourself where you actually belong
> use case pages: "[category] app for [specific person]", nurses, truckers, students, parents of toddlers

3\ program the ones that repeat

> if the page shape is identical and only one variable changes, generate them
> "best budgeting app for [city]" or "[competitor] alternative for [platform]" scales to hundreds of pages off one template
> keep them genuinely useful or google strips the whole set (obviously)

4\ actually answer the question in the first 100 words

> nobody scrolls past a 600 word intro to find the answer
> answer it, then expand
> this is also what gets you cited in ai engines, which is a second traffic source nobody is optimising for yet (especially for apps)

5\ then convert

> download button after the answer
> one line on why your app solves that exact thing
> screenshots showing the specific feature the page is about (not your generic app store shots)

6\ mine your own search console every week (setup a gsc mcp via api)

> the queries with impressions and zero clicks are easy wins
> rewrite the title to match what they actually typed
> rewrite the losers instead of publishing more

they searched the exact problem your app solves

thats the highest intent traffic you will ever get, and its free

one of my users for  actually ran this strategy

and hes at $14k mrr purely from seo, compounded over a few months

its a free app with one paid feature, no ad spend and no heavy paywall

heres what his trial converts look like right now

## 180. Thread by @adamlyttleapps

I've had a bunch of people ask me how I made this

This is my exact process:

First I drew a sketch of what I wanted the subway station to look like. I asked Nano Banana 2 to turn it into a render using this prompt:

"turn this sketch into a photorealistic Unreal engine generated game scene from an AAA game studio. its a subway station looking straight down the walkway between the platforms with trains on either platform. load baring pillars and the stairwell ahead that takes you to the subway above"

Then I asked GPT-6 Astra on Ultra to turn into a technical demo that showcases its capabilities with this prompt:

"Create a threejs interactive technical demo that showcases your models technical capabilities of this subway scene '/Users/adamlyttle/dev/subway-test/subway-scene.png'. It must look exactly like this scene with trains entering and leaving the platforms in a realistic manner. Doors opening and the interactive first person view being able to walk around the platform, enter a train when it’s at the station and explore upstairs. Upstairs will have the ticket gates, toilets and a convenience store that is stocked. The brief is for the entire interactive area to have the same photo realism as the photo attached. it must be able to run on desktop using a mouse + keyboard or gamepad using normal game controls and no ability to walk through walls. I should be able to explore the platform, the train carriages, the upstairs area, the toilets, the convenience store and I should be able to look at the ticket gates but not exit. This is a closed world after all. Every scene built needs to feel like its part of the same world as the original photo, a person looking must immediately think they are part of the same demonstration with the same photo realism. When I enter a train the train will not leave the platform. You have access to fal ai mcp for asset generation (textures, mesh, etc). Your job is to build the scene to be a perfect replica of the attached photo and expand on the world into the train carriages and the upstairs area. Quality is AAA game with photo realism perfect replica of attached photo. You are not authorised to look at the other agents work in the subway-test folder. In the progress folder generate a index.html file which documents your process including screenshots in a way that a human looking will understand your thinking and process and see the journey as it unfolds. Build it."

That's it. Astra did the rest for me.

It only took 26m 9s and used 10,460,032 tokens to generate 🤷🏻‍♂️

## 183. Thread by @bcherny

[1/6] Every day, I get a lot of of emails and messages like this one. I try to respond to as many as I can.

Sharing my response below, for anyone else in a similar situation. What do you think? 

[2/6] Hey ████,

I think there is room for both.

1. Prototypes and other throw-away code can be treated as totally black box. If you’re going to throw it away anyway, and if the blast radius of it breaking is low, it doesn’t need to be perfect.
2. Production code written by Claude should have a higher bar than if it was written by a human. At Anthropic, we have many guardrails in place to make sure this is happening: lots of lint rules, lots of tests, Claude-driven end to end tests, Claude-powered fuzzers running daily, automated code reviews and security reviews, automated code refactoring, and so on. Without these, you can end up with a mess that is hard to maintain down the line. Luckily, the model makes it increasingly easy to do these well — run a few daily routines, use Claude Code Review, etc.

Your job is to hold the bar on code quality. If Claude’s code doesn’t meet the bar, try:
- Using the latest frontier model (Opus 5 or Fable 5.1)
- Increase effort to high or xhigh
- Invest in your CLAUDE.md and skills to succinctly teach Claude how to work in your codebase

If all else fails, steer Claude more when you work with it, or have Claude fix accumulated debt and rewrite your codebase to make it easier to work with. Or, wait for the next model.

Best,
Boris

[3/6] @Greg_TheBuilder Agree - should choose the right approach for the job

[4/6] @ibesh_tech Exactly

[5/6] @hellovidya I love listening &amp; helping. Every company is different and we're all just trying to figure it out

[6/6] We are in a weird moment: coding is automated, but the rest — system design, code review, and so on — are not yet fully automated. For the next 6-12 months, some engineering skills you learn in school will remain critical for people that write software. After that, they will become basic “background” skills you learn in school, like geometry and history are today. You don’t use these every day but they give you frameworks and ways of thinking about the world that come in handy later on.

## 186. Thread by @RohanNayak2

[1/9] We grew from ~0 to $500M ARR, adding $250M last year alone while being EBITDA profitable.

1,300-word post on every growth tactic that worked for us:

1. What got us from 0-$400M ARR in the US works in every country.
2. Retention and Monetisation Hacks.
3. Localization > Translation.
4. Experiment with $1M internal seed checks.
5. Pivoting to only AI content production unlocked 100% ARR growth.

Pocket FM is like Netflix for audio-only dramas, with our own pool of one-person studios.

1. Acquisition Playbook for 0-$10M ARR in any country in 6 months

We run a 90 sec video trailer of an audio drama as an Ad and ask users to download the app if they're interested in the rest of the story.

Our core insight after spending >$100M on acquisition is: If the clickthrough rate (CTR) for an ad goes from 2% to 2.25%, our customer acquisition cost (CAC) decreases by ~ 30%.

We remodelled our system around this insight and built an AI-first 2.25% CTR ad manufacturing machine that works in every country.

For every new country, we take our hit shows -> use LLMs to extract and most intriguing moments -> Write a 5-min script combining all the best parts. The first 60 seconds has to have a hook every 5 seconds and needs to end with a crazy cliffhanger to force a download mid-scroll.

If a marketing video is not hitting our benchmarks (2.5% CTR and 55% 3-second through-play), a creative director gets involved to change the hook or cliffhanger to get the numbers there.

AI lets us make 1,000 Ads per show, and in total we do ~17.5k Ads per month. When a business creating scales from 1k to 10k Ads, the normal thing is for CAC to skyrocket. But with what I just shared, we 7-8x'd our User Acquisition budget without increasing our CAC materially.

It took us 8 months to figure this out, but then the timeline from 0-$10M in every country got shorter and shorter:

US revenue grew to $25M in 19 months. (US is now 78% of total)
Germany to 21M in 11 months.
France to $10M in 3 months.

2. Retention and Monetisation Hacks.

We knew we wanted to create an audio entertainment platform, but there was no standard format. For the first 2-3 years, we tried 10 different formats before landing on the winner.

After we got it right with audio drama (8-12min chapters, written for mobile fiction, serialized, episodes have strong hooks and end on cliffhangers), avg daily streaming time went from ~25mins to 150+.

Audio drama made us realize that Pocket FM was creating a whole new medium. There was no playbook for anything that we were doing. Everything had to be thought & built from scratch.
This is what we did for each major bullet:

- Monetization: Users have some free daily minutes to listen; then it's pay per episode. We also added the option for users to unlock episodes by watching ads, which is doing extremely well. Ads scaled from zero to a ~$90M run rate in 12 months.
- Engagement & production: You don't become obsessed with an app you open once a week. To have users engage daily, we make the next episode free every day. Also helps them build the habit.
- Discovery. Huge problem because people consuming Pocket FM enter the app, tap the show, and lock the screen. We fixed it by doing "playlists" of episodes. Once users's free minutes on a show are done, we ask users if they want to pay. If they say no, we play a new show, one where they haven't used their free mins. And we stitch episodes of different shows together that way, creating natural discovery.

3. Localization > translation.

78% of our revenue is still concentrated in the US. Localisation is fixing this: You might write a show for a Spanish audience where language, jokes, folklore, have a certain flavor; if you merely translate the show for, say, a Norwegian audience, that color is lost and hurts the show in the Norway. Listeners would relate more if the show was written by a Norwegian.

That's why, instead of translating, we localize shows to different regions. We use AI to take the spine of stories and adapt their whole cultural layer to the other country.

The results: a US show that was localized for a German audience had 50% higher retention than the translated version.

Localization + our user acquisition playbook led to:
- $10M+ ARR in France within 3 months
- $21M+ ARR in Germany within 11 months.

And this expands writers' addressable market.
We get messages of writers thrilled to have revenue coming from the US, India, EU, LatAm, without them doing much incremental work.

4. Experiment.

We give $1M checks to new internal initiatives, and the team has 12-18 months to prove their thesis. If they prove it, we double down.
This is how Pocket Saga came about, our AI video microdrama app. It's an AI video equivalent of Pocket FM. Same shows and structure, but as a vertical 2-min mobile video series.

We launched it two months ago and it's at ~$15M ARR.

Pocket's broader thesis is to help creators tell their stories to as many people as possible. Start with audio drama -> multiple languages or localize to diff countries -> microdrama -> more formats like movies, TV shows, and games.

Best of all is that writers get revenue streams not only from countries they wouldn't have tapped into, but also from formats they wouldn't have thought possible.

5. Pivoting to only AI content production unlocked 100% ARR growth.

In mid-2024, we pivoted to only AI content production.
Our growth took a very direct hit. Pocket was already at $200M ARR, growing 50% YoY, and we flatlined for a whole semester.

Six months later, the business exploded.

After the switch, we went from ~25k hours of content produced per year to over 2.5M hours, which are also higher quality. Because AI orchestrates the writing and more data is fed into it, more blockbusters come out.
Over 90 titles have $1M+ in lifetime earnings and 13 crossed $10M.

Quality control is done with LLM as a judge, and LLMs are quite tough.
Results are very encouraging:
- 12-month revenue retention went from 44% to 76%.
- In the past year, we added $250M in net new ARR.
__________________________________

Pocket Entertainment (Pocket FM + Pocket Saga) has become the largest AI entertainment platform.

We have the largest storytelling catalog with 770,000 titles, 550k creators, and 5.5B hours of playtime with minute by minute retention & engagement data.  We are using all of this data to improve every aspect of our business.

Our Bet: In the next 3 years we will be able to produce Naruto-like series for $1000.

Netflix has to spend $17B to find 100 Blockbusters per year.
What happens when you can produce 1M high quality shows for $1B?

The streaming wars caused a $300B reallocation of market cap.
The AI entertainment wars may be a $1T+ reshuffling of market cap.

We are at a unique spot. Unlike other AI creator tools like Runway or Midjourney, we own both the supply and demand side of AI content.

We've attracted over 550,000 writers who are producing an annualized 2.5M hours of content every year. This pairs with 137B+ minutes streamed.

Because of this, growth is accelerating as we scale further.
We have multiple S-curves inflecting at the same time:

- AI produces better and more ads -> scale faster in new countries
- ⁠Writers + AI produce more shows -> more blockbusters
- ⁠Localisation -> more reach per blockbuster
- ⁠Audio to video format expansion -> larger TAM -> more creators

Every aspect of our business is designed to improve another, and everything is aligned so that the number of blockbusters continues to rise.

If you're interested in building at the intersection of ai, tech and entertainment, DM me.

## 188. Shopify is moving from React Native to Native

Shopify is moving all of our mobile apps from React Native back to Swift and Kotlin.

Going all-in on React Native in 2020 was hugely successful for us. We saved a ton of time by building features once, enabled developers with no mobile background to contribute to our apps, and stopped constantly chasing feature parity.

Since then, LLMs have gotten dramatically better. They can use an iOS implementation to build the Android version and vice versa, help developers work outside their usual stack, and keep both platforms aligned through shared specs, tests, and review.

Native still means building and maintaining software on two platforms. That hasn’t gone away. But LLMs can now do enough of the implementation, translation, testing, and review that it’s no longer the deciding factor it was in 2020.

That has made greenfield rebuilds realistic. Shop went from proof of concept to a fully rebuilt native app in the app stores in 12 weeks. Other apps are also underway.

From the beginning, we wanted to contribute back to React Native and help make it better. We’re grateful for how the community embraced React Native Skia, FlashList, and Restyle, and we’re committed to transitioning them cleanly, with clear timelines and no surprises for the people who rely on them.

React Native was the right choice for us in 2020. Native is the right choice for us now. The goal hasn’t changed: build better apps and ship faster.

More details here: https://shopify.engineering/back-to-native

## 191. Thread by @mybitstar

[1/17] 有人靠「切视频」这门手艺，一单收 300，一天接 5 单就是 1500，工具还是免费开源的。

我刷 GitHub 时挖到的 short-video-generator-AI（刚上线几天就冲了 700+ star）

专干月费几百的付费切片工具的活，一分钱不花：

① 高光检测——LLM 把整段转写稿按「情绪爆点/观点炸弹/冲突」打分 0-100，自动挑最可能爆的片段

② 一键出片——裁成 9:16 竖版，本地 faster-whisper 自动加字幕，还能让 AI 生成开场钩子

③ 本地 Web UI——贴一排链接排队批量切，还带 API 能接进你自己的项目

直接 上手：
1. git clone  && pip install -r requirements.txt

2. .env 填 LLM key（Gemini 有免费档：）

3. python  "视频链接" --n 3 --ratio 9:16，成品在 output/ 文件夹

4. 想批量就 python3  开网页版，一次排一队

怎么变现：
给口播博主做切片代运营，一个号包月 2000-5000

把自己 2 小时的直播切成 20 条钩子短视频，发一条测一条，跑出爆款再投流

最骚的是它支持本地文件——你录的长视频自己切，剪辑师的钱全省了

需要避坑：
直接搬 YouTube 内容有版权风险，先拿自己录的或 CC 授权内容练手

本地转写吃性能，下载分辨率先用 720p，别贪 1080

切片号活下来的靠主页人设，不是数量，别当纯搬运工

做知识付费、带货直播的都能套——赛道换成你的，工具白嫖。

## 193. Thread by @LottiSchmitt

We deleted 200 blog posts end of last year and since then our Google traffic more than tripled.

Last year we published hundreds of AI-written SEO pages. Exactly zero hit Google's top 10.

This year we focused on quality. And search just became our #1 acquisition channel: 15% of new signups in spring, 20% in July, 25% in August, 36% (so far) in September.

Google traffic went from ~1K to nearly ~4K monthly visitors.

Here's the playbook we follow now:

1. Delete first.
We killed ~200 thin AI pages with low quality backlinks and kept a few dozen worth maintaining.

2. Same AI tools, better inputs.
The failed pages were written from models' imagination and little input. The new ones are written from data: live search volumes + SERPs from an SEO API (@ahrefs or @dataforseo), and verbatim quotes from real users on Reddit, Hacker News and X, every quote linked to its source using the @Octolens MCP.

3. Write the pages buyers search for.
Best-of lists that rank competitors honestly with up-to-date pricing breakdowns. Alternative pages for tools people are leaving.

4. Customer stories for recognition and original content.
Prisma, Vercel, Tally, PostHog, Render, Metabase. Original stories like these feed many other blogs and pages, which helps those rank too. Not to mention great content for socials.

5. Review everything.
People try to sell you automated SEO that just posts blogs on auto pilot and makes your domain rating grow. But from my experience its rather damaging then helpful to your SEO and brand image. Taking the time to read over content and edit it is (at least now) still necessary.

I recently open-sourced our blog writting process as a skill.

## 199. Thread by @MengTo

[1/14] Astra can create beautiful three.js UIs entirely in code.

You don't need an assets library or MCP to create detailed 3D models. These are all code and are easy to customize for your agent. Give Astra a video, URL, or code reference, then ask it to keep comparing the result against the reference and fixing mistakes.

Liquid Glass, shaders, motion design, procedural three.js models, and full environments. All from a few prompts.

Some prompts I used:

"Recreate this in a single HTML file with three.js. Keep comparing it against the reference and fix any differences."

"Add Liquid Glass buttons. On hover, animate the lighting around the borders and make the background gradients move slightly with the pointer. Add subtle particles."

"Improve the lighting, edges, and modeling. Give the keys more depth and verify the result against the real MacBook."

## 202. Thread by @igus_ai

[1/11] Metí 250$.

Este bot los convirtió en +13.000$.

Y ni siquiera lo programé yo.

Cogí un proyecto open source de GitHub.

Le dediqué un par de horas en Claude Code para limpiarlo, entender cómo funcionaba y hacer algunos ajustes.

Le di a ejecutar.

Pensaba que iba a fundir la cuenta en menos de una hora.

Minimicé la terminal y seguí con mi día.

Cuando volví por la noche, ya no eran 250$.

Eran +600$.

Desde ese momento, simplemente dejo los logs abiertos en el segundo monitor y veo cómo ejecuta operación tras operación.

La clave está en el timing.

El bot espera hasta los últimos dos minutos de cada ronda de 5 minutos de BTC.

Para entonces, Bitcoin ya ha hecho gran parte del movimiento y la dirección suele estar bastante definida.

En ese momento entra a favor de la tendencia, comprando contratos que cotizan entre 0,80$ y 0,99$.

Unos minutos después, si el movimiento se mantiene, la posición se liquida a 1$.

La lógica es sorprendentemente simple:

→ Esperar hasta que falten unos 2 minutos para el cierre de la ronda
→ Comprobar que BTC ya se ha movido entre 70$ y 100$ durante ese intervalo
→ Operar siempre a favor del movimiento, nunca en contra
→ Cubrir una pequeña parte de la posición únicamente si el mercado se desequilibra demasiado (por ejemplo, 95/5)

Es como comprar un boleto de lotería cuando prácticamente ya han anunciado el resultado.

El código es público y cualquiera puede usarlo en su pc:



Gratis.

El bot fue creado siguiendo el tutorial de abajo, échale un ojo si quieres crear el tuyo👇

## 208. How to Tell Every Local Business in a State Exactly Who's Beating Them on Google (At Scale)

Most SEO agency outbound sounds like every other cold email.

"I noticed your website could use some improvements." 

"We help businesses like yours grow their online presence." 

"Thought this might be relevant."

None of it is real. None of it earns a reply.

To be clear, the problem is not the copy. The problem is that there is no actual evidence behind it. The sender does not know where the prospect ranks. They do not know who is beating them. They are guessing, reaching, and the prospect can feel it.

We built a different approach for a client who sells SEO and paid search to law firms.

The pitch is incredibly specific: we know which firms are showing up when someone searches for a lawyer in your city. We know you are not one of them. We know roughly how much traffic that costs you. And we have already built the competitive breakdown.

Here is how it works.

The architecture

Three scripts. One Railway container. One persistent volume. The container runs until the state is done, then exits cleanly.

The file structure

Stage 1: Harvest

harvest_maps.py queries the DataForSEO Google Maps API across city and practice area grids.

39 practice queries. Every major city in the state. The scraper keeps only positions 4 through 10.

That cutoff matters. The top 3 are already winning. They are not prospects. The firms on page 2 and beyond have traffic to recover. That is the pitch.

For North Carolina alone: 60 cities × 39 queries × up to 7 results per map pack = roughly 16,000 firms in the harvest before deduplication. Most fall out at the next stage. That is fine. The funnel is wide by design.

Stage 2: Fit check

For each firm in the harvest, the pipeline fetches the website via Jina Reader and passes it to a cheap LLM.

The first check is not about ranking. It is about fit. Personal injury only? BigLaw? Out-of-territory? These get dropped before any expensive SERP call is made.

The LLM returns a practice area and the exact search query to use. That query is what gets fired in the next step.

Stage 3: Live SERP pull

This is where the engine earns its specificity.

For every firm that passes the fit check, the pipeline runs two live Serper calls:

1. Google Maps places for that practice + city
2. Google organic search for that practice + city

The LLM then analyzes both results and returns a structured research block.

The output is a KV block that looks like this:

Two segments matter: RANKING_GAP (firm is not visible anywhere) and DIRECTORY_DEPENDENT (firm shows up only in directories, not on its own site). Everything else gets dropped.

Stage 4: Exact position measurement

Before copy is written, the pipeline runs one more Serper organic call to measure the firm's exact position in the first 100 results.

That position feeds a traffic loss calculation.

The output is a concrete evidence block used in copy generation.

That block is deterministic. The position is measured, not estimated. The percentage comes from public organic CTR data. The names come from the live SERP.

Stage 5: Copy generation

Two emails per contact. Claude Sonnet 4.6 via @concentrateai.

Email 1 is under 80 words. It names the firms beating them, states their exact position, gives the traffic loss percentage, and offers to share the competitive breakdown.

Email 2 is the follow-up. It opens with a locked sentence: "I reached out the other day but likely did not do the best job explaining how this works." It references a proof point from a real client. It proposes a next step. Under 850 characters.

Both emails get run through a deterministic validator before they are accepted.

The validator also checks word count, em dashes (stripped automatically), locked openers, locked CTA phrasing, and whether the evidence block is present. Three regeneration attempts per email. Failures get injected back into the next prompt with the specific violation listed.

Stage 6: Push

Verified leads push to EmailBison campaigns named ATB {STATE} Prospects - QE.

rolling_push.py runs every 30 minutes inside the container on a background loop. It reads the prospect CSV from the Railway volume, runs the QA gate one final time, and pushes in batches. On a 422 duplicate error it recovers the lead by ID rather than re-creating it.

The state of every push is written to rolling_state.json. If the container restarts, it picks up exactly where it left off.

The A/B test built in

Half the firms get the standard SEO angle. The other half get an AEO angle: how the firm appears in AI search results — ChatGPT, Perplexity, Google AI Overviews.

Same SERP evidence. Different frame. Same deterministic QA on both paths. The reply data from EmailBison tells you which angle is winning at scale.

The deploy

Environment variables:

Set restart policy to NEVER. Mount a Railway volume at /data for persistence across deploys.

What makes this different

The email your prospect receives contains:
- The names of the firms ranking above them
- Their exact position in the first 100 Google results
- A specific percentage of searches they are missing
- A mention of assets they already have that should be earning more

None of that was written by a human. All of it came from a live SERP pull run minutes before the email was generated.

That is the gap between a signal-based outbound engine and a list-and-blast.

Where this goes

The current setup runs state by state. Once all target states are covered the cycle resets: 90 days later the same markets get re-run and a new cohort of firms that have not been contacted falls into the harvest.

The next layer is AEO at full scale. AI search is changing what "page 1" means. The firms that appear in ChatGPT and Perplexity answers are a different list than the ones ranking organically. A new signal type. A new pitch. The same infrastructure handles it with a prompt swap and an additional Serper call.

The insight compounds. The code stays the same.

Look: photoreal cinema, slight filmic grain, mild halation on highlights, teal-cyan skies, punchy crimson reds, deep forest greens, warm skin, crushed-but-not-crushed blacks.

Lens language: 35mm and 50mm for portraits, 24mm for landscapes and stairs, occasional 85mm compression on faces. Shallow DOF on close-ups, deep focus on mountain wides.
Shutter: 180-degree cinematic, natural motion blur on hair and fabric, no soap-opera 60fps look. 24fps. No CGI look, no plastic skin, no beauty-filter smoothing, no extra fingers, no warped architecture.
Do not invent logos other than the Adidas trefoil already on the garments. Do not add watermarks or random UI.

[SCENE]
A beautiful woman @image1 in her early 20s spends a sunlit day moving through China - first a Soviet-era hotel park, then a Taoist wishing platform hung with red plaques, then the misted quartz-sandstone pillars of Zhangjiajie - always in Adidas, always wearing white wired earphones, always slightly smiling like the song in her ears is the whole world.

[CHARACTERS]
LEAD - lock identity for the entire clip:
Young woman @image1, early 20s, long straight black hair, heavy winged black eyeliner, long lashes, glossy nude-rose lips, slim athletic build, long manicured nails (white/nude). No glasses. Expression default: soft closed-mouth smile that opens into a real laugh. She never speaks. White wired earphones in both ears in every shot she appears, cord visible against the jacket. 

FRIEND (appears only 21s–30s):
Young East-Asian woman, early 20s, long dark hair, rounder softer face, wearing a loose mint/light-teal oversized t-shirt and black wide trousers. Same joyful energy. No earphones.

BACKGROUND PEOPLE: real Chinese tourists and workers, mixed ages, natural clothing, never looking at camera, never frozen, never identical twins.

[WARDROBE - three looks, change only at the stated cuts]
LOOK A (0–7s): crimson-red Adidas firebird track jacket, high collar zipped, white shoulder stripes, white wired earphones, holding a peach/rose gold iPhone in a dark case.
LOOK B (7–16s): dark forest-green Adidas cropped jersey with white 3-stripes on sleeves + white trefoil, white crop undershirt peek, baggy mint-teal track pants with side stripe, thick silver chain with small gold star pendant, stacked gold bangles, small white structured handbag, midriff visible.
LOOK C (16–30s): black Adidas track jacket, white 3-stripes, white trefoil on left chest, black pants, silver studded belt, same earrings. Hair can be slightly wind-tossed.

[LOCATIONS - use these exact environments, do not generic-swap them]
LOC 1 City park: tall beige Soviet-modern hotel tower with many balconies, blue sky with stacked white clouds, green trees, a small glass greenhouse roof in the lower corner.
LOC 2 Temple / Wishing Platform: dark timber pavilion, signboard 「祈愿台 / Wishing Platform」, dense hanging red wooden prayer plaques with gold tassels, stone incense burner, carved stone pillar, red lanterns, lush green hillside behind.
LOC 3 Stone stairway packed with tourists, yellow monumental type overlaid later.
LOC 4 Close incense: three thick red-and-gold ceremonial incense sticks planted in ash, Chinese blessing characters in gold, thin smoke.
LOC 5 Traditional multi-eave temple complex against a steep green mountain, tiled roofs, circular window, plaza paving.
LOC 6 Wooden rope-and-post bridge over green water, palace roofs behind.
LOC 7 Cliff viewpoint: rough log railing, sheer green canyon dropping away.
LOC 8 Classic Zhangjiajie sea-of-clouds: tall quartzite pillars rising through white mist.
LOC 9 Forest path: two bamboo-and-wicker sedan chairs (huagan) carried on shoulder poles by four working men, tourists walking the same paved trail under dense canopy.
LOC 10 Turtle release pond: wooden sign 「千年放生池」, stone turtles in shallow water, small fountain jet.
LOC 11 Forest floor: one wild macaque sitting on a mossy rock, looking at camera, still.
LOC 12 Mountain-gate plaza: grey stone paving, rows of red vertical banners, cable-car cabins in the far green slope, a single pillar peak in mist behind tiled roofs.

[CONSISTENCY LOCK]
Same lead face, hairline, makeup, ear shape, and nail style in every shot.
Earphones stay white wired earbuds, cord always present.
Adidas stripes stay physically correct - three stripes, not two, not four.
Architecture stays Chinese traditional or Zhangjiajie geology - no Japanese torii, no generic Bali, no Alps.
Crowd density stays naturalistic, never empty theme-park, never stadium crush.
No readable random Chinese text except the specified signs and the two English title cards.

[FIRST FRAME]
Medium shot, lead in LOOK A, standing in LOC 1 park. Hotel tower rises out-of-focus behind her right shoulder. She looks down at the phone held in both hands. Soft overcast-blue daylight. Hair still. End state of this beat: she is still looking at the phone.

[TIMELINE — 30 seconds, hard cuts on movement, one job per beat]

0.0–2.4s  SHOT 1 - OPEN
Medium, slightly low, 35mm. LOC 1. Lead in red jacket, head tilted down, thumbs moving on the peach iPhone. Hotel tower and clouds hold the top third. Gentle handheld drift, almost still. Hair and jacket fabric have real weight. Hard cut on her thumb tap.

2.4–4.2s  SHOT 2 — PHONE INSERT
High-angle ECU of the phone in her hands (long white nails). Music-player UI on a dark lock-screen-style player, play/pause visible, white earphone jack in the bottom. Soft daylight wrap. Tiny finger adjustment. Hard cut.

4.2–7.0s  SHOT 3 — EARPHONES IN
Medium close-up, 50mm, LOC 1 trees now a creamy green bokeh. She lifts her right hand and seats the white earbud, then drops the hand. Eyes half-closed, a private smile starts. Very slow push-in. End state: she looks down, smiling, both buds in. Hard cut.

7.0–9.2s  SHOT 4 — TEMPLE PORTRAIT
Medium close-up. Instant wardrobe snap to LOOK B (green jersey, star necklace). LOC 2 wishing wall of red plaques behind her, warm late-afternoon side light. She looks into camera with a small knowing smile, then the smile widens. Soft handheld. Hard cut.

9.2–11.4s  SHOT 5 — STAIRS + TITLE
Wide, 24mm, low on a long stone stairway crowded with tourists. Lead in LOOK B, mid-stairs, one arm raised, one knee lifted in a playful step, white bag in the down hand. Giant bold yellow sans-serif type slams on: 【CHINA】 filling the frame around her, letters sharp, no drop shadow crawl. She holds the pose inside the type. Hard cut.

11.4–13.2s  SHOT 6 — HAIR WHIP / LAUGH
Medium, tracking slightly with her as she spins. LOC 2 hillside, red plaques at frame left. LOOK B, midriff visible. Hair fans in a real-physics arc. Big open-mouth laugh, chin up, looking off toward the peaks. Hard cut.

13.2–14.6s  SHOT 7 — INCENSE ECU
Macro-ish close of three ceremonial incense sticks in ash — red body, gold characters, grey burned tips, a thread of smoke. Shallow DOF, green bokeh. Almost still, one breath of smoke. Hard cut.

14.6–16.2s  SHOT 8 — WISHING PLATFORM
Medium walking shot. Lead in LOOK B approaches the timber Wishing Platform, laughing, red plaques and warm interior lamps behind. A tourist in a hat crosses behind her left. Handheld follow at chest height. Hard cut.

16.2–18.0s  SHOT 9 — BRIDGE POSE
Medium-wide. LOOK C begins here (black track jacket). She leans on a rope-and-wood bridge rail, torso twisted back toward camera, white bag on the shoulder, mountains and palace roofs soft behind. Confident look-to-camera. Gentle breeze in hair. Hard cut.

18.0–19.6s  SHOT 10 — CLIFF BACK
Over-shoulder / behind, 24mm. She stands at a log railing looking into a deep green canyon. We see only the back of the black jacket and falling hair. She lifts one hand to the rail. End state: she is still facing the drop. Hard cut.

19.6–21.4s  SHOT 11 — PILLAR PORTRAIT
Medium, she now sits / leans on the same log rail but facing us, LOOK C, Zhangjiajie pillars and sea of clouds stacked behind her, smile open. Soft overcast mountain light, cool mist in the valleys. Tiny handheld breathe. Hard cut.

21.4–23.2s  SHOT 12 — SEDAN CHAIRS
Wide tracking from behind on a forest road. Two huagan sedan chairs being carried uphill by four men, a woman in a red hooded top in the left chair. Other hikers ahead in the misty green tunnel of trees. Camera follows at walking pace. Hard cut.

23.2–24.6s  SHOT 13 — SEA OF CLOUDS
Locked-off-feeling wide of quartzite pillars punching through thick white cloud, pine tips in the lower left. Slow almost-imperceptible drift. No people. Hard cut.

24.6–26.2s  SHOT 14 — TWO GIRLS WALK
Medium two-shot, walking toward camera then glancing at each other. Lead in LOOK C + Friend in mint tee, both laughing, stone path, blue umbrellas and timber shopfronts behind. Handheld walk-and-talk energy, no dialogue. Hard cut.

26.2–27.4s  SHOT 15 — TURTLES / MONKEY INSERT (split beat)
0.6s ECU fountain and stone turtles in the release pond under the wooden sign, then 0.6s a single macaque sitting on moss looking into lens. Natural forest light. Hard cut.

27.4–30.0s  SHOT 16 — PLAZA DANCE + TITLE
Wide-to-medium in LOC 12 plaza. Lead in LOOK C and Friend dance — she throws both arms up, jacket sleeves flying, friend grabs her own hair and laughs. Red banners and a misted pillar peak hold the background, tiny cable cars on the slope. At 28.2s giant yellow type hits: 【NEVER SAY】 then on the next step 【NEVER】 stacking over them, same yellow block gothic as the CHINA card. Camera handheld, slightly low, pushing a half-step closer. Final frame: she is mid-laugh, arms high, type still on. Cut to black on the last frame. No fade.

[CAMERA / OPTICS]
Portraits: 50mm, eye-level or a touch below, subject left or center-weighted, background melts.
Landscapes: 24mm, horizon in the upper third, real atmospheric perspective.
Inserts: 85–100mm compression.
Movement vocabulary only: gentle handheld breathe, slow push-in, walking follow, one hair-spin track. No drone orbit unless the sea-of-clouds shot. No crash zooms. No Dutch angles except a 2-degree handheld accident.
Every cut lands on a gesture (tap, bud-in, spin, step, arm-throw), never on a frozen mannequin.

[PHYSICS]
Hair has real mass — delayed follow-through on the spin, individual strands, no helmet-hair.
Tracksuit nylon wrinkles and catches wind.
Incense smoke rises in thin broken threads, not CGI fog slabs.
Cloud around pillars is volumetric and slow.
Sedan-chair poles flex a little under weight.
Jacket sleeves lag a frame when she throws her arms up.

[LIGHTING]
City park: bright overcast-blue, soft top light, no hard noon shadows.
Temple: warm practical red lamps mixed with late-day sun from camera left.
Mountains: cooler, bigger, mist-filled; faces get a soft white bounce from cloud.
Plaza: open daylight, slight overcast, colors still saturated.
Skin always has a real highlight on the cheekbone and a tiny catchlight in both eyes.

[ON-SCREEN TEXT — only these two, exact]
9.2–11.4s: 【CHINA】 — huge mustard-yellow condensed sans, centered, letters occupy most of the frame, the girl dances inside the negative space of the letters.
28.2–30.0s: 【NEVER SAY NEVER】 — same yellow condensed sans, stacked two or three lines, over the dancing pair. No other captions, no lyrics, no location lower-thirds, no subtitles.

[AUDIO]
<soft thumb taps on glass at 2.4s>
<earbud click at 4.6s>
<distant temple crowd murmur, wooden wind chime, thin incense crackle 7–16s>
<open mountain wind, far cable-car hum 16–24s>
<sedan-chair pole creak, footsteps on wet stone 21–23s>
<water jet, turtle splash 26.2s>
<plaza footsteps, cloth rustle, a short laugh that is not dialogue 27.4–30s>
No spoken lines. No voiceover. No language other than the two English title cards.

[MAINTAIN CONSISTENCY]
One lead. One friend after 21s. Three outfits only, changing only at the cuts named. White wired earphones on the lead in every shot she is in. Zhangjiajie geology + southern-China temple architecture only. Yellow typeface identical on both cards. Photoreal, no stylized 3D, no anime, no beauty-app skin.

## 226. Thread by @alexgroberman

I'm a little confused...

Why isn't everyone isn't taking advantage of the easiest way to get traffic + sales from Google and ChatGPT in 2026?

It works regardless of industry, but for whatever reason it hasn't caught on yet.

So many B2B companies mess up one of the most important sections of their site.

They treat their blog and article categories like a place for announcements and recycled press releases, or they spam it with garbage un-related to their ICP in any meaningful way.

That completely ignores the fact that vendor blogs have literally become some of the most cited sources in ChatGPT, Gemini, Perplexity and Google AI Overviews.

And we have clear proof of this that anyone can verify.

(If you want to see where your site stands across Google, ChatGPT, Claude and broader AI search, start here - it's totally free:

)

Search “New Relic alternatives” on Google.

The AI Overview cites SigNoz, and the source is SigNoz’s own blog.

You can try this search several different times across several different sessions and you will get similar results.

SigNoz gets cited via its own blog.

AppSignal’s blog is cited later in the same Overview for them.

Honeycomb’s blog appears as well, same query, same structure, cited as its own source.

ChatGPT mirrors this pattern.

Ask “What is the best New Relic alternative?” and SigNoz appears again as the cited source.

Rankscale, which I have zero affiliation with, recently analyzed thousands of commercial queries such as:

“Best CRM tools for small business.”

“Best online course platforms.”

They tested these across ChatGPT, Gemini, Perplexity and AI Overviews.

The findings were pretty clear.

A large portion of citations did not come from TechCrunch, G2, Capterra or major publishers.

They came directly from company blogs.

Thinkific.

LearnWorlds.

Monday.

Pipedrive.

HP.

All cited inside AI answers using their own blog content. (Not guest posts or PR placements, literally their own blogs.)

And to be clear, again, I have no affiliation with Rankscale.

The study stood out because it matches exactly what we have been seeing internally with SEO Stuff ().

It also aligns with what recent studies from Ahrefs and at least one other notable company (I forget which) found on the importance of blog posts for AI visibility.

Here is why vendor blogs work so well:

AI engines need structured, factual text.

Vendor blogs often have clean HTML, definitions, lists, comparisons, etc that are easy to extract.

There is also a massive content gap.

Third-party publishers do not produce detailed, CREDIBLE comparison content for every niche. (Google is detecting spam increasingly well, as this current update is proving.)

So AI models pull from whoever does because they care about clarity, structure and recency, and they are terrible at weeding out bias.

If your post is titled “7 Best Monitoring Tools for 2026 Ranked by Cost and Features” and it is well structured, AI sees it as useful and extracts it.

Freshness matters as well.

Gemini, ChatGPT and AI Overviews weigh recency heavily, and vendor blogs update far more often than large media outlets.

So if you are in SaaS, tech or any B2B vertical, your blog is crucial.

It is your entry point into AI search visibility.

Every best of or comparison post trains AI systems to associate your brand with your category.

You are teaching the next generation of search engines to trust you.

Brands already doing this, like SigNoz, AppSignal and Honeycomb, are now being cited as their own sources.

The brands that start now will own their category in 6 to 12 months.

The ones who wait will be buried under competitor content that is already indexed, cited and reinforced.

This shift is exactly what SEO Stuff () was built around.

The done-for-you package:

10 long-form, snippet-optimized pieces of content plus 3 DR50+ backlinks from websites already appearing in AI search.

Each piece is built for high-intent best and top searches that AI engines reuse most often.

Every article includes AI-readable formatting like clean HTML, proper sections, question-based H2s and TLDR blocks.



SEO Stuff Premium Content Bundle

60 optimized pieces of content built in the exact format Rankscale found being cited across ChatGPT and Gemini.

Each article is structured for both Google and AI Overview extraction, with clean data blocks and internal linking.



Together, these two plans:

Turn your vendor blog into an AI citation engine.

Build authority that compounds across Google and ChatGPT.

Generate traffic that converts from both traditional and AI-driven search.

The game now is teaching AI systems to see your brand as the authority.

The brands already doing this quietly through their blogs are the ones being cited, summarized and surfaced across every AI search engine.
