## 321. I Got 8 Interviews in One Week

The exact system I used to beat the ATS and land callbacks

Let me tell you about the worst feeling in job hunting: sending out application after application into what feels like a black hole. You spend hours perfecting your CV. You write thoughtful cover letters. You hit submit. And then... nothing. Silence. Maybe an automated rejection email three weeks later if you're lucky.

I know that feeling because I lived it. For months, I was stuck in the same loop as everyone else, blasting out the same CV to dozens of companies, wondering what I was doing wrong.

Then I changed my approach completely. And in a single week, I landed 8 interviews.

This isn't some vague motivational story about "believing in yourself." I'm going to walk you through exactly what I did differently, the specific tactics that work against modern Applicant Tracking Systems like Greenhouse, Workable, and Lever. These are the strategies that turned radio silence into a calendar full of interviews.

The Uncomfortable Truth About Your CV

Here's the thing nobody wants to hear: your CV probably isn't about you. Or at least, it shouldn't be.

I used to think a CV was my chance to showcase everything I'd accomplished. All my skills, all my experience, all neatly packaged into two pages of professional pride. Turns out, that's exactly backwards.

Your CV is not a summary of your career. It's a proof document showing you match what they're looking for.

The moment I internalized this, everything changed. I stopped asking "how do I present myself?" and started asking "what do they need, and how do I prove I have it?"

One CV Per Application (Yes, Really)

I never sent the same CV twice. Every single application got a freshly tailored CV optimized specifically for that job description.

I know what you're thinking: "That sounds exhausting." It is. It's also the single biggest factor in whether you get callbacks or crickets.

Here's why this matters so much: ATS systems score your CV based on keyword matching against the job description. When you send a generic CV, you're typically hitting maybe 30-40% of the keywords they're scanning for. That puts you in the reject pile before a human ever sees your name.

A tailored CV can hit 85% or higher. That's not a marginal improvement, that's the difference between your application getting flagged for review or getting automatically filtered out.

Even roles that sound similar use different language. One company's "Data Analyst" job description emphasizes SQL and Python. Another emphasizes stakeholder communication and business insights. Same job title, completely different keyword profiles. Your generic CV might nail one and completely miss the other.

The Copy-Paste Strategy That Feels Like Cheating

This one felt almost too simple to work, but it's incredibly effective: I copied exact phrases from the job description into my CV.

Not paraphrased. Not cleverly reworded. The exact words.

If a job description says "Strong SQL skills, with the ability to query, join, and manipulate large and complex datasets" - that's exactly what my CV says. Word for word.

This isn't lazy, and it's not dishonest (assuming you actually have those skills). It's strategic. ATS systems are pattern-matching machines. They're looking for exact keyword matches. When you get creative with synonyms or rephrase things in your own words, you're essentially gambling that the system will recognize your version as equivalent. Often, it doesn't.

And here's the thing about recruiters: they wrote that job description. When they skim your CV and they are absolutely skimming, not reading they're unconsciously looking for the familiar phrases from their own posting. Give them exactly what they expect to see.

Mirror Their Structure

Many job descriptions organize their requirements into clear categories. Maybe they have sections like "What You'll Do" with specific responsibilities, or headers like "Data Strategy & Leadership" and "Analytics & Insight Generation."

When I saw that structure, I used those exact same headers in my experience section.

Instead of organizing my CV chronologically or by generic categories, I'd create sections that perfectly matched their framework. If they care about "Data Governance & Quality," I had a section called "Data Governance & Quality" with relevant bullets underneath.

This does two things. First, it makes the ATS happy because you're hitting all their structural keywords. Second, and maybe more importantly - it makes the human reviewer's job incredibly easy. They can instantly see that you match their requirements because your CV is literally organized around their requirements.

The Role Title Trick

This one might feel a little uncomfortable at first, but hear me out.

I changed my job title on my CV to match the role I was applying for as long as it was reasonably accurate to what I actually did.

Applying for "Data Governance Specialist"? My CV title became "Data Governance Specialist." Applying for "Analytics Lead, Operations"? That's what my CV said.

Why this works: ATS systems often score exact role title matches very highly. It's one of the first things they check. And when a recruiter glances at your CV, seeing their exact job title at the top creates instant alignment.

Important caveat: only do this if your actual responsibilities genuinely match. Don't claim to be a "Chief Data Officer" if you were a junior analyst. The goal is to translate your experience into their language, not to fabricate credentials you don't have.

Why Your Beautiful CV Template Is Hurting You

I used to have a gorgeous CV. Clean design, nice colors, professional-looking icons, two-column layout. I was proud of it.

It was also completely invisible to most ATS systems.

Here's the brutal reality: those fancy CV templates from Canva or wherever? Many ATS systems can't parse them properly. Tables, columns, text boxes, graphics all of these can confuse the automated systems that screen your application before any human sees it.

The formatting rules are boring but essential:

• File format: .docx, not PDF (unless they specifically request PDF). Many systems parse Word documents more reliably.

• Layout: Single column only. No tables, no graphics, no text boxes.

• Fonts: Calibri, Arial, or Times New Roman. Nothing creative.

• Bullets: Standard bullet points only. No custom symbols or icons.

• Headers: Clear section headers like PROFESSIONAL EXPERIENCE, EDUCATION, SKILLS.

• Avoid: Headers/footers (often unreadable), images, logos, photos, infographics.

I know it's not sexy. Your CV will look plain compared to those designer templates. But a plain CV that gets read beats a beautiful CV that gets automatically rejected.

Numbers Are Your Secret Weapon

"Improved team efficiency" is forgettable. "Reduced reporting time by 30%" is memorable.

I made sure every major accomplishment on my CV had a number attached to it. Percentages. Dollar amounts. Time saved. Records processed. Teams managed. Projects completed.

Some examples that worked for me:

• "Achieved 3,050% ROI on strategic investments"

• "25% increase in forecast accuracy"

• "Managed datasets exceeding 10 million records"

• "Implemented across 15 departments"

• "Delivered 12-month roadmap on schedule and under budget"

Even if you have to estimate, having specific numbers is dramatically more compelling than vague claims. "Improved efficiency" could mean anything. "30% reduction in processing time" tells a concrete story.

Speaking Their Language (Literally)

Many companies list their values right in the job description. When I saw that, I didn't just note it I created a dedicated section in my CV that explicitly aligned with those values.

For example, one company listed their values as Bold, Credible, Human, and Together. So I added a "Values Alignment" section with bullets like:

• Bold: Act with ambition and determination in driving data transformation

• Credible: Act with rigour in implementing data quality standards

• Human: Act to have positive impact through training and stakeholder support

• Together: Act collaboratively with data owners and cross-functional teams

Is this over the top? Maybe. But it sends a powerful signal: "I didn't just skim your job posting. I actually read it, understood your culture, and took time to show you I'm a fit." That's rare, and recruiters notice it.

Cover Letters Still Matter (Here's the Formula)

I wrote a cover letter for every application that had a field for it. Even when it said "optional."

"Optional" is a test. Companies want to see who cares enough to do the extra work. When you skip the optional cover letter, you're telling them you're doing the bare minimum.

Here's the structure that worked for me:

Paragraph 1 — The Hook: Open with genuine enthusiasm for this specific company. Mention something concrete about them, their mission, a recent product, a challenge they're facing. Don't be generic. "I'm excited to apply for this role" is worthless. "I've been following your work on [specific project] and was impressed by [specific thing]" shows you actually did your homework.

Paragraph 2 — Technical Match: List your relevant skills using their exact terminology. Keep it natural but keyword-rich. This is another opportunity to hit those ATS keywords.

Paragraph 3 — The Story: Give a brief example of relevant work you've done. Use numbers. "I built dashboards that reduced reporting time by 30%" beats "I have dashboard experience."

Paragraph 4 — Why This Role: Explain what specifically excites you about this position—not just any position, this one. Reference something from their job description.

Paragraph 5 — Close: Confirm practical details (location, availability) and express interest in discussing further. Keep it short.

One more thing: sound like a human. Use contractions. Skip phrases like "I am writing to express my interest in..." everyone writes that, and it's instantly forgettable. Keep it under 400 words. Respect their time.

Speed Matters More Than You Think

I applied to jobs as quickly as possible after they were posted. Many of the roles where I got interviews had been posted just hours earlier.

This isn't about desperation, it's about understanding how the hiring process actually works.

Most companies review applications on a rolling basis. The first qualified candidates often get priority. Some roles close early when they find strong applicants. And let's be honest: recruiter fatigue is real. After reviewing 200 applications, even great candidates start to blur together.

Being early means your application gets seen when attention is highest and competition is lowest.

Set up job alerts. Check them daily. When you see a good match, prioritize it. Yes, this means the tailored CV approach takes real effort but applying thoughtfully to 10 jobs beats blasting a generic CV to 100.

What I Didn't Do

Sometimes knowing what to avoid is as important as knowing what to do.

I didn't use a generic "one size fits all" CV. I didn't use fancy templates with graphics and columns. I didn't skip the cover letter when it was optional. I didn't use vague statements without numbers. I didn't apply to roles I wasn't genuinely interested in you can always tell when someone's just going through the motions. I didn't copy-paste the same cover letter for every application. And I didn't wait days to apply after seeing a good job.

Each of these mistakes is something I see job seekers do constantly. Each one chips away at your chances.

The Checklist I Used Before Every Application

Before hitting submit on any application, I ran through this mental checklist:

1. Does my CV title match or closely align with their job title?

2. Have I included verbatim keywords from their job description?

3. Does my experience section mirror the structure of their requirements?

4. Have I included quantifiable achievements with actual numbers?

5. Is my CV in ATS-friendly format (.docx, single column, standard fonts)?

6. Have I addressed any location or logistics concerns proactively?

7. Does my cover letter show genuine interest in this specific company?

8. Have I used their exact technical terminology throughout?

9. Have I aligned with their stated values (if they listed them)?

10. Am I applying quickly, ideally within hours of the posting?

If I couldn't check every box, I went back and fixed what was missing before submitting.

The Bottom Line

This approach takes more time per application. I won't pretend otherwise. While other people were blasting out 20 generic applications a day, I was spending real time tailoring each one.

But the results speak for themselves: 8 interviews from one week of focused applications.

The job market is tough right now. I'm not going to sugarcoat that. But it's not random. It's a system and systems can be understood, optimized, and beaten.

When you stop competing with hundreds of generic applicants and start positioning yourself as someone who clearly understands what the company needs, everything changes. You're no longer hoping to get lucky. You're engineering your own luck.

More here: https://drive.google.com/drive/folders/1xTpKHnH9j_5ELRIG3rW3IFriYh_1ZwfE?usp=drive_link

Good luck with your applications. You've got this.

— Splendor

January 2026

## 338. Thread by @Alvin1492840

[1/13] A guy bought a $1,500 Samsung TV 3 years ago.

He watched Netflix. He watched YouTube. He thought the picture looked fine. He assumed that's just what a TV looks like.

His friend, a home theater installer who calibrates TVs for a living, walked into his apartment and looked at the screen for 5 seconds.

"You're watching everything in demo mode. The motion smoothing is on. The eco dimmer is cutting your brightness by 40%. Your TV is taking a screenshot of your screen every 30 seconds and selling your viewing data to advertisers. And you're watching a $1,500 panel in the same picture mode Best Buy uses under fluorescent lights to make TVs pop on a showroom wall."
He changed 9 settings in 12 minutes.

The picture looked like a different television. The soap opera effect disappeared. The colors became natural. The TV stopped spying on him.
Here's every setting he changed 🧵

[2/13] First what your smart TV is actually doing out of the box.

Every major TV manufacturer — Samsung, LG, Sony, Vizio, TCL, Hisense — ships their televisions with factory settings designed for one purpose: looking as bright and eye-catching as possible on a showroom floor under fluorescent lights next to 40 other TVs.

That means oversaturated colors, artificially boosted brightness, motion interpolation that turns 24fps cinema into 60fps video, and sharpening filters that add digital noise instead of actual detail. These settings make the TV pop at Best Buy. They make everything look like a soap opera in your living room.

On top of that, every smart TV ships with a tracking system called ACR — Automatic Content Recognition — enabled by default. It fingerprints whatever is on your screen every 15 to 60 seconds and sends that data to advertising partners. Samsung settled a lawsuit with Texas over this in February 2026. The other brands are still fighting theirs.

His friend had calibrated over 3,000 TVs in people's homes. He said every single one arrived in the same condition: factory demo mode, tracking enabled, picture quality sabotaged by settings nobody asked for.
Here are the 9 things he fixed.

[3/13] Fix 1: He switched the picture mode from Vivid to Movie.

This was the single biggest change. One menu. One selection. The entire image transformed.
Every TV ships in "Vivid" or "Dynamic" mode. These modes crank saturation, boost contrast, and push brightness to maximum so the panel screams on a showroom shelf. Skin tones turn orange. Blue skies look radioactive. White walls glow. The picture looks impressive for 5 seconds and exhausting for 5 hours.

He switched it to "Movie" mode. On Samsung, it's Settings → Picture → Picture Mode → Movie. On LG, it's the same path. On Sony, it's called "Cinema." Every brand has an equivalent.

Movie mode dials everything back to the color temperature and brightness the content was actually mastered in. Skin tones look like skin. Shadows have detail instead of crushed blacks. Highlights stop clipping into white blobs.

He said most people have owned their TV for years in Vivid mode and never realized they've been watching a showroom demo in their bedroom. Movie mode is what the director intended you to see. It was one tap away for 3 years.

[4/13] Fix 2: He turned off motion smoothing.

Samsung calls it Auto Motion Plus. LG calls it TruMotion. Sony calls it MotionFlow. Vizio calls it Smooth Motion Effect. Every brand has a different name for the same feature that does the same terrible thing.

Motion smoothing takes content shot at 24 frames per second — which is every movie and most prestige television — and interpolates artificial frames to make it look like 60fps. The result is what the industry calls the "soap opera effect." Movies look like daytime television. Cinematic scenes look like behind-the-scenes footage. The Dark Knight looks like it was shot on a home camcorder.

Tom Cruise recorded a video asking people to turn it off. Christopher Nolan publicly criticized it for years. The entire Filmmaker Mode standard was invented by the UHD Alliance specifically because manufacturers refused to ship TVs with this setting disabled.

His friend went to Settings → Picture → Expert Settings → Auto Motion Plus and set it to Off. On LG, it's Settings → Picture → Advanced Settings → Clarity → TruMotion → Off. The picture immediately looked like a movie again instead of a live broadcast.

The setting that ruins every film you watch takes 4 seconds to disable. It's been on since you plugged the TV in.

[5/13] Fix 3: He turned off Eco Mode.

His friend noticed the picture looked unusually dim for a panel that costs $1,500. He checked Settings and found Eco Solution enabled. It was automatically dimming the backlight based on ambient room light — cutting brightness by 30–40% in most conditions.

TV manufacturers enable eco mode by default because energy regulations in many states require certain brightness limits out of the box. The setting helps the TV meet efficiency standards on paper. It also makes your $1,500 panel look like a $400 one in a normally lit room.

On Samsung, he went to Settings → General → Power and Energy Saving → Eco Solution and turned everything off. On LG, it's Settings → Picture → Energy Saving → Off. On Sony, it's Settings → Display & Sound → Picture → Power Saving → Off.

The screen brightened immediately. HDR content that had looked flat and muddy suddenly had the punch and contrast the panel was designed to deliver. He'd been watching a dimmed version of every show for 3 years because the TV decided his electricity bill mattered more than his picture quality.

[6/13] Fix 4: He disabled ACR tracking.

This was the one that made the guy angry.
ACR — Automatic Content Recognition — is a system built into every major smart TV brand. It fingerprints whatever is on your screen every 15 to 60 seconds. 

Doesn't matter if you're streaming Netflix, watching cable, or playing a PlayStation through HDMI. The TV captures the content, identifies it, and sends that data to the manufacturer's advertising partners. They use it 
to build a viewing profile and sell targeted ads.

Samsung calls it Viewing Information Services. LG calls it Live Plus. Sony uses a third-party company called Samba TV. Vizio calls it Viewing Data. Every brand enables it by default. Every brand buries the off switch 3–4 menus deep.

His friend went to Settings → General & Privacy → Terms & Privacy → Viewing Information Services and toggled it off. Then he went to Privacy Choices and disabled Interest-Based Advertising.

The guy had no idea his TV was watching him. For 3 years, every movie, every show, every game session had been logged, fingerprinted, and sold to advertisers. The off switch was sitting in a menu he'd never opened.
Multiple users have reported that ACR re-enables itself after firmware updates. Samsung and Vizio are the worst offenders. His friend told him to check this toggle every time his TV updates.

[7/13] Fix 5: He dropped sharpness to zero.

This one surprised the guy because it sounds backwards. Less sharpness means a better picture.
Every TV ships with the sharpness slider set somewhere between 30 and 50 out of 100. What this actually does is apply a digital edge-enhancement filter that adds artificial halos around edges of objects. The result is a harsh, noisy image that looks "detailed" on a showroom floor but introduces artifacts that weren't in the original content.

Modern 4K content — especially streaming from Netflix, Apple TV+, and Disney+ — is already mastered at maximum detail. Adding sharpness on top of a 4K signal doesn't reveal more detail. It adds digital noise.
His friend went to Settings → Picture → Expert Settings → Sharpness and dropped it to 0. On some panels he said 5 to 10 is fine, but never above 10.

The image immediately looked cleaner. Fine details that had been hidden behind edge-enhancement halos became visible for the first time. Facial textures, fabric weaves, and background elements all sharpened naturally when the artificial filter was removed.

The sharpness slider is the most counterintuitive setting on any TV. Turning it down makes the picture look sharper. Turning it up makes it look processed.

[8/13] Fix 6: He turned on Filmmaker Mode.

After disabling all the settings that were ruining the picture, his friend showed him the one mode designed to fix everything at once.

Filmmaker Mode is a picture preset created by the UHD Alliance in partnership with directors like Christopher Nolan, Rian Johnson, Martin Scorsese, and Patty Jenkins. When activated, it automatically disables motion smoothing, preserves the original frame rate, maintains the correct color temperature, and turns off post-processing effects.

One button. Everything the director intended. No manual calibration needed.
On Samsung, it's Settings → Picture → Picture Mode → FILMMAKER MODE. On LG, same path. Most TVs sold since 2020 from Samsung, LG, Sony, Vizio, Hisense, and TCL include it. Filmmaker Mode 1.1, introduced in 2025, even adjusts for ambient room lighting automatically using the TV's built-in sensors.

His friend said this is the mode for people who don't want to think about settings. Switch to Filmmaker Mode and forget about it. Every movie and show plays as the creators intended.

The irony is that every TV manufacturer ships with Vivid mode — the mode directors hate — as the default. The mode directors actually endorse sits in the same menu, one scroll away, and nobody selects it.

[9/13] Fix 7: He fixed the sound.

The guy had been watching everything on the TV's built-in speakers with the default sound mode enabled. Dialogue was muddy. Explosions drowned out voices. He kept reaching for the remote to turn volume up during conversations and down during action scenes.
His friend opened Settings → Sound → Sound Mode and switched from "Standard" to "Clear Voice" — a mode every major brand includes that prioritizes dialogue frequencies over bass and ambient effects. Voices immediately cut through background noise without raising the overall volume.

Then he enabled "Night Mode" — sometimes called "Auto Volume Leveling" or "Volume Normalization" depending on the brand. This compresses the dynamic range so loud scenes and quiet scenes play at closer to the same volume. No more jumping for the remote during explosions at midnight.

On Samsung, both settings live under Settings → Sound → Expert Settings. On LG, it's Settings → Sound → Sound Mode Settings.
Most people assume bad TV audio means they need to buy a soundbar. Sometimes they do. But for the 60% of Americans who watch on built-in speakers, these two toggles fix the most common complaints without spending a dollar.

[10/13] Fix 8: He turned on HDMI-CEC.

The guy had 4 remotes on his coffee table. One for the TV. One for the soundbar. One for the Roku stick. One for the PlayStation. He juggled between them every time he switched inputs, adjusted volume, or turned the system on.

His friend went to Settings → General → External Device Manager → HDMI-CEC and turned on Anynet+ (Samsung's name for it). LG calls it SimpLink. Sony calls it BRAVIA Sync. Every brand has their own name for the exact same universal protocol.
HDMI-CEC lets every device connected via HDMI communicate with each other. Turn the TV on and the soundbar turns on automatically. Press play on the Roku and the TV switches to the correct input. Adjust volume on the TV remote and the soundbar volume changes. Power everything off with one button.
One remote replaced four. The entire entertainment system started behaving like a single device.
He'd had 4 HDMI-CEC-compatible devices plugged into a CEC-compatible TV for 3 years. Every device already spoke the same language. Nobody had ever introduced them to each other.

[11/13] Fix 9: He showed him 300+ free channels he didn't know existed.

This was the last thing his friend showed him and it had nothing to do with picture quality.
Every major smart TV ships with a free ad-supported streaming service built directly into the interface. Samsung has Samsung TV Plus. LG has LG Channels. Vizio has WatchFree+. Roku has The Roku Channel. All of them offer live channels covering news, sports, movies, cooking, true crime, kids' content, music, and reality TV — playing 24/7 like cable television.
No subscription. No sign-up. No app download. Already installed. Already running.

He scrolled through Samsung TV Plus and found over 300 channels he'd never seen. Live news from CNN, ABC, and Bloomberg. Sports highlights. Classic movie channels. 24/7 cooking streams. A dedicated channel for nature documentaries.
He was paying $75/month for cable and had never opened the free channel guide sitting one button press away on his remote. His friend said roughly 80% of smart TV owners have never scrolled past the app row to discover the channels their TV manufacturer included for free.

[12/13] The full picture of what changed.

Before the 12 minutes in Settings: a $1,500 TV displaying a showroom demo picture with artificial smoothing, crushed detail, dimmed brightness, and orange skin tones. A tracking system screenshotting his screen every 30 seconds and selling his viewing data to advertisers. Muddy dialogue drowning under explosions. 4 remotes on the coffee table. 300 free channels sitting undiscovered behind the app row.

After: a cinematic picture with natural colors, correct brightness, and zero artificial processing. ACR tracking disabled. Filmmaker Mode preserving every movie exactly as the director intended. Clear dialogue at midnight without waking the house. One remote controlling every device. 300 free channels replacing half his cable package.
Same TV. Same panel. Same price. 9 factory settings changed in 12 minutes.

[13/13] The uncomfortable truth.

Every TV manufacturer ships their televisions with settings designed for one audience: the person standing in Best Buy under fluorescent lights deciding between 40 TVs in 90 seconds.

Vivid mode wins that 90-second contest. It's the brightest, most saturated, most eye-catching picture on the wall. It's also the worst picture for your living room.
Once the TV leaves the store, nobody tells you to switch to Movie mode. Nobody tells you motion smoothing is on. Nobody tells you the eco dimmer is cutting your brightness. Nobody tells you ACR is screenshotting your screen. Nobody tells you the sharpness filter is adding noise. Nobody tells you about Filmmaker Mode, HDMI-CEC, or the 300 free channels.

Because the manufacturer doesn't care how you watch after you pay. They already have your $1,500. Now they're making more money from your viewing data than they made from the sale itself.

The installer's last line: "Every TV I walk into looks the same. Vivid mode. Motion smoothing. Eco dimmer. ACR on. Sharpness at 50. Four remotes on the table. The owner has been watching a showroom demo in their bedroom for years and never knew it. The picture they paid $1,500 for has been sitting behind one Settings menu the entire time. No manufacturer will ever tell you to change it because the demo version keeps you buying the next TV 2 years later thinking the picture will finally be better."
One evening. 9 settings. 12 minutes. The TV finally looks like what you paid for.

## 345. You are not too big for a job.

Tom Blomfield co-founded GoCardless. Then he co-founded Monzo and ran it as CEO. Two companies, more than nine billion dollars in combined peak value. The man does not need money.

On Monday he announced he is leaving Y Combinator to join Anthropic. Not as CEO. Not as a board member. As a member of technical staff on the compute team.

Mike Krieger co-founded Instagram. Facebook bought it for a billion dollars when the company had thirteen people. He joined Anthropic as Chief Product Officer in 2024. Then this year he gave up that title to go and co-lead a small internal team building early products.

A founder with a billion dollar exit walked away from the C-suite to go back into a team and build.

Ask yourself why.

They looked at the world, they asked where the largest value is being created right now, and they went and stood there. The title did not matter. The org chart did not matter. Whose name is on the door did not matter. The work mattered.

We have a problem where I come from - Nigeria. We treat a job as a step backwards. We treat it as the thing you do when your own thing has failed. So a man will keep a dying company alive for five years, burning his savings and his health, because going back to a job feels like admitting something in public. He is not staying because the business is working. He is staying because he cannot face the "shame" of taking a job.

I have been through this. I built Jobberman. I built Whogohost. And after both of them, I went and took a job at Indeed. Not because I had run out of ideas. Because that was where I was going to learn the most about the thing I cared about, at a scale I could not build myself yet. Nobody had to talk me into it.

And I will tell you plainly: if someone calls me tomorrow to co-found something, or to be a product manager on something, and that thing would create more value than everything I have built put together, I am taking that call. And I am sure my LPs and investors would agree as long as I can make them whole. And the maths is straightforward. It's really about calculating the value to be created as a whole (for you, for the larger ecosystem).

If someone asked a wildly successful entrepreneur to come become Chief of Staff to a governor, entrepreneur - they will take it up. So why not startup founders?

Am I saying people should not start companies? No. If you know me, you know I believe everyone should think like an entrepreneur whether you start a company or not. The problem is thinking a job is beneath you. You can work on validation for your opportunity on the side before you take the plunge. Hold a job, hone the craft, bank a salary, and letting the small thing grow underneath us until it was ready to be fed properly. You can test your own thing little by little while the job pays for the test.

We are losing our strongest youngest talent to this "I want to be my own boss - anything else is wrong" mentality. Good people who could be inside the biggest companies in the world, learning how real scale actually works, are instead running small businesses badly and calling it entrepreneurship. We are building smaller things when we should be building bigger ones.

Being a founder is not who you are. It is one way of working, and some seasons may call for a different one. If this post helps at least one person with the clarity for their next life decision, its done its job.

Go where the work is. Let God guide the rest.

Footnote
------
This post is for people with founder or nothing mentality and are not making headway and could use the skills honing and credibility building inside a larger org. If you have always been inside the safety net, this is not advice to justify taking that bet to build something. Look to Tosin Eniolorunda of Moniepoint and Olugbenga Agboola of Flutterwave for inspiration, not this post!

[Open](https://x.com/opeawo/status/2077441540587192422)

## 350. Thread by @AmeboWithMoh

My neighbor wasn’t wealthy.

Yet he sponsored his wife’s younger sister through university.

Nobody knew how many sacrifices he made till she graduated.

After she got a good job, his wife started disrespecting him.

She constantly reminded him that her family owed him nothing.

He kept quiet.

Last month, she moved out but her sister stayed.

## 356. Thread by @muratcan

Jokes aside, it’s time to move away from this style of prompting.

For anything that actually runs long hours, multiple context windows, parallel workers, or loops, you need long-horizon-prompting instead.

The new style uses a pseudo-formal task brief:
- Defines exactly what “done” looks like with clear, measurable success conditions
- Explicitly lists what doesn’t count as a win (stops agents from gaming the result)
- Adds an independent fresh-context reviewer that double-checks everything
- Forces the agent to explore different approaches instead of endlessly tweaking the same idea
- Sets strict rules for when to stop and what to actually deliver

## 359. Thread by @align_all

Can we reach $100k MRR in 8 months? 🧵

Step 01: Why does an app icon matter so much?

Before changing our app icon, we were getting around 3k-5k downloads per month.

Our original “GO” icon felt personal and meaningful to us. We loved it. Still, we decided to test a new icon featuring a shoe 👟

Since making that change, downloads have increased to 15k-20k per month.

The lesson: When designing an app icon, focus on what resonates with users, not just what you personally like as a designer.

Users don’t see the story behind your design. They make a split-second decision based on what they immediately understand.

Step 02: Keep it simple.

In the next build, we’re making it even easier to get to your dashboard right after downloading @goclub_app We’re also restructuring in-app content, redesigning and organising key stats more thoughtfully, and experimenting with a new pricing model.

The guiding principle is simple: Keep it simple.

Our goal is ambitious but clear: reach $100k MRR within the next 6-8 months.

I’ll be sharing everything we learn, build, test, and ship along the way. If you’re interested, save this post and follow the journey. 🙂

## 360. AI agent builders charge $175–$300/hr. The skill takes one weekend. Here's the path.

I want to show you two numbers from the same market, same skill, same year.

The median AI engineer on Upwork charges about $50 an hour.

Independent AI agent builders working with direct clients charge $175 to $300 an hour. Specialists at the top of the niche bill $250 to $350.

Same tools. Same work. A 5x gap.

That gap is not talent. It is not a degree. It is positioning, and it is learnable. The core build skill genuinely takes a weekend. The positioning takes this article.

Let me walk you through the whole thing.

Why businesses pay this much

Here's the situation nobody on the business side will say out loud.

Every company now knows AI exists. Their competitors talk about it. Their board asks about it. And almost none of them have any idea how to actually implement it.

Meanwhile the boring math is brutal. A small business pays real salaries for work that is mostly repetitive: answering the same support emails, chasing invoices, qualifying leads, writing reports. An agent that takes over even one of those processes saves them thousands a month, forever.

So when someone shows up who can actually build that, the hourly rate stops mattering. A $250/hour builder who ships a working system in two weeks is cheaper than a $70/hour one who takes three months. Clients who have been burned once know this very well.

You are not selling hours of coding. You are selling a machine that saves money every month. That's the whole reframe, and it's worth keeping in your head for the rest of this article.

The skill itself (yes, a weekend)

Strip away the buzzwords and an AI agent is one simple pattern:

That's it. A trigger, a brain, an action.

The stack is two tools. Claude is the brain: it reads the input, makes the decision, writes the output. n8n is the plumbing: a visual, no-code builder that connects 400+ apps and fires the whole chain automatically. You drag blocks with a mouse.

One weekend is enough to build your first real agent: a lead responder that answers every form submission with a personalized email in 60 seconds and logs everything to a CRM. Saturday you learn the tools and wire it up. Sunday you break it with weird test inputs and fix it.

Will you be an expert? No. Will you have a working system that a real business would pay for? Yes. And that's all the first client needs to see.

I won't repeat the full build steps here, that's its own guide. The point for today: the barrier to entry is one weekend, not one degree.

The rate card (screenshot this)

Now the money. These are current market numbers, verified, not wishes:

Look at that last line again. It's the most important one in the table.

The market is not paying for the skill. The skill on a bidding platform is worth $50. The market pays for the outcome, sold directly to a business owner who understands what it saves them. Same hands, same keyboard, 5x the rate.

Which brings us to the actual secret.

Don't sell hours. Sell the machine.

Here's a trap almost every beginner walks into.

You get good, you get faster, and hourly billing punishes you for it. An agent that takes you 3 hours to build at $150/hour is $450. The same agent, packaged as an outcome, is a $2,500 setup fee plus $1,000 a month to maintain. The client happily pays it, because it saves them $5,000 a month in labor.

So price the machine, not the minutes:

The retainer is the business. Agents need monitoring and occasional fixes, clients know it, and they'd rather pay you monthly than think about it ever again. Setup fees pay your bills this month. Retainers build the thing that pays them next year.

Use the hourly rates from the table for one thing only: as your floor when you scope a package. If a build takes you 10 hours, the package should never price those hours below $175 each. That's how the rate card and the packages connect.

Landing the first client (the script)

Nobody hires a stranger off one message. They hire after watching a 2-minute demo of a machine doing a job they hate paying for.

So the sequence is: build the demo agent first (your weekend project), record a 2-minute screen video of it working, then reach out. Form gets submitted, reply lands in the inbox in 60 seconds, CRM updates itself. Narrate what's happening. That video does 80% of your selling.

Then the outreach. Short, specific, zero fluff:

Send that to 30 local businesses that live on inbound leads: agencies, law firms, home services, clinics, real estate. Expect most to ignore you. You need one yes.

On the call, don't pitch. Ask. "Walk me through what happens when a lead comes in. How many hours a week does that take? At your team's rate, what does that cost per month?" Write their number down where they can see it. Then show the demo and put your price next to their number. When your $1,000 retainer sits next to their $4,000 monthly cost, the conversation closes itself.

And one rule that beginners break constantly: never start building the full custom system before a contract is signed. Demo first, sell, then build. Forty hours of unpaid perfectionism is how this business dies in month one.

Your first 30 days, honestly



Realistic outcome: 1 client, $1,500 to $3,000 setup + a monthly retainer.
Not $20k/month. That comes from doing this loop ten more times.

The loop compounds. Client one gives you a case study and a referral. The case study makes outreach twice as effective. By client three you have templates, and each new build takes half the time at the same price.

The honest part

I'm not going to pretend this is free money, because three things are true and you should know them going in.

First, the $175 to $300 rates are direct-client rates. If you park yourself on a bidding platform and compete on price, you will get platform prices. The premium is earned by going to businesses directly with proof in hand. That means outreach, calls, and hearing "no" a lot. This is a sales game wrapped around a technical skill, roughly half and half.

Second, nobody bills 40 hours a week. Real freelancers land at 50 to 70% billable time; the rest is finding clients, calls, and admin. Do your income math on 20 to 25 billable hours, not 40.

Third, the weekend gets you the core skill, not mastery. Your first paid build will take longer than you quoted, something will break at 11pm, and you'll fix it. That's normal. The gap between you and the $300/hour specialist is maybe fifteen delivered projects. The gap between you and everyone still watching tutorials is one weekend.

The demand side, though, is real and verified: AI agent work sits at the very top of freelance rates right now, businesses are actively paying retainers of $2,000 to $8,000 a month for it, and the number of people who can actually deliver is still tiny. The window is genuinely open.

The bottom line

The skill takes a weekend. The rate takes positioning: direct clients, outcomes instead of hours, a demo instead of a resume.

Build the agent this weekend. Record the video. Send the 30 messages. Run the calls with their own numbers on the table.

One yes turns this from an article you read into an income line you have.

→ Follow for AI systems that actually make money, posted weekly → Save this, the outreach script alone is worth it → Comment "GUIDE" and I'll reply with the full discovery call flow

## 367. Thread by @shugarDadddy

Before you launch your vibe coded project, run this prompt first:

“Perform a comprehensive audit of the application, covering security, reliability, concurrency, accessibility, and UI consistency.

Review the relevant codebase, architecture, data flows, API interactions, authentication and authorization logic, state management, async operations, error handling, and user-facing interfaces. Trace important flows end-to-end rather than reviewing files in isolation.

Specifically investigate:

Security vulnerabilities and data exposure
- Authentication and authorization flaws, including missing server-side permission checks, privilege escalation, insecure direct object references, and cross-tenant data access.

- Sensitive information exposed through client-side code, environment variables, API responses, logs, analytics, URLs, local storage, session storage, cookies, error messages, or source maps.

- Injection risks, including SQL, command, template, prompt, HTML, and script injection where applicable.
XSS, CSRF, SSRF, insecure redirects, unsafe file uploads, path traversal, weak session handling, insecure token storage, and missing security boundaries.

- Overly permissive database rules, API endpoints, CORS policies, storage buckets, webhook handlers, or third-party integrations.

- Secrets, API keys, credentials, internal endpoints, personal data, or implementation details that could be unintentionally exposed.

- Missing validation and sanitisation at trust boundaries. Do not assume client-side validation is sufficient.

Race conditions, concurrency, and state integrity

- Duplicate submissions caused by repeated clicks, retries, refreshes, or concurrent requests.

- Non-idempotent operations that can create duplicate records, payments, messages, bookings, jobs, or side effects.

- Stale state, optimistic update failures, lost updates, conflicting writes, and out-of-order async responses.

- Effects, subscriptions, listeners, timers, and requests that are not correctly cleaned up.

- UI states where actions remain available while an operation is already in progress.

- Cache invalidation problems and inconsistencies between client state, server state, and persisted data.

- Multi-tab, multi-device, and poor-network scenarios where relevant.

Reliability and failure handling
- Unhandled promise rejections, swallowed errors, silent failures, infinite loading states, broken retry loops, and incomplete rollback behaviour.

- Missing loading, empty, error, offline, timeout, and partial-success states.

- Failure paths that leave data or the UI in an inconsistent state.

- Assumptions about API responses, nullability, ordering, timing, or network availability that could cause production failures.

- Memory leaks, unnecessary rerenders, expensive operations, and obvious performance bottlenecks that materially affect the user experience.

Accessibility
- Semantic HTML and correct use of landmarks, headings, labels, lists, tables, buttons, and links.

- Keyboard navigation, logical tab order, focus visibility, focus trapping, and focus restoration.

- Missing or incorrect accessible names, labels, descriptions, and ARIA attributes.

- Colour contrast, text legibility, touch-target sizes, zoom behaviour, reduced-motion support, and reliance on colour alone to communicate meaning.

- Screen-reader behaviour for modals, menus, dropdowns, tabs, toasts, validation errors, loading states, and dynamically updated content.

- Forms with unclear instructions, inaccessible validation, missing autocomplete attributes, or poor error recovery.

- Test against WCAG 2.2 AA expectations where applicable.

Visual and interaction consistency
- Inconsistent spacing, typography, colour usage, border radii, shadows, icon sizing, alignment, component dimensions, and responsive behaviour.

- Components that visually appear identical but behave differently, or behave identically but are implemented inconsistently.

1/2

## 377. How I Built a Faceless YouTube Channel to $41k a Month With Claude

How I Built a Faceless YouTube Channel to $41k a Month With Claude

Eight months ago I had a laptop, a $20 Claude subscription, and no idea what I was doing.

No camera, no editor, no team. Last month the channel did $9,400 and I've never shown my face once.

The whole thing is six steps. Claude does the heavy lifting on four of them. Here's the exact build, with the prompts I actually paste.

First, the money

Everyone asks what YouTube pays per view. Wrong question. The niche decides everything:

Finance and investing: $15-50 RPM
Technology and AI: $12-30
Health and longevity: $10-25
General and entertainment: $3-8


My channel is finance. Around 480,000 views last month at an $18 RPM is about $8,600 from AdSense alone, before one affiliate link. A cooking channel at $3 RPM needs five million views for the same check.

Views are the engine. The niche is the fuel.

Step 1: Pick the niche by RPM, not by what you like

I picked a cooking idea first because I liked it. That mistake cost me three weeks. Now I make Claude run the math before I commit:



It handed me "debt payoff for people on a normal salary" inside finance, plus a year of titles. That's a $300 consultant call for free.

Step 2: Write the script. This is the actual business

My first 5 videos flopped. Clean voice, fine footage, 200 views each. The problem was the writing.

YouTube only really measures one thing, do people keep watching. A script that holds 70% gets pushed to new viewers, one that holds 30% gets buried. That gap is the script, not the production.

This is the prompt I paste for every video:


Once I fixed the script, the same channel that did 200 views started doing 40,000. Nothing else has changed.

Step 3: Voice it once, keep that voice forever

ElevenLabs reads the script. I picked one voice and never change it, so the channel keeps an identity viewers recognize. Set the speed to 0.9x for finance, it reads with more authority. Drop [pause] markers into the script where it should breathe, that's what makes it stop sounding like a robot

$22 a month. This step is not where you win or lose, so don't overthink it.


Step 4: Assemble it in CapCut

No camera means no editing, just matching footage to the voiceover. The rule: the visual changes every 3 to 5 seconds. Pexels and Pixabay cover most of the B-roll for free.

Turn on CapCut's auto-captions. They lifted my watch time about 15% because most people scroll with the sound off.

Step 5: Let Claude write the metadata

I had one video that deserved 80,000 views sitting at 800. Great script, clean voice, decent thumbnail. The title was terrible.

Now Claude writes the whole metadata layer in one shot:

That one prompt is worth $50 a video if you hire it out. I run it in 20 seconds.

Step 6: Post 20 to 30 videos before you judge anything

I almost quit at video 8. Nothing was moving. The channel didn't start climbing until video 24.

The algorithm needs 20 to 30 videos before it learns who to show you to. Most people post 8, see flat numbers, and walk away one month before the curve turns. Same day, same time, every week, no exceptions for the first six months.

What it costs

Claude: $20/mo
ElevenLabs: $22/mo
CapCut, Canva, Pexels: free
Total: ~$57/mo

Against what this replaces, a scriptwriter at $150-400 a video, a voice actor, an editor, an SEO person. I'm running all of it on the price of two coffees a month.

The part nobody tells you

For fifteen years the barrier to YouTube was the camera. You needed a face, a voice, presence, gear, years of reps on camera.

That barrier is gone, and losing it showed me something that was always true. The camera was a distraction from the actual job, which is writing things people don't want to stop watching.

The faceless channel didn't remove the hard part. It removed everything that was hiding it. I stopped competing on being watchable and started competing on being un-skippable.

That one's a writing problem, and Claude already solved it.

[Open](https://x.com/woody_research/status/2061700552422195234)

## 386. Thread by @r0ck3t23

Egypt forgot how to build the pyramids.

Rome forgot how to build the aqueducts. Some still carry water today. What they built still stands. Neither civilization remembers how they did it.

Musk: “You look at great civilizations like ancient Egypt, and they were able to make the pyramids, and they forgot how to do that.”

Musk: “And the Romans, they built these incredible aqueducts. They forgot how to do it.”

No army invaded them. The knowledge just stopped getting used, and the moment it did, it was gone.

Same collapse. Compressed into fifty years instead of a thousand.

Musk: “In 1969, we were able to send somebody to the moon… Then the space shuttle retired, and the United States could take no one to orbit.”

Musk: “People are mistaken when they think that technology just automatically improves… it will, by itself, degrade.”

Capability doesn’t sit in a vault. It only exists inside the people doing the work right now.

The second they stop, it doesn’t pause.

It disappears.

That should not scare you. It should focus you.

Nobody loses a civilization to war. They lose it the moment they stop building.

Nobody is owed the future. It belongs to whoever keeps building it.

## 403. Thread by @JPoehnelt

Two months ago I was fired by Google for creating the Google Workspace CLI. It went viral, hit #1 on Hacker News, gained thousands of GitHub stars and many thousands of actual users in just a couple days.

It was an incredible, confusing journey, from directors and leaders asking what they could learn from the tool to getting grilled by legal about why the Google logo and brand colors are on the Google Workspace GitHub code repositories.

I think the cause was that Workspace and certain leaders (and projects) were afraid of being disrupted. But the fear wasn't specific to my CLI, it was a broader fear in what agents meant for Workspace. Either way, the irony of my termination was the announcement at Google Cloud Next two days before I was fired that an official Workspace CLI was coming.

I want this out there because it is easier for me to explain my story and it is an experience I want to fully own. It's also part of my healing.

Nearly 7 years at Google was an incredible opportunity for me and I was fortunate to have wonderful teammates and a manager that fully supported me through these last few months. Thank you.

## 405. How I Run Profitable Apple Search Ads for My Apps

Most app founders run Apple Search Ads with the wrong scoreboard.

They look at taps, installs, CPI, maybe CPA.

Useful, but still not the real question.

The real question is: which keyword is actually making money?

Apple gives new Apple Ads accounts $100 in free credit, so if your app has a clear search intent, it is worth testing.

Not with 50 campaigns. Just a clean test to see if people are already searching for the problem your app solves.

Apple Search Ads is not magic paid traffic. It is intent capture.

Someone types a problem into the App Store. Your job is to make the whole funnel feel like the obvious answer.

search query → ad → App Store page → onboarding → first value moment → paywall → subscription

If one step breaks the promise, the keyword dies.

A cheap keyword can bring installs all day and never turn into trials, subscriptions, or renewals.

An expensive keyword can look scary and still print revenue.

That is why I manage Apple Ads around keyword ROAS, not CPI.

My current US campaigns are around x2 ROAS (no hard paywall). This is the setup I use.

1. Start with one search intent

Do not start by selling the whole app.

Start with one job the user is searching for.

When you are getting started, build around one search intent.

It is much easier to make one tight funnel work than to make five vague funnels work at the same time.

Example for a running planner:

running plan
5k training plan
marathon training plan

If the keyword is '5k training plan', the page should not try to sell route tracking, calories, social challenges, shoe mileage, and recovery scores.

It should make the user think:

yes, this is exactly what I searched for

That is the whole game.

The tighter the intent, the easier the funnel gets.

Broad app:

Sport tracker with routes, workouts, calories, recovery, races, habits...

Search-intent funnel:

Get a running plan for your next 5K

Once one intent works, then I started to use custom product pages for other intents (segmentation):

running plan → page about building a weekly running plan

5K training → page about getting ready for a 5K

marathon training → page about long-term race prep

Same app, different promise.

2. Make the App Store page sell

Apple Ads sends people to your App Store page.

So the page has to sell before the campaign can work.

Before spending, check:

icon

title

subtitle

first screenshot

screenshot text

onboarding promise

paywall promise

Also try to keep at least 5 screenshots live.

Not because 5 is a magic conversion number, but because I have seen weird cases where Apple does not show screenshots the way you expect when the set is too thin. Maybe it is category/account dependent. I still would not risk it.

Maybe it is category/account dependent. I still would not risk it.

For my apps, I like to see roughly:

TTR: ~15%+
install conversion: ~75%+

Not universal benchmarks. Just my “is the page obviously broken?” check.

Low TTR usually means the ad/listing does not look relevant enough.

Low install conversion usually means the page is not selling well enough.

Fix that before raising bids.

3. Campaign structure

My starter setup:

Search Results campaign

one country per campaign

one intent per ad group

manual bids

Search Match off

exact match keywords

iPhone only if the app is built for iPhone

I do not rely on broad/discovery at the start.

It can work later, but early on it burns too much budget before the signal is clean enough.

Exact match answers the question I care about:

did this specific keyword create revenue?

One country per campaign also keeps the account readable.

I want campaigns like:

US - Exact

UK - Exact

CA - Exact

BR - Exact

If you mix countries, the data gets muddy fast.

Canada might spend, Brazil might convert, and the UK might get cheap taps. I want to see that separately.

4. Start with obvious keywords

I do not get my first exact keywords from discovery.

I start with the obvious intent:

what would someone search if they wanted exactly what my app does?

For a running planner, I would rather start with:

running plan

5k training plan

marathon training plan

than dump every fitness keyword into broad match and hope Apple figures it out.

Discovery can come later.

First, I want clean data on the keywords I already believe should work.

5. Start bids near Apple's suggestion

For bids, I usually start around Apple's suggested/default CPT, but a bit lower.

The goal is not to win every auction on day one.

The goal is to buy enough signal without getting cooked.

If a keyword gets no impressions, I check:

is the bid too low? (probably)

does Apple think my app is not relevant enough?

is the keyword too low-volume? (rarely the case except for long tailed keyword)

If the keyword is relevant and the page matches the intent, I raise the bid slowly.

Then I watch impression share:

good ROAS + low share = maybe raise bid

good ROAS + high share = maybe raise budget or test nearby keywords

bad ROAS + high share = lower bid or pause

Good ROAS with low impression share means there may be more volume to win.

Good ROAS with high impression share means the keyword might already be close to maxed out.

The mistake is thinking every problem is a bid problem.

Sometimes the bid is wrong.

Sometimes the page is leaking.

Sometimes onboarding is not delivering the intent.

Sometimes the keyword is just bad.

6. Read the funnel, not just the ad account

Apple-side metrics diagnose the problem.

Revenue decides what to do.

This is how I read it:

low TTR
→ icon, title, subtitle, first screenshot, keyword fit

good TTR + low install conversion
→ App Store page is leaking

installs + no trials
→ onboarding or first value moment is weak

trials + no paid users
→ offer, pricing, product, or trial quality problem

revenue + low ROAS
→ acquisition cost is too high or LTV is too low

This is why managing Apple Ads only inside Apple Ads feels incomplete.

The dashboard stops at the install.

The business starts after the install.

7. Do not judge trials too early

Subscription apps are annoying because revenue is delayed.

If someone starts a free trial today, today's ROAS can look worse than reality.

So I separate:

active trials

mature trials

paid users

renewal revenue

A keyword with active trials is not the same as a keyword with installs and zero signal.

You can kill good keywords too early if you ignore trial delay.

You can also keep bad keywords alive too long if you only look at cheap installs.

Both are expensive.

8. Use popularity + share before changing bids

This is the new piece I added to AppSprint ASO.

Apple Ads has a 'searchPopularity' value for search terms.

Basically:

Popularity = how much search demand Apple thinks the keyword has

So now I read keywords like this:

high popularity + low share
= meaningful scale might be available

low popularity + high share
= you may already dominate a small keyword

Low share on a huge keyword matters way more than low share on a tiny one.

That changes bid decisions.

9. Where AppSprint ASO fits

This is why I am building AppSprint ASO.

It started as an ASO tool: keyword tracking, rankings, competitors, metadata, screenshots.

But if you care about ASO, Apple Ads belongs in the same workflow.

Paid keywords show you which search intents actually monetize.

So AppSprint ASO now connects:

Apple Ads spend

installs

trials

subscriptions

revenue

ROAS

impression share

impression-share rank

keyword popularity

at keyword level.

Revenue comes from RevenueCat or Superwall, and the SDK is intentionally tiny.

No full MMP migration.

No giant attribution setup.

Just: which Apple Ads keywords make money?

That is the question I care about (and you should too).

TL;DR

use the $100 Apple Ads credit if you have it

pick one monetizable search intent

build the App Store page around that intent

keep the funnel consistent after install

one country per campaign

exact match first

Search Match off

manual bids

start around Apple's suggested bid, slightly lower

watch TTR + install conversion

watch impression share + popularity

judge keywords by ROAS

CPI is useful. CPA is useful. But the scoreboard is:

spend  installs → trials → revenue → ROAS

Stats to watch for:

good ROAS + low share + high popularity→ raise bid

good ROAS + high share→ raise budget or test nearby keywords/countries

bad ROAS + high share→ lower bid or pause

bad ROAS + low share→ wait for more data, unless CPI is already impossible

If you run Apple Ads for a subscription app and want this view, I am building it inside AppSprint ASO.

Let's stop guessing which keywords make money.

[Open](https://x.com/arthursbuilds/status/2068586140441956427)

## 407. Thread by @frederickjames

My app hit $769/m in 21 days.

This is what I did:
1. Made a habit tracker.
2. Exploited a deep human need.
3. Had one viral, shareable, feature.
4. Obsessed over design, feel & ui.
5. iOS only, native swift.
6. 13 step onboarding.
7. Pricing: 7.99/w & 49.99/y.
8. Adding 14.99/m boosted conversion.
9. Hard paywall in RevenueCat.
10. No notifications: this 2x’d my retention.
11. Measured onboarding with PostHog.
12. Iterated till 85%+ onboarding.
13. Tweaked paywall till 3-4% conversion.
14. Ratings during onboarding (patched).
15. Invested in tools: Mobbin, Claude etc.
16. Burnt $200 on ads to find winners.
17. $20-£50/day in Apple Store Ads.
18. Did not localise pricing or screenshots.
19. For ads: niche locations & popular keywords.
20. Figma for store screenshots: contrast & depth.

More importantly:
1. Got 8+ hours of good sleep / night.
2. Ate super clean diet & gym 3x a week.
3. Had special time with wife 2x a week.
4. Blocked noise & negativity.
5. Had a positive abundance mindset.
6. Followed The Law of Attraction.
7. Failed for 3 years, and 13 different ideas.

This was just my playbook: there are infinite ways to win.

## 408. Thread by @kedytcom

One ASO trick that keeps working for me:

I DON’T start with the highest-volume keywords.

Instead:

Find keywords with difficulty < 20
Check the Top 10 apps ranking for them
If the exact keyword isn’t used in their title/subtitle → that’s my opportunity
Put the main keyword in Title or Subtitle
Put keyword combinations in the keyword field

Result:

🇯🇵 +129
🇦🇺 +98
🇦🇪 +86
🇺🇸 +67
Most ASO isn’t finding keywords.
It’s finding keywords your competitors forgot to optimize for. 🚀
Or a shorter viral version:

My ASO strategy is surprisingly simple:

Find keywords with low difficulty.

Then check if the Top 10 apps are actually using them in their metadata.

If they’re not:

🎯 I take the keyword.

That’s how I keep finding ranking opportunities across multiple countries.

Most developers compete.

I look for gaps. 📈🌍

## 414. How to Create AI UGC Ads That Actually Sell Your App

Building apps has never been more accessible. Anyone can ship something now. The hard part isn't building anymore.

It's getting anyone to care about your app.

Most founders hit the same wall: no marketing budget, no audience, no idea how to make content that doesn't look like a boring ad.

So here are 2 video formats that work for promoting apps on TikTok: short, native feeling and effective regardless of your niche. And I'll show you how to make both of them using Claude and Arcads MCP, without expending any money on UGC creators or influencers.

Two TikTok formats that actually work for apps

Most app ads fall flat because they look like app ads. What converts on TikTok is content that feels native,  like something a real user would post.

These are the two formats I've been building with Claude + Arcads MCP.

What is Arcads MCP?

MCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open standard that lets Claude connect directly to external tools. Instead of jumping between apps, Claude talks to them in the background and you prompt to Claude.

Arcads MCP is Arcads' implementation of this. It gives Claude access to AI actors, video generation, and your entire Arcads studio, all from a single chat.

Setup takes about 2 minutes:

Open Claude → Settings → Connectors

Add the Arcads MCP server URL

Sign in with your Arcads account

That's it. No API keys. No config files. No workflow builder.

Now you can use Arcads inside Claude:

Format 1: The Reaction Video

You've seen this format a hundred times. Someone discovers something, reacts emotionally ("why did nobody tell me about this?") and then you see the thing that caused the reaction.

For apps, it maps perfectly:

Top half: AI-generated reaction clip. A person laughing, crying, looking genuinely shocked

Bottom half: A real screen recording of your app in action, POV style

The hook writes itself: "Why is no one talking about this app?" or "I wish I found this sooner."

How to build it in Claude:

First, write the brief in Claude. Describe your app, your target user, and the pain it solves. Ask Claude to suggest the ideal emotional reaction and actor profile for your audience. Then:

Once you approve the image, generate the video:

Then stitch it in CapCut: reaction on top, your screen recording on the bottom, bold caption as the hook. Done.

Here's the result:

Format 2: The Green Screen Walkthrough

This one is underused. An AI spokesperson appears over a live recording of your app, talking through exactly what they're seeing on screen, like a friend showing you something cool.

It feels more trustworthy than a voiceover. More personal than a demo. And it takes maybe 20 minutes to produce.

How to build it in Claude:

Then generate the actor:

Generate the video:

In CapCut: place your screen recording as the background layer, drop the AI actor on top, key out the background, position them in the corner or side. Add captions. Export.

Here's the final result:

Why this beats the old workflow

The old way: brief → Fiverr/UGC platform → wait → receive clips → reshoot because the product looks wrong → wait again.

This way: brief → Claude → Arcads MCP → done. In one conversation.

The quality is good enough that most viewers can't tell. And more importantly ,  you can iterate. If the hook isn't landing, regenerate it in 5 minutes and test again. That kind of speed is only possible when the whole process lives in one place.

What it's best for

This workflow makes the most sense if:

You're running paid TikTok or Meta ads for a B2C app

You need volume. Multiple angles, multiple hooks, fast

You don't have budget for a full creative agency

You want to test before you scale

It's not magic. You still need a good product and a real screen recording. But the barrier to getting your first 10 test creatives out the door just dropped significantly.

Building a bootstrapped business has never been easier than now. You can build an app in a weekend with Anything, then promote it with AI videos using Arcads. No agency, no team, no excuses.

If you want to follow my journey building in public, I share everything at @cesaralvarezll.

## 428. Thread by @SEOKeval

One of the biggest SEO lessons I've learned is that you can almost guarantee SEO success just by copying the strategy of low-authority competitors that are ranking well.

Let's say your brand sells pillow covers.

Take a look at the competitor Woven Nook:

34 domain rating.

(anything under 40 is low)

Ranks in the top-3 positions for 500+ transactional keywords.

If they can rank for major keywords like "pillow covers" and "24x24 pillow covers" with a 34 domain rating...

You can too.

Take a look at their XML sitemap to see the architecture of their website:

wovennook .com/sitemap.xml.

Build out all of the Collection pages they have to model their keyword targeting.

Optimize the Collection pages for their target keyword.

Add those Collection pages to your main navigation menu in the same way that they did.

Skip blog content, as they haven't updated their blog in over four years.

Next reverse engineer their link building.

How many do-follow, DR 30+ links are pointing to their homepage?

20.

Build more than that.

What about individual Collection pages?

Let's take a look at their main Pillow Covers page.

3.

Build that many. But slowly. One a month.

Run this strategy while concurrently dialing in paid ads, retention, CRO, etc, you will dominate the pillow case industry on Google in 6-12 months.

SEO is as simple as that.
